What should a trainee do? | Notify the team, report current station and patient status, and assist under the site’s power-outage plan and clinical direction. | Notify clinical leadership quickly. Conditions across machines may differ, so the trainee reports status and carries out assigned contingency actions. | Objective 4. [Philippine DOH · Dialysis facility standards](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/10/50687) |
| 15 | Water service returns after a disruption. Before preparing a station for treatment, what should you verify? | The authorized team completed required checks and released the affected system and equipment under the facility recovery plan. | Utility restoration is an input to recovery. Required water and equipment checks and authorized release must occur before use. | Objective 4. [CDC · Healthcare water system recovery](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/hcp/reopen-health-facilities/water-system-repair.html) |
| 16 | A patient has a seizure in the dialysis chair. Which first action is within the technician’s scope? | Call for help, protect the head and access arm from injury, and note the start time | The technician calls, protects from injury, and times the event; nothing goes in the mouth and the circuit is the nurse’s. |  |
| 17 | After a suspected dialyzer reaction, what should happen to the used dialyzer and its package? | Keep them for the nurse, because they are evidence | The team may need the dialyzer type, lot, and circuit to find the cause. |  |
| 18 | During the daily cart check the oxygen gauge reads below the minimum your unit sets. What do you do? | Record it, tell the charge nurse, and request a full cylinder now | A cart item that fails its check is corrected immediately, not later. |  |
| 19 | You are assigned as recorder during a code. Which practice is correct? | Write each event with its time as it is called out, using one clock throughout | A code record is a time log made as events happen, on a single clock. |  |
| 20 | In an emergency clamp-and-disconnect, what happens to fistula needles? | They are left in place, secured, and removed later in a safe area | Removing needles takes time and needs pressure on each site; in an evacuation they stay in until the patient is safe. |  |
| 21 | The power fails and machines switch to battery. Which technician action is correct in the first five minutes? | Stay with your patients, report which machines are on battery or dark, and bring flashlights and hand cranks | The technician observes, reports, and brings equipment; the nurse decides what happens to each treatment. |  |
| 22 | Which entry in your written account after an event is a fact, not an opinion? | Machine 4 screen went dark at 14:06 | Accounts record what was seen and when; causes are worked out in the review. |  |

## Module 11: Dialyzer Reprocessing and Equipment Quality

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does the label ‘single use only’ mean for the dialyzer? | It is intended for one use. Follow facility disposal procedure afterward. | Product labeling and instructions determine whether the device is intended for single use. A single-use dialyzer is discarded under the facility process after use. | Objective 1. [Manufacturer example · Dialyzer instructions for use](https://freseniusmedicalcare.com/en-us/support/product-support-documents/instructions-for-use/) |
| 2 | When reuse is authorized, for whom may the previously used dialyzer be prepared? | Only the same patient, with identity verified under the program | An authorized dialyzer reuse program is patient-specific. A similar prescription or shared station does not establish patient identity. | Objective 1. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.50, dialyzer reuse](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.50) |
| 3 | The label permits multiple use, but the facility runs a single-use program. What does the model’s label establish? | The model may be eligible for reuse in an authorized program. The facility still follows its own single-use policy. | A multiple-use designation is one prerequisite. Local policy, applicable requirements, and a validated program govern whether reuse occurs. | Objective 1. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.50, dialyzer reuse](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.50) |
| 4 | What does an acceptable documented residual disinfectant check indicate? | That the residual disinfectant requirement was met for this recorded check | The result addresses a specific chemical safety check. Identity, integrity and all other required criteria need separate verification. | Objective 1. [CDC · Dialysis infection-control assessment tool, reuse section](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/media/pdfs/icar-dialysis-508.pdf) |
| 5 | Stock is low and a coworker suggests reprocessing a dialyzer already used once even though the label specifies single use. What should you do? | Keep it out of reuse and notify the supervisor of the supply issue. | A stock shortage does not change product labeling. The supervisor can arrange an authorized replacement or alternate plan. | Objective 1. [Manufacturer example · Dialyzer instructions for use](https://freseniusmedicalcare.com/en-us/support/product-support-documents/instructions-for-use/) |
| 6 | What makes a reprocessed dialyzer traceable? | A legible link among the dialyzer identity, verified patient and relevant record of processing and disposition | Traceability connects the specific device and patient with processing history and outcome. The unit defines the required identifiers and checks. | Objective 2. [CDC · Dialysis infection-control assessment tool, reuse section](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/media/pdfs/icar-dialysis-508.pdf) |
| 7 | A dialyzer has a recorded acceptable integrity result, but its patient identifier is unreadable. Which requirement remains unmet? | Patient-to-device match | A device test and a verified patient match address different risks. Acceptable test results cannot resolve an unreadable identifier. | Objective 2. [CDC · Dialysis infection-control assessment tool, reuse section](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/media/pdfs/icar-dialysis-508.pdf) |
| 8 | An authorized reviewer has put a dialyzer on hold because its record is incomplete. What does hold mean? | It is segregated from ready stock pending the authorized disposition decision. | A hold prevents use while the concern is reviewed. The applicable policy defines further evaluation, rejection or authorized disposition. | Objective 3. [CDC · Dialysis infection-control assessment tool, reuse section](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/media/pdfs/icar-dialysis-508.pdf) |
| 9 | At preparation, a reprocessed dialyzer label and the chart disagree. Recorded technical checks are acceptable. What should you do? | Set it aside in the designated hold area, notify the authorized staff and document the unresolved mismatch. | Prevent use and preserve the record so the authorized team can investigate. A technician should never infer or recreate an uncertain identity. | Objective 3. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.50, dialyzer reuse](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.50) |
| 10 | Which entry best documents the mismatch for follow-up in the authorized log? | 07:20 · Dialyzer asset D-31 held · Patient identifier unreadable · Verified match unavailable · Charge nurse informed and disposition pending. | The note identifies the item, problem, hold status, notification and pending disposition. Record patient identifiers only through the approved record process. | Objective 2. [CDC · Dialysis infection-control assessment tool, reuse section](https://www.cdc.gov/infection-control/media/pdfs/icar-dialysis-508.pdf) |
| 11 | Which document identifies the approved way to operate a specific model of dialysis machine? | The current manufacturer instructions for that model, used with the facility procedure | Operation and maintenance must follow the applicable model instructions and authorized facility procedures. | Objective 4. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.60, equipment maintenance](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.60) |
| 12 | Why does a preventive maintenance record matter separately from a successful pre-use check? | It documents scheduled equipment work, which serves a different purpose from readiness checks before use. | Preventive maintenance and pre-use verification address different tasks and timing. Each needs its own documentation under the facility program. | Objective 4. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.60, equipment maintenance](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.60) |
| 13 | A machine shows the same safety fault twice during required checks. What should you do within your role? | Prevent clinical use, label or segregate it per policy, and notify the responsible nurse and technical team. | Prevent patient exposure to equipment with an unresolved failure. Capture the asset and fault details so qualified staff can investigate. | Objective 4. [Philippine DOH · Dialysis facility standards](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/10/50687) |
| 14 | You receive verbal word that a held machine is probably repaired. What should you verify before making it available? | That authorized release and required post-service and pre-use checks are documented under facility policy | The return-to-service process verifies completion of required technical and clinical readiness steps. A tentative verbal update does not establish release. | Objective 4. [U.S. reference · 42 CFR §494.60, equipment maintenance](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-42/chapter-IV/subchapter-G/part-494/subpart-B/section-494.60) |
| 15 | A second shift needs to know why a machine remains unavailable. Which message best supports the handoff? | Asset HD-12 held after a required safety check failed at 06:50. Charge nurse and biomedical team notified. Service review and release are pending. | Communicate the equipment identity, observed problem, action taken and outstanding responsibility. This keeps the next team from releasing equipment prematurely. | Objective 4. [Philippine DOH · Dialysis facility standards](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/10/50687) |
| 16 | A dialyzer’s original total cell volume was 120 mL. Today it measures 93 mL. What is the correct decision? | Discard: it is below 80 percent of the original | 93 divided by 120 is about 78 percent, which is below the 80 percent limit. |  |
| 17 | In automated reprocessing, which task still depends entirely on a person? | Confirming the dialyzer belongs to the patient named in the record | No machine verifies identity; it will reprocess the wrong dialyzer perfectly. |  |
| 18 | Why is water pushed backward across the membrane during rinsing? | To lift clots out of the fibers | Reverse rinsing dislodges clots; too much pressure can tear fibers. |  |
| 19 | Before rinsing a stored dialyzer at the chair, the germicide presence test is negative. What does that mean? | The dialyzer may not have stayed disinfected and must not be used | No germicide in storage means no assurance of disinfection. |  |
| 20 | On one morning, every dialyzer fails the automated volume test. What is the most sensible first action? | Stop and repeat the machine’s calibration check | A fault shared by every dialyzer points to the equipment. |  |
| 21 | Which patient’s dialyzer is not reprocessed? | A patient with hepatitis B | Dialyzers from patients with hepatitis B are not reprocessed. |  |
| 22 | In semi-automated reprocessing, which step is most often missed? | The tests done by hand between device steps | Hand steps that sit between automatic steps are easy to forget. |  |
| 23 | Germicide splashes into a technician’s eye. What comes first? | Flush at the eyewash immediately | Flushing within seconds limits the injury; reporting follows. |  |

## Module 12: Integrated Practice and Competency Assessment

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which information should you compare before matching a patient to a dialysis order? | Two approved patient identifiers and the current authorized record | Two person-specific identifiers tie the patient to the correct record and treatment order. Location and schedule do not establish identity. | Objective 1. [Joint Commission · Two patient identifiers](https://www.jointcommission.org/en-us/knowledge-library/support-center/standards-interpretation/standards-faqs/000001463) |
| 2 | What does a dialysis water log record? | Assigned measurements, sampling details, results and any required action or release status | A usable log links results to the sampling location, time and required response. It lets staff verify whether the affected system is cleared. | Objective 1. [CDC · Dialysis water use and quality](https://www.cdc.gov/dialysis-safety/hcp/recommendations-resources/water-use-in-dialysis.html) |
| 3 | The station is clean and the equipment checks passed, but a required water result is marked pending. How should this affect readiness? | The affected station has an unresolved readiness requirement and remains on hold under site policy. | Cleanliness, equipment readiness and water quality are separate checks. Pending water verification prevents authorized release of affected use. | Objective 1. [CDC · Dialysis water use and quality](https://www.cdc.gov/dialysis-safety/hcp/recommendations-resources/water-use-in-dialysis.html) |
| 4 | At shift start, the water check remains unresolved and a patient identifier does not match the order. What is the safest response? | Hold the affected treatment start and report both discrepancies to the responsible staff for separate verification. | Both discrepancies need resolution through the appropriate pathways. Neither can be cleared using information from the other. | Objective 1. [Joint Commission · Two patient identifiers](https://www.jointcommission.org/en-us/knowledge-library/support-center/standards-interpretation/standards-faqs/000001463) |
| 5 | A machine passes its self-test, but the station disinfectant has not reached the required wet contact time. What should the trainee do? | Keep the station on hold until the required turnover procedure is complete and documented. | Machine and infection-control checks are independent. Complete the required disinfection process before introducing the patient or clean supplies. | Objective 1. [CDC · Dialysis station routine disinfection](https://www.cdc.gov/dialysis-safety/media/pdfs/env-checklist-508.pdf) |
| 6 | Which communication technique has the receiver repeat an instruction and the sender confirm or correct it? | Check-back | A check-back uses confirmation between team members to verify that the instruction was understood. | Objective 2. [AHRQ · TeamSTEPPS tools](https://www.ahrq.gov/teamstepps-program/resources/modules/index.html) |
| 7 | Which report gives the receiving nurse an actionable situation and request? | Station 2 is on hold because the identifier differs from the order and the water result is pending. Please confirm who will verify each exception. | A concise handoff names the affected station, identifies each observed exception, states the hold and requests action. | Objective 2. [AHRQ · TeamSTEPPS tools](https://www.ahrq.gov/teamstepps-program/resources/modules/index.html) |
| 8 | A preceptor says to hold a station until authorized release. The trainee repeats the instruction, but the preceptor has not confirmed. What remains incomplete? | The communication loop, which needs confirmation or correction | A repeat is followed by the sender’s confirmation or correction. Continue following the hold while clarifying the instruction. | Objective 2. [AHRQ · TeamSTEPPS tools](https://www.ahrq.gov/teamstepps-program/resources/modules/index.html) |
| 9 | During a mock treatment, the patient reports sudden chest pain and breathlessness. No alarm is active. What should you do first? | Call for immediate clinical help using the unit procedure, giving the station and observed symptoms. | Urgent symptoms require immediate escalation. State the location and observations, then assist within your assigned emergency role. | Objective 2. [National Kidney Foundation · Hemodialysis treatment day checklist](https://www.kidney.org/sites/default/files/2026-09/441-0518_2608-patflyer_checklist_hemodialysis-v2.pdf) |
| 10 | The nurse asks you to notify the water lead while the nurse verifies the patient identifiers. What action best closes the handoff? | Confirm the assignment, notify the water lead and report back with the response and any pending action. | A handoff is useful when responsibility is explicit and follow-through is reported. Each unresolved hold remains active until appropriately cleared. | Objective 2. [AHRQ · TeamSTEPPS tools](https://www.ahrq.gov/teamstepps-program/resources/modules/index.html) |
| 11 | What is a near miss? | A safety event intercepted before it reached the patient | A near miss reveals a hazard or process weakness even when intervention prevented patient exposure. | Objective 3. [AHRQ · Event reporting, investigation and analysis](https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/candor/modules/notes4.html) |
| 12 | Which proposed outcome would best show whether a new pre-use water-readiness checklist is being followed? | The number of shifts with a completed and verified water-readiness entry before first treatment divided by all shifts audited | This measures the intended behavior with a defined denominator and can be compared across audits. | Objective 3. [AHRQ · Event reporting, investigation and analysis](https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/candor/modules/notes4.html) |
| 13 | A learner passed the written quiz but missed a required critical step in the simulated emergency station. What does that mean for skills sign-off? | The learner needs feedback and an observed reassessment of the critical step before sign-off under the course rubric. | Written knowledge and observed performance are distinct. Capstone sign-off requires evidence that the learner can complete required critical steps. | Objective 4. [Module 12 study guide · Capstone sign-off](Study%20Guide.md) |
| 14 | A trainee has observed a procedure but has not been signed off for it. A colleague asks them to perform it without the assigned supervisor. What should the trainee do? | Clarify authority and supervision with the assigned preceptor and remain within the approved role. | Observation records exposure. Authorization for supervised or independent performance requires the applicable competency and facility process. | Objective 4. [Philippine DOH · Dialysis facility standards](https://elibrary.judiciary.gov.ph/thebookshelf/showdocs/10/50687) |
| 15 | The identifier mismatch is caught before treatment. Which entry and follow-up best support improvement? | Record the observed mismatch, time, hold, notification and outcome in the approved system, then contribute facts to the near-miss review. | Objective chronology supports review of how the issue reached the station, how it was caught and which process could be tested for improvement. | Objective 3. [AHRQ · Event reporting, investigation and analysis](https://www.ahrq.gov/patient-safety/settings/hospital/candor/modules/notes4.html) |
| 16 | In the opening walk-through, why are the water room and chlorine test done before stations are set up? | A water problem stops every station, so finding it first avoids preparing stations that cannot be used | Checks with the widest effect come first. |  |
| 17 | The total chlorine test reads 0.3 mg/L against a limit of 0.1 mg/L. A repeat test reads lower. What do you record? | Both results with times and sample point, and the hold stays until the designated person releases it | Every result is recorded, and a better second test does not cancel a failed first one. |  |
| 18 | A patient’s target weight is 58.0 kg and the confirmed pre-weight is 63.4 kg. What is the gain above target weight? | 5.4 kg | 63.4 minus 58.0 equals 5.4 kg, about 5,400 mL. |  |
| 19 | A low venous pressure alarm sounds and the patient is dozing under a blanket. What is your first move? | Go to the patient and lift the blanket to see the access and lines | A fall in venous pressure can mean a dislodged needle bleeding out of sight. |  |
| 20 | At a practical station you perform two of three criteria satisfactorily and miss the third. What is the station result? | Not yet satisfactory; you practice and retest on a different scenario | A station is satisfactory only when every criterion is, and stations are not averaged. |  |
| 21 | On the self-rating grid, which description matches “performs independently”? | Correct, complete, and in order without prompts, and can say why each step is there | Independence means no prompts and an understanding of the purpose of each step. |  |
| 22 | You were signed off on one machine model and the unit installs a different model. What should you do? | Ask to be trained and checked on the new model before working on it alone | Competence is specific to the task and the machine model. |  |

# Water Treatment System Technician

## Module 1: Why Dialysis Water Is Different

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why must dialysis water be cleaner than drinking water? | Contaminants cross the dialyzer membrane directly into the blood, in very large weekly volumes | The gut protects a person who drinks water. In dialysis, hundreds of liters a week are separated from blood only by a membrane. |  |
| 2 | Which contaminant destroys red blood cells and is removed mainly by carbon tanks? | Chloramine | Chloramine and chlorine cause hemolysis. Activated carbon is the barrier against them. |  |
| 3 | What is endotoxin? | A fragment of dead bacteria that can cause fever and chills | Endotoxin comes from the cell wall of certain bacteria and can cause reactions even when no live bacteria are found. |  |
| 4 | In the international standard, what is the maximum total chlorine in dialysis water? | 0.1 mg/L | The limit is 0.1 mg/L, far below the level in tap water. |  |
| 5 | Which fluid has the strictest limits for bacteria and endotoxin? | Ultrapure dialysis fluid | Ultrapure fluid has limits of less than 0.1 CFU/mL and less than 0.03 EU/mL. |  |
| 6 | What is an action level? | A result below the maximum at which the unit must already act | Acting at the action level keeps results from ever reaching the limit. |  |
| 7 | The utility announces a higher disinfectant dose. The reverse osmosis display is normal. What should you do first? | Test total chlorine after the carbon tanks and inform the nurse | The reverse osmosis display does not measure chlorine. Test where chlorine is removed and tell the nurse before patients are connected. |  |
| 8 | Who carries overall responsibility for dialysis water quality in a facility? | The medical director | The medical director is responsible. The technician’s duty is to test, record, stop, and report. |  |
| 9 | A test kit reports hardness as 2 grains per gallon. About how many mg/L as calcium carbonate is that? | 34 | One grain per gallon is about 17 mg/L, so two is about 34 mg/L. |  |
| 10 | Several patients’ hemoglobin levels fall over two months with no other explanation. Which water problem should a technician think of first? | Low-level chloramine breakthrough | Chloramine damages red blood cells and can show first as a pattern of worsening anemia. |  |

## Module 2: Source Water and System Design

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A unit switches from city water to a deep well. What is true of the well water? | It usually has no disinfectant and is often harder | Well water is typically undisinfected and mineral-rich, so the softener works harder and microbial control needs attention. |  |
| 2 | Which valve must be closed and locked or tagged during treatment? | Carbon tank bypass valve | An open carbon bypass sends chlorine and chloramine directly downstream. |  |
| 3 | Iron in the feed water mainly threatens which components? | The softener resin and the membrane | Iron fouls resin and membranes and leaves reddish deposits. |  |
| 4 | Why does adding stations threaten the carbon tanks even if the reverse osmosis unit keeps up? | Higher flow shortens contact time in the carbon bed | Contact time is bed volume divided by flow; more flow means less time. |  |
| 5 | What is validation? | Documented proof that the system produces water within limits before patients use it | Validation is the evidence that a new or changed system is fit for use. |  |
| 6 | A tanker delivers water during a shortage. How is it handled? | Tested and treated as a new source | Each delivery can differ, so it is tested before use. |  |
| 7 | After a service visit, what is checked before release? | That every valve is back in its normal position | Valves left in service positions are a common and dangerous error. |  |
| 8 | On a feed water report, which line most directly tells you how hard the softener must work? | Hardness | Hardness is the calcium and magnesium the softener must remove. |  |

## Module 3: Sediment Filtration and Softening

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A filter reads 58 psi before and 46 psi after. What is the pressure drop? | 12 psi | 58 minus 46 is 12 psi. Compare it with the unit’s limit and with earlier readings. |  |
| 2 | What does a softener remove? | Calcium and magnesium | The resin exchanges calcium and magnesium for sodium, protecting the membrane from scale. |  |
| 3 | Why are softeners set to regenerate outside treatment hours? | Brine could reach the machines and the softener is off line during the cycle | A valve or timer fault during regeneration can send salt water downstream. |  |
| 4 | After a power interruption, which softener check comes first? | The clock and regeneration setting | A wrong clock moves regeneration into treatment hours. |  |
| 5 | During a backwash, in which direction does water flow through the bed? | Upward, lifting the bed | Upward flow lifts the media and carries trapped dirt to drain. |  |
| 6 | Sand appears in the drain during backwash. What does this suggest? | Flow is too high or an internal part is broken | Media should stay in the tank; losing it means a fault. |  |
| 7 | A clock-based softener runs out early during an unusually busy week. Why? | It regenerates by time, not by the amount of water used | More water used means the resin is exhausted before the scheduled regeneration. |  |
| 8 | Pressure is normal at the feed gauge and after the sediment filter, but low after the softener. Where is the problem? | In or around the softener | The fault lies between the last good gauge and the first bad one. |  |

## Module 4: Carbon Tanks and Chlorine Control

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A carbon bed of 240 liters receives 40 liters per minute. What is the empty-bed contact time? | 6 minutes | 240 divided by 40 is 6 minutes. |  |
| 2 | Chlorine is detected after the first carbon tank but not after the second. What does this mean? | The worker tank is becoming exhausted; the polisher is protecting patients, and the unit’s response plan starts | Breakthrough after the worker is a warning to increase testing and arrange replacement as the procedure states. |  |
| 3 | Does backwashing restore exhausted carbon? | No; exhausted carbon must be replaced | Backwashing prevents channeling but does not renew the carbon’s capacity. |  |
| 4 | A new 5-micrometer cartridge clogs in three days. What is the best interpretation? | Something upstream, such as the carbon or the softener, is shedding particles | Rapid clogging points to a problem upstream that needs to be found. |  |
| 5 | Which test detects chloramine? | Total chlorine test | Total chlorine includes free chlorine and chloramine. A free chlorine test misses chloramine. |  |
| 6 | Where is the first chlorine sample of the day taken? | After the first (worker) carbon tank | Testing after the worker shows whether it is still doing its job. |  |
| 7 | Chlorine is above the limit after the second carbon tank. What happens next? | Stop treatments on this water and inform the nurse and medical director at once | Breakthrough after the polisher means chlorine can reach patients. |  |
| 8 | Why must the color be read at exactly the stated time? | The color keeps changing, so an early or late reading gives a wrong value | Test chemistry is timed; follow the kit instructions. |  |
| 9 | A unit uses a free chlorine test. The utility switches to chloramine. What will the test show? | Little or nothing, even though chloramine is present | A free chlorine test does not detect chloramine. |  |
| 10 | Why test the feed water with the same kit once a week? | To prove the kit can show a positive result | A kit that cannot read positive on chlorinated feed water cannot be trusted. |  |
| 11 | After carbon replacement, what must be done before release? | Rinse until clear and test total chlorine after each tank at working flow | New carbon sheds fines and must be proven to remove chlorine. |  |
| 12 | Which carbon is not used for dialysis water treatment? | Regenerated (reactivated) carbon | Regenerated carbon may carry contaminants from its previous use. |  |

## Module 5: Reverse Osmosis

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What passes through a reverse osmosis membrane most easily? | Water | Pressure forces water through; most dissolved and suspended material is held back. |  |
| 2 | Feed conductivity is 500 and product conductivity is 25. What is the percent rejection? | 95% | (500 − 25) ÷ 500 × 100 = 95%. |  |
| 3 | Feed flow is 20 liters per minute and product flow is 12. What is the percent recovery? | 60% | 12 ÷ 20 × 100 = 60%. |  |
| 4 | Rejection has fallen steadily for three weeks but no alarm has sounded. What should you do? | Report the trend and record that you reported it | A trend is information. Reporting it early prevents a failure. |  |
| 5 | Which of these does conductivity NOT detect? | Chloramine | Chlorine and chloramine at harmful levels barely change conductivity. Only the chlorine test detects them. |  |
| 6 | Product flow drops on an unusually cold morning with no other change. What is the most likely reason? | Cold water passes through the membrane more slowly | Membrane output falls as feed water temperature falls. |  |
| 7 | Product flow is rising and rejection is falling. What is the most likely cause? | Membrane damage, classically from chlorine | A damaged membrane lets more water and more salt through. |  |
| 8 | The low feed pressure alarm stops the pump. What is checked first? | Supply, prefilter, and whether a tank is in backwash or regeneration | Low feed pressure originates upstream of the pump. |  |

## Module 6: The Distribution Loop, Storage, and Final Filters

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does the loop return water to its starting point? | To keep water moving so bacteria have less chance to settle | Stagnant water allows biofilm to form. |  |
| 2 | What is a dead leg? | A branch of pipe where water does not circulate | Dead legs let bacteria grow and seed the rest of the loop. |  |
| 3 | Cultures are under 5 CFU/mL at the loop start and 38 CFU/mL at the loop return. What does this suggest? | Bacteria are being added along the loop | A clean start and a dirty return point to a source within the loop, such as a dead leg. |  |
| 4 | What is the main weakness of a tank-fed system? | The tank is a common site of contamination | Stored water gives bacteria time and surfaces to grow on. |  |
| 5 | Why is an ultrafilter placed after an ultraviolet lamp? | To remove dead bacteria and the endotoxin they release | The lamp kills bacteria but does not remove them or their fragments. |  |
| 6 | An ultraviolet lamp is glowing. Does that prove it is working? | No; output falls with age and a coated sleeve blocks light | Lamp hours, intensity readings, and sleeve cleaning show whether it is effective. |  |
| 7 | Which material must not contact product water? | Copper or brass | Very pure water dissolves copper, brass, zinc, and aluminum into the water. |  |
| 8 | Loop cultures are clean, but dialysis fluid from two machines is above the action level. Where do you look? | The connection hoses, the machines, and the bicarbonate | The contamination is entering after the loop. |  |

## Module 7: Concentrates and Central Delivery

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why are acid and bicarbonate supplied as two separate concentrates? | Calcium and magnesium would react with bicarbonate and form a solid if stored together | Keeping them apart until dilution prevents precipitation. |  |
| 2 | Two acid concentrates differ only in potassium. Will the machine’s conductivity alarm tell them apart? | No; conductivity is nearly the same | Conductivity measures total salts; the label is the safeguard. |  |
| 3 | Which water is used to mix bicarbonate concentrate? | Dialysis water | Only treated dialysis water is used. |  |
| 4 | Why should bicarbonate not be mixed vigorously for a long time? | Carbon dioxide is lost and the solution changes | Over-mixing drives off carbon dioxide and makes the solution more alkaline. |  |
| 5 | A bicarbonate container is half full of yesterday’s batch. What is correct? | Discard, clean and disinfect the container, then prepare a fresh batch | Old solution seeds bacteria into the new batch and is past its use time. |  |
| 6 | Low conductivity alarms occur on six machines at once shortly after a new bicarbonate batch goes on line. What is the likely cause? | The batch, or the supply running low or drawing air | Simultaneous alarms point to the shared supply. |  |
| 7 | By convention, which color marks acid concentrate connectors? | Red | Acid is red and bicarbonate is blue. |  |
| 8 | A mixed batch fails verification. What do you do? | Discard it and prepare a new batch | An out-of-range batch is discarded, not corrected. |  |

## Module 8: Daily Monitoring, Logs, and Trends

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which log entry is acceptable? | “0.04 mg/L total chlorine, port after tank 1, 05:40, J. Cruz” | A usable record gives the number, unit, place, time, and person. |  |
| 2 | You forgot the midday chlorine test. What do you write? | That the test was not done, why, and whom you told; then test now | Records must be true. Report the gap and test immediately. |  |
| 3 | Pressure drop across the sediment filter has risen a little every week for a month. No limit has been reached. What should you do? | Report the trend so that a backwash or change can be planned | Trends allow planned maintenance instead of emergency repair. |  |
| 4 | Product conductivity is excellent today. What does this tell you about chlorine? | Nothing; conductivity does not measure chlorine | Each check answers one question only. |  |
| 5 | What is a baseline? | The usual value of a reading when the system is healthy | Baselines come from your own system in good condition. |  |
| 6 | A log shows the same pressure reading to the decimal for three weeks. What does this most likely indicate? | Copied entries or a stuck gauge | Real systems vary slightly day to day. |  |
| 7 | Who should carry out the weekly log review? | A second person | Fresh eyes catch drift the daily recorder has stopped seeing. |  |
| 8 | What is the last step of the start-of-day round? | Telling the charge nurse the system is released, and signing | Release is a stated, recorded handover. |  |

## Module 9: Chemical Analysis and Microbiological Sampling

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the international standard, what is the bacteria limit for dialysis water? | Less than 100 CFU/mL | The limit is less than 100 CFU/mL, with an action level typically at 50. |  |
| 2 | A culture result lies between the action level and the limit. What does this mean? | Act now, while there is still a margin | Action levels exist so that the limit is never reached. |  |
| 3 | When are routine culture samples best taken? | Just before a scheduled disinfection | Sampling before disinfection shows the highest level patients were exposed to. |  |
| 4 | Why is the sample port flushed before collecting? | To clear water and bacteria that sat in the port, so the sample represents the loop | Stagnant water in the tap does not represent the system. |  |
| 5 | How often is chemical analysis of product water done, at a minimum, in most monitoring plans? | At least yearly, and after a change of source or membrane | Chemical contaminants change slowly, but any change of source or membrane calls for a new analysis. |  |
| 6 | A laboratory cultures dialysis water at body temperature for 48 hours. What is the likely effect? | Falsely low counts | Water bacteria grow slowly and need low-nutrient media at room temperature for about a week. |  |
| 7 | Endotoxin is high but the culture is low. How should this be treated? | As a real finding needing investigation | Endotoxin comes from dead as well as live bacteria. |  |
| 8 | How should culture samples be stored before transport? | Refrigerated | Refrigeration holds the count steady; warmth raises it and freezing lowers it. |  |

## Module 10: Disinfection and Biofilm Control

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is biofilm? | A layer of bacteria attached to a surface inside a protective slime | Biofilm sheds bacteria and endotoxin and resists disinfectant. |  |
| 2 | After a chemical disinfection, what must be shown before any patient is connected? | A negative residual test at every outlet | Residual disinfectant reaching a patient causes harm. |  |
| 3 | Why must bleach never be mixed with acid products? | It releases chlorine gas | Chlorine gas is toxic. Use and store these products separately. |  |
| 4 | Several patients on one shift develop chills at about the same time. What is the technician’s first step? | Tell the charge nurse at once that the water or dialysate may be involved | Report first. The clinical team decides about patients, and samples should be taken before disinfection. |  |
| 5 | A procedure calls for 100 liters at 2 percent. The stock is 10 percent. How much stock is needed? | 20 liters | (2 × 100) ÷ 10 = 20 liters, made up to 100 liters. |  |
| 6 | Why are two different test strips used during chemical disinfection? | One proves full strength arrived; a more sensitive one proves it has been rinsed out | High-range and residual strips answer different questions. |  |
| 7 | Counts return to their old level two weeks after disinfection. What does this suggest? | Mature biofilm or a source seeding the loop | Rapid regrowth means something survived or keeps reseeding. |  |
| 8 | Why might the reverse osmosis membrane and the loop have different disinfection procedures? | Some agents that suit the loop, such as bleach, damage many membranes | Agent compatibility differs between components. |  |

## Module 11: Maintenance, Component Replacement, and Validation

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What triggers carbon replacement before its scheduled date? | Chlorine breakthrough after the worker tank | Breakthrough shows the carbon is exhausted. |  |
| 2 | After carbon is replaced, which check is essential before release? | Total chlorine within limit after each tank at working flow | New carbon must be proven to remove chlorine, and the bypass must be closed. |  |
| 3 | A new reverse osmosis membrane has been fitted. What should be recorded? | Rejection, flows, and pressures as the new baseline | Future readings are judged against the new baseline. |  |
| 4 | A section of loop pipe was opened for repair. What is required? | Disinfection, rinse with negative residual, and cultures as the plan states | Opening the loop can introduce bacteria. |  |
| 5 | An engineer leaves a report saying only “Serviced. OK.” What is correct? | Ask for work done, parts, test results, and final valve positions | A report must give evidence that can be checked later. |  |
| 6 | Why write the installation date on each replaceable part? | So anyone can see when it is due for replacement | Visible dates prevent overdue parts. |  |
| 7 | A pressure gauge has read exactly the same value for a month. What should you suspect? | A stuck gauge | Gauges fail too; compare with a reference. |  |
| 8 | What is the first step before any maintenance that takes a stage out of service? | Agree the timing with the charge nurse and confirm no patient is on dialysis | Maintenance is planned around patient treatment. |  |

## Module 12: Failures, Emergencies, and Capstone

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What are the first three moves for any out-of-range water result? | Stop, hold, report | Stop new starts, place a hold, and report. Investigation follows. |  |
| 2 | The first chlorine test is above the limit; a repeat is below it. What is correct? | Keep the hold until the responsible person is satisfied | Conflicting results mean the true value is unknown. |  |
| 3 | Who decides what happens to patients already on dialysis when the water is in doubt? | The nurse and physician | The technician reports quickly; clinical decisions belong to the clinical team. |  |
| 4 | Several patients have fever and chills and contamination is suspected. Why sample before disinfecting? | Disinfection would destroy the evidence of what patients were exposed to | Samples taken afterward cannot show what was in the water during the event. |  |
| 5 | Power returns after a long outage. What should be checked before treatment resumes? | Timers, a flush of the loop, and the start-of-day tests | A restart is not a release. Timers drift and stagnant water may have grown bacteria. |  |
| 6 | Delivered tanker water arrives during a supply cut. How should it be regarded? | As a new source that must be tested | A different source can bring different contaminants. |  |
| 7 | Which is a root cause rather than a description? | There was no reorder point for replacement carbon | A root cause is something that can be changed to prevent a repeat. |  |
| 8 | Before handling a disinfectant for the first time, what do you read? | The safety data sheet | The safety data sheet gives hazards, protective equipment, and first aid. |  |
| 9 | In an urgent water report, which fact tells the nurse how many patients are at risk right now? | Which stations are running and whether patients are connected | The clinical team’s first decision concerns patients already on dialysis. |  |
| 10 | You cannot reach the charge nurse within a minute during a breakthrough. What do you do? | Go up the unit’s escalation list | A hold must be communicated; use the next contact. |  |

# Hemodialysis Center Biomedical Technician

## Module 1: The Biomedical Technician’s Role and Safe Working

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Who decides that a repaired machine is technically fit, and who accepts it for patient use? | The biomedical technician certifies fitness; the charge nurse accepts it for clinical use | Technical fitness and clinical use are separate decisions made by different people. |  |
| 2 | How should any machine that has been used on patients be regarded before service? | As contaminated until disinfected | Treat every used machine as contaminated: disinfect it, wear protective equipment, and clean hands afterward. |  |
| 3 | What is the purpose of lockout/tagout? | To prevent equipment from being energized while someone works on it | Locking and labeling the energy source protects the person working inside. |  |
| 4 | A machine passes its self-test after an unexplained conductivity alarm. What does this prove? | Only that the tested alarm functions work; the cause is still unknown | Self-tests verify protective functions; they do not diagnose an earlier fault. |  |
| 5 | Why must repairs follow the manufacturer’s service manual? | Improvised repairs can remove a protection the operator still relies on | Safety depends on the machine being maintained exactly as designed. |  |
| 6 | What is the main purpose of an out-of-service tag? | To make the machine’s status obvious to everyone so it is not used by mistake | Most equipment errors are communication errors. |  |
| 7 | Which practice protects circuit boards from static damage? | A grounded wrist strap and antistatic bags | Static discharge can damage components without visible signs. |  |
| 8 | A machine assigned to the hepatitis B isolation area is repaired in the workshop. Where does it go afterward? | Back to the same assigned area | Machine assignment is a clinical infection-control rule; servicing does not change it. |  |
| 9 | Before using a service procedure, which three things should match the machine in front of you? | Model, software version, and manual revision | Procedures and tolerances are specific to model and software version. |  |
| 10 | You find dried blood around an internal pressure port. What does this tell you? | A transducer protector failed or was wetted in use; decontaminate by the manual and inform the charge nurse | Blood inside the machine is an infection risk and a sign of a use problem to correct. |  |

## Module 2: Electrical and Electronic Fundamentals

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the water picture of electricity, what is voltage most like? | The water pressure | Voltage is the push; current is the flow; resistance is the restriction. |  |
| 2 | A heater element of 23 ohms is connected to 230 volts. About how much current flows? | 10 amperes | Current = voltage ÷ resistance = 230 ÷ 23 = 10 amperes. |  |
| 3 | A fuse blows a second time after replacement. What is correct? | Take the machine out of service and find the fault | A repeated blown fuse signals a real fault; a larger fuse removes the protection. |  |
| 4 | What does the protective earth connection do? | Carries fault current safely away so the case does not become live | It makes a fault blow the fuse instead of energizing the case. |  |
| 5 | Which measurement must only be made with the machine unplugged? | Resistance | Resistance and continuity are measured on dead circuits. |  |
| 6 | A sensor reading is jumping and a connector feels warm. What kind of fault is likely? | High-resistance joint | Loose or corroded connections cause heat and intermittent readings. |  |
| 7 | Why do machines have two sensors for safety-critical quantities? | One controls and an independent one protects | Independence means one failed sensor cannot both cause and hide a hazard. |  |
| 8 | How should a circuit board be handled? | By its edges, with a grounded wrist strap | Static discharge can damage components invisibly. |  |

## Module 3: Dialysate Side I: Water Inlet, Heating, and Degassing

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is water degassed before concentrates are added? | Air bubbles cause false conductivity readings and disturb fluid balance | Dissolved air released as bubbles interferes with sensors, the balancing system, and the membrane. |  |
| 2 | Why must dialysate never be allowed to overheat? | Overheated dialysate damages red blood cells | Excess heat causes hemolysis, so machines have independent temperature protection. |  |
| 3 | What does the heat exchanger do? | Uses warmth from used dialysate to pre-warm incoming water | It recovers heat without mixing the two fluids. |  |
| 4 | Six machines alarm for low water at shift start. Where is the cause most likely? | The water supply | Simultaneous alarms point to something shared. |  |
| 5 | The display reads lower than a reference thermometer. Why is this the more worrying direction? | Dialysate is warmer than shown | A machine under-reading temperature may deliver dialysate that is too hot. |  |
| 6 | A heater has power but water is not flowing past it. What happens? | It overheats quickly | Heaters depend on flow to carry heat away; protection must cut power. |  |
| 7 | One machine warms slowly while its neighbors are normal. What do you check before replacing parts? | Its descaling history and inlet filter | Scale and restricted inlet flow are the common causes. |  |
| 8 | Bubbles in the dialysate lines and unstable conductivity suggest a problem with which stage? | Degassing | Poor degassing lets air reach the sensors and the balancing system. |  |

## Module 4: Dialysate Side II: Proportioning, Conductivity, and Bypass

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does dialysate conductivity measure? | The total amount of dissolved salts | Conductivity reflects total ionic content, not which salts are present. |  |
| 2 | What is bypass? | Diverting dialysate to the drain so it does not reach the dialyzer | Bypass is the safe state when dialysate temperature or conductivity is out of limits. |  |
| 3 | Why can a wrong concentrate sometimes produce a normal conductivity reading? | Conductivity measures the total salts, not their identity | Different recipes can give similar totals, which is why labels are checked. |  |
| 4 | Against what is a machine’s conductivity sensor verified? | A calibrated reference meter | Only an independent, calibrated instrument can show whether the sensor is right. |  |
| 5 | By roughly how much does conductivity change per degree Celsius? | 2 percent | About 2 percent per degree, which is why readings are temperature-compensated. |  |
| 6 | Your reference meter and the machine disagree by several percent, and both pass their own checks. What should you suspect first? | Different reference temperatures | Mismatched temperature compensation gives a false disagreement. |  |
| 7 | A nurse asks you to adjust conductivity so an alarm stops. What is correct? | Decline; find the cause and calibrate only if a verified reference shows the sensor is wrong | Calibration is not an alarm-silencing tool. |  |
| 8 | A machine borrowed from another center will not stabilize conductivity. What do you check? | Whether its configured concentrate system matches the concentrates in use | A mismatch between configuration and concentrate gives the wrong mixture. |  |

## Module 5: Fluid Balance and Ultrafiltration Control

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In a balancing-chamber machine, where does the fluid taken by the ultrafiltration pump come from? | Across the membrane, from the patient’s blood | The loop is closed and balanced, so any extra volume removed must cross the membrane. |  |
| 2 | One machine consistently leaves patients heavier than expected, with no alarms. What is the most likely area of fault? | The fluid balance system or ultrafiltration pump calibration | A small, steady error in balancing or pump delivery may not trigger an alarm. |  |
| 3 | A machine is set to remove 2,000 mL and you collect 2,100 mL in the test. What is the error? | +5 percent | (2,100 − 2,000) ÷ 2,000 = +5 percent: it removes more than it reports. |  |
| 4 | Why is the tightness test done before the ultrafiltration accuracy check? | A leaking loop makes pump calibration meaningless | Fluid entering or leaving through a leak would be mistaken for pump error. |  |
| 5 | Transmembrane pressure rises steadily through a treatment. What is a likely cause? | Fibers are clotting | Less open membrane means more pressure is needed for the same removal. |  |
| 6 | On a volumetric machine, what does the operator set? | The volume to remove; the machine produces the pressure needed | Pressure is the result, not the setting. |  |
| 7 | Which is an independent daily check on every machine’s fluid removal? | Patient weights before and after treatment | Weights reveal steady errors that produce no alarm. |  |
| 8 | Wet, worn seals on the dialyzer connectors can cause which problem? | Air drawn in or fluid lost from the balanced loop | The connectors are part of the closed loop. |  |

## Module 6: The Blood Side: Pump, Pressures, and Heparin Pump

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How does the machine arrive at the blood flow shown on the screen? | It calculates it from pump speed and tubing size | Displayed flow is calculated, so wrong tubing size or poor occlusion makes it untrue. |  |
| 2 | Why is pre-pump arterial pressure normally negative? | The pump is pulling blood from the access | Suction ahead of the pump produces negative pressure. |  |
| 3 | What is the purpose of a transducer protector? | To keep blood out of the machine’s pressure sensor | It is a barrier filter; a wet one gives false readings. |  |
| 4 | Can a venous pressure alarm be relied on to detect a dislodged needle? | No; pressure may change too little to alarm | Most of the resistance is in the needle, so pressure can stay within limits. The access must remain visible. |  |
| 5 | The display shows 350 mL/min. A delivery test collects 1,575 mL in 5 minutes. What is the true flow? | 315 mL/min | 1,575 ÷ 5 = 315 mL/min, 10 percent below the display. |  |
| 6 | The unit changes to a different brand of heparin syringe. What must be checked on every machine? | The syringe pump’s configured syringe type and its delivery | Delivery is calculated from syringe diameter; a different syringe changes the dose. |  |
| 7 | A pressure display does not read zero with the port open to air. What is needed? | Zero calibration by the manual | Zero drift is corrected by calibration. |  |
| 8 | Why must the hand crank be present on every machine? | To return the patient’s blood if power fails | It is part of the emergency procedure for power loss. |  |

## Module 7: Protective Systems and Alarm Testing

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What must happen when the air detector senses air above its limit? | The blood pump stops and the venous clamp closes, with an alarm | Detection must trigger automatic action, not just a warning. |  |
| 2 | What does the blood leak detector examine? | Used dialysate, for blood that has crossed a broken membrane | It looks for blood in dialysate leaving the dialyzer. |  |
| 3 | A protective system cannot be made to pass its test before the next shift. What is correct? | Keep the machine out of service | A machine without a working protective system is not used. |  |
| 4 | Which is a likely cause of an air detector failing to alarm? | The venous line is not seated properly in the detector | Incorrect seating, a wrong tubing set, or a dirty or drifted sensor can all defeat detection. |  |
| 5 | What does single-fault safety mean? | One failure alone must not be able to harm the patient | Independent protective systems catch a failure in the control system. |  |
| 6 | What can the machine’s start-up self-test not prove? | That sensors are accurate and correctly aligned with the tubing | Accuracy and alignment are shown by periodic functional tests. |  |
| 7 | Operators report frequent false blood leak alarms on one machine. How should this be treated? | As a fault to find and fix | False alarms teach staff to ignore real ones. |  |
| 8 | You find a jumper wire across a door switch. What do you do? | Remove the machine from service, repair, and report | A defeated safety device is a serious hidden hazard. |  |

## Module 8: The Preventive Maintenance Program

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is preventive maintenance? | Scheduled work done before failure occurs | It replaces wearing parts and checks performance on a schedule. |  |
| 2 | A center has ten machines with a six-month maintenance interval. About how many visits are needed per year? | 20 | Ten machines, twice a year each, is twenty visits. |  |
| 3 | A center has fourteen machines on a six-month interval. How many visits are due each year? | 28 | Fourteen machines, twice a year each, is twenty-eight visits. |  |
| 4 | Why should patient scales be in the equipment inventory? | A wrong weight leads to wrong fluid removal | Scales and blood pressure monitors drive clinical decisions and need checking. |  |
| 5 | What is the first step when a manufacturer’s safety notice arrives? | Identify which machines are affected by model, serial range, and software version | Action starts with knowing which of your machines are covered. |  |
| 6 | Before starting a maintenance visit, whom should you ask about the machine? | The operators who use it | Operators know the intermittent problems. |  |
| 7 | Most of your downtime is machines waiting for parts. What should change? | The spare parts stock list | Downtime reasons tell you where the program is weak. |  |
| 8 | After a software update, what must be recorded? | The old and new versions, the tests repeated, and the date | Software version is part of the machine’s identity and history. |  |

## Module 9: Calibration and Test Equipment

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does calibration mean? | Comparing a reading with a trusted reference and adjusting to match | Calibration ties the machine’s reading to a known value. |  |
| 2 | Why record the as-found value before adjusting? | It shows how far out the machine was, which may matter for patients already treated | A large as-found error is clinical information. |  |
| 3 | A reference meter is past its calibration due date. What is correct? | Do not use it until it is calibrated | An unverified reference can make every machine wrong in the same direction. |  |
| 4 | What does traceable mean for a reference instrument? | Its accuracy is linked by documented calibrations to a national or international standard | Traceability is the documented chain behind the instrument’s accuracy. |  |
| 5 | A reference meter is discovered to have been reading wrong. What must be reviewed? | Every machine it was used on since it was last known to be correct | All work done with a faulty reference is in doubt. |  |
| 6 | Which item belongs in every service record? | The identity and calibration due date of the test instruments used | Without the instrument’s identity, the measurements cannot be traced. |  |
| 7 | The tolerance is ±0.5 °C. The machine shows 37.0 and a reference thermometer reads 37.4. What is correct? | Within tolerance; record the as-found value and watch the trend | A difference of 0.4 is within ±0.5, but close enough to follow. |  |
| 8 | Why should you never dip a probe directly into the stock bottle of standard solution? | It contaminates the stock and changes its value | Pour out a portion and discard it after use. |  |

## Module 10: Disinfection, Descaling, and Machine Fluid Hygiene

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is scale inside a dialysis machine mostly made of? | Calcium carbonate from bicarbonate dialysate | Calcium and magnesium combine with bicarbonate to form a hard crust. |  |
| 2 | Which agent removes scale? | An acid such as citric acid | Acid dissolves carbonate scale; bleach does not. |  |
| 3 | Why must bleach and acid programs never be run back to back without rinsing? | They release chlorine gas | The mixture releases toxic chlorine gas. |  |
| 4 | What does a residual test show? | That no disinfectant remains after rinsing | It protects the patient from chemical remaining in the fluid path. |  |
| 5 | Residual test strips have run out. What is correct? | Do not use the machine until a residual test is negative | Without a valid test there is no proof the chemical is gone. |  |
| 6 | The machine displays “disinfection complete.” What still has to be shown before use after a chemical program? | A negative residual test | Completion of the program does not prove the chemical was rinsed out. |  |
| 7 | Why must the drain hose end above the drain with a visible gap? | To prevent waste water being drawn back into the machine | An air gap makes backflow physically impossible. |  |
| 8 | A dialysis fluid filter fails its integrity test. What is correct? | Replace it by the manual | A failed filter may pass bacteria and endotoxin. |  |

## Module 11: Electrical Safety Testing and Facility Utilities

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the purpose of the protective earth connection? | To carry fault current safely away from the case | It prevents the case from becoming live if insulation fails. |  |
| 2 | When is an electrical safety test required apart from the scheduled interval? | After repairs affecting electrical safety, or after the machine is dropped or flooded | Any event that could affect insulation or earthing calls for a retest. |  |
| 3 | Why are dialysis patients especially vulnerable to small electrical currents? | They are connected to the machine by conductive blood and saline, sometimes through a catheter near the heart | A direct conductive path makes very small currents hazardous. |  |
| 4 | A nurse feels a tingle from a machine’s metal case. What is the correct first action? | Unplug the machine, tag it out, and keep the station out of use | A tingle means current is flowing through a person; the hazard is immediate. |  |
| 5 | Why is the power cord flexed during the protective earth test? | A broken strand may connect in one position only | Flexing reveals intermittent breaks. |  |
| 6 | Why are extension leads not used for dialysis machines? | They add resistance to the earth path, overload, and may lack an earth | The earth path must be direct and reliable. |  |
| 7 | On mains failure, what does a machine’s internal battery commonly keep running? | The blood pump, air detector, and alarms for a limited time | Batteries typically support the blood side briefly; check the model’s instructions. |  |
| 8 | A machine passes its electrical safety test but its wall outlet has no earth. Is the patient protected? | No; the earth path depends on the outlet and building wiring too | Protection is a chain from the building to the machine. |  |

## Module 12: Troubleshooting, Life Cycle, and Capstone

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Three machines fed by the same concentrate line alarm for low conductivity within minutes. What is the most likely cause? | A problem with the shared concentrate supply | Simultaneous faults on several machines point to something they share. |  |
| 2 | What is the first step in fault-finding? | Gather the exact alarm text and the circumstances | Facts first; they often identify the cause without opening the machine. |  |
| 3 | Why check simple causes first? | They are commonly the cause, and it avoids disturbing a healthy machine | Supplies, connections, water, and settings account for many alarms. |  |
| 4 | A part was replaced and the alarm stopped, but the reason the part failed is unknown. What is true? | The diagnosis is incomplete and the fault may return | Without a cause, the same failure may recur. |  |
| 5 | The fluid path was opened during a repair. What is required before release? | Disinfection and a negative residual test, plus the manual’s verification tests | Opening the fluid path can introduce contamination. |  |
| 6 | A required post-repair test cannot be completed before the next shift. What is correct? | Keep it out of service and state when it will be ready | A machine is released only when every required test has passed. |  |
| 7 | Who accepts a repaired machine for clinical use? | The charge nurse, after the technician’s handover | The technician certifies technical fitness; the clinical lead accepts it for use. |  |
| 8 | Which indicator should always be zero? | Test instruments in use past their calibration due date | An overdue instrument undermines every measurement made with it. |  |
| 9 | A new machine arrives. What must happen before its first patient? | Acceptance testing, configuration for the unit, disinfection, and entry in the inventory | New machines can be damaged in transport or configured for different supplies. |  |
| 10 | After fixing a worn seal that caused an intermittent alarm, what is the most valuable next step? | Ask why it wore and check the same part on the other machines | Finding the cause and checking for the same problem elsewhere prevents repeats. |  |

# Physician on Duty in the Dialysis Unit

## Module 1: Your Role on the Dialysis Floor

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which statement best describes the physician on duty in a hemodialysis unit? | A licensed physician who gives acute care during treatment, under the supervision of the attending nephrologist and the clinical head | The physician on duty is physically present and gives acute care during the shift. The prescription and long-term plan belong to the attending nephrologist. |  |
| 2 | A nurse asks you to lower a patient’s dry weight. What is the best response? | Examine the patient, then call the attending nephrologist and pass on the nurse’s observation | Dry weight is part of the prescription. The nurse’s observation is useful evidence and should reach the nephrologist, who decides. |  |
| 3 | When should you call the attending nephrologist about a patient with chest pain during dialysis? | During the first steps of care | In an emergency, call during the first steps, not after. Someone else can dial while you examine the patient. |  |
| 4 | In SBAR, which part contains what you found on examination and what you think is happening? | Assessment | Assessment gives your findings, with numbers, and your working diagnosis. |  |
| 5 | What does the published evidence show about SBAR? | A systematic review found moderate evidence of improved patient safety, especially for telephone calls, with few high-quality studies | The evidence is moderate and comes mostly from before-and-after studies. It is strongest for telephone communication. |  |
| 6 | At which points in a session are problems most likely? | Before the start, in the last hour, and when the needles come out | Assessment before the start finds unstable patients. The last hour brings low pressure and cramps. Disconnection brings bleeding and dizziness on standing. |  |
| 7 | Your unit has no written protocol for a situation you face. What is the safest approach? | Treat it as a situation that needs a call to the attending nephrologist | Without a protocol, give emergency care as any physician would, and call. Do not assume that general ward practice fits a dialysis patient. |  |
| 8 | Which of these may a physician on duty change without calling the attending nephrologist? | None of these; each is part of the prescription | The dialysis fluid, dry weight, and treatment time are all part of the nephrologist’s prescription. |  |
| 9 | A patient wants to stop the session one hour early. What should you do? | Explain the risk, respect the decision, record the discussion, and inform the nephrologist the same day | A patient may decline treatment. Your duties are to make sure the choice is informed, to record it, and to tell the nephrologist. |  |
| 10 | You want to share an interesting electrocardiogram from a patient in a private chat group of physicians. What applies? | Health information is sensitive personal information; do not share a patient’s record or image without consent | The Data Privacy Act of 2012 protects health information wherever it is stored or sent, including personal telephones. |  |
| 11 | What is a near miss? | An error that was caught before it reached the patient | Near misses are reported because they show where the next harmful error could happen. |  |
| 12 | Before you telephone the nephrologist about an overloaded patient, which set of facts should you have ready? | Weight gain, vital signs, access, last session, and latest potassium | These five items answer most of the nephrologist’s first questions. |  |
| 13 | A nurse says, “He is not himself today,” about a patient whose vital signs are normal. What should you do? | Go and examine the patient | An experienced nurse’s impression is a clinical finding. Early confusion, infection, and low blood sugar can show before vital signs change. |  |
| 14 | Which item is a critical part of the start-of-shift round? | Checking that the defibrillator, oxygen, suction, and emergency cart are ready | You cannot look for emergency equipment during an emergency. |  |
| 15 | You cannot reach the attending nephrologist during an emergency. What do you do? | Follow the unit’s escalation list to the covering nephrologist or the clinical head while continuing emergency care | Every unit should have an escalation list. Emergency care continues while someone else makes the calls. |  |

## Module 2: Life Without Kidney Function I: Water, Salt, Potassium, and Acid

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | About how much fluid do two healthy kidneys filter each day? | About 180 liters | The kidneys filter about 180 liters a day and take back about 99%, leaving 1 to 2 liters of urine. |  |
| 2 | A patient who makes no urine is given one liter of intravenous saline. Where is that liter six hours later? | Still in the body, in the blood vessels and tissues | Without urine output, the fluid stays until dialysis removes it. |  |
| 3 | Why does telling a patient to “drink less” often fail when it is the only advice? | Salt intake drives thirst, so a patient who eats salty food will drink | Salt raises the blood sodium slightly and causes thirst. Cutting salt reduces thirst and weight gain. |  |
| 4 | Fluid gained between sessions collects mainly in which compartment? | Outside the cells, in the blood vessels and tissues | Salt and water expand the extracellular fluid. |  |
| 5 | Why does blood pressure fall when fluid is removed too fast during dialysis? | The tissues cannot refill the blood vessels as fast as the machine empties them | Dialysis takes water from the plasma. If plasma refilling from the tissues cannot keep up, blood volume and pressure fall. |  |
| 6 | A 50-kg patient needs 3,000 mL removed in 4 hours. What is the fluid removal rate? | 15 mL per kg per hour | 3,000 ÷ 4 ÷ 50 = 15 mL per kg per hour. |  |
| 7 | In the analysis of the HEMO Study, fluid removal rates above 13 mL per kg per hour were: | Associated with a higher risk of death in an observational analysis | The association was observational (adjusted hazard ratio 1.59 compared with rates up to 10). No randomized trial has tested a specific limit. |  |
| 8 | About what share of the body’s potassium is inside cells? | 98% | About 98% is inside cells. The blood test measures only the small amount outside. |  |
| 9 | How do insulin with glucose and inhaled salbutamol lower the blood potassium? | They move potassium into cells | They shift potassium into cells and buy time. In a patient without kidney function, dialysis is what removes it. |  |
| 10 | A dialysis patient with possible hyperkalemia has a normal ECG. What does this mean? | A dangerous potassium level is not ruled out | The ECG is an insensitive test. In a review of 90 patients with a potassium of 6.0 mmol/L or more, no ECG measurement reliably identified hyperkalemia. |  |
| 11 | A dialysis patient fasts for a long time before a procedure. Which change is most likely? | Potassium rises because low insulin lets potassium leave cells | With little insulin, potassium moves out of cells, and without kidneys it cannot be excreted. |  |
| 12 | How does hemodialysis correct metabolic acidosis? | Bicarbonate in the dialysis fluid moves into the patient’s blood | The dialysis fluid has a higher bicarbonate level than the blood, so bicarbonate crosses into the patient. |  |
| 13 | When is the blood bicarbonate of a dialysis patient usually lowest? | Just before a session, especially after the long gap | Acid builds up between sessions and uses up bicarbonate. |  |
| 14 | In the study of 32,065 patients dialyzing three times a week, what was found on the day after the two-day gap? | A higher death rate and more cardiovascular admissions than on other days | The death rate was 22.1 compared with 18.0 per 100 person-years on other days. |  |
| 15 | A blood sample taken immediately after dialysis shows a low-normal potassium. What should you expect a few hours later? | A rise, as potassium moves back out of cells | Potassium rebounds after dialysis. The immediate post-dialysis level underestimates the level later that day. |  |

## Module 3: Life Without Kidney Function II: Uremia, Residual Function, and Starting Dialysis

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is urea described as a marker of uremia rather than its main cause? | Urea is only mildly toxic itself and rises along with many other retained solutes | Urea is easy to measure and moves with the retained load, but it causes few of the symptoms itself. |  |
| 2 | A man has a Kt/V above target but reports new night itch, poor appetite, and morning nausea. What is the best interpretation? | A good urea-based measure does not rule out uremia, so report the symptoms | Kt/V is a surrogate that tracks urea, not middle or protein-bound solutes. |  |
| 3 | Why does conventional hemodialysis remove indoxyl sulfate poorly? | Most of it is bound to albumin, and only the small free fraction crosses the membrane | The healthy tubule secretes protein-bound solutes. Dialysis cannot. |  |
| 4 | What did the European Uremic Toxin Work Group find about the 90 retained solutes it cataloged? | Most were small, but many were middle molecules or protein-bound | Of 90 solutes, 68 were under 500 daltons, 22 were middle molecules, and 25 were protein-bound. |  |
| 5 | What did the IDEAL trial show? | A planned early start did not improve survival compared with waiting under close follow-up | Deaths were 37.6% with early start and 36.6% with late start. |  |
| 6 | Why must the IDEAL result be read with care? | Most patients in the late-start group began dialysis above the target GFR because symptoms developed | It tested waiting with close follow-up, not waiting in a symptomatic patient. |  |
| 7 | A referred woman with an estimated GFR of 9 has a pericardial rub, vomiting, and asterixis. What is the best next step? | Call the attending nephrologist now about an urgent start | A pericardial rub and asterixis are urgent uremic indications whatever the GFR, and the vomiting adds to them. |  |
| 8 | How does KDIGO 2024 grade its practice points on when to start dialysis? | They are practice points, which KDIGO does not grade | Practice points are ungraded. Do not borrow another system’s grades. |  |
| 9 | A resident calculates an estimated GFR from the creatinine of a patient on dialysis for a year. What is wrong? | Equations that estimate GFR from creatinine are not valid once dialysis has started | Dialysis removes creatinine, so the blood level no longer reflects kidney function. |  |
| 10 | What did the CHOICE cohort find about urine output at one year on hemodialysis? | It was associated with lower all-cause mortality, in an observational study | The hazard ratio was 0.70 in an observational cohort, so confounding is possible. |  |
| 11 | A timed collection gives a much lower clearance than before. The patient forgot to collect two voids. What is the best interpretation? | The result underestimates her clearance. Record the missed voids and tell the nephrologist | An incomplete collection lowers the calculated clearance. Record it honestly. |  |
| 12 | A man who passes a liter of urine a day is scheduled for a non-urgent contrast CT scan. What should you do? | Ask whether the attending nephrologist wants an alternative or a plan before the scan | Residual function is worth protecting, and the decision belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 13 | What did Moist and colleagues find about ACE inhibitor use and loss of residual function? | Use was associated with less loss, which the authors treated as a hypothesis | The adjusted odds ratio was 0.68 in an observational cohort, which does not prove protection. |  |
| 14 | A woman on twice-weekly dialysis has rising weight gains and says her urine has fallen. What do you do? | Report the trend to the attending nephrologist, who decides whether to step up the schedule | Rising gains and falling urine are triggers to step up. The schedule and the fluid goal belong to the nephrologist. |  |
| 15 | What have the completed randomized trials of incremental dialysis shown so far? | Incremental starts are feasible in selected patients, and results on protecting residual function disagree | The British and Spanish trials found no protection. Two smaller trials suggested benefit. TwoPlus is in progress. |  |

## Module 4: Life Without Kidney Function III: Heart, Blood, Bone, and Defenses

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does the left ventricle thicken in kidney failure? | Stiff arteries, retained fluid, anemia, and access flow raise its workload | Each load raises wall stress, and the ventricle thickens in response. |  |
| 2 | 4D and AURORA lowered LDL cholesterol by more than 40% in hemodialysis patients. What happened to the main cardiovascular outcome? | It did not fall significantly | The surrogate moved, but events did not. This heart disease is not only atherosclerosis. |  |
| 3 | A man who has taken a statin for years starts hemodialysis. What does the KDIGO lipid guideline suggest? | Continue the statin (2C) | KDIGO suggests not starting statins on dialysis (2A) but continuing them (2C). The nephrologist decides. |  |
| 4 | In stable hemodialysis patients tested with an hs-cTnT assay, how many were above the general-population 99th percentile? | About 99% | Nearly all were, so one value means little. A troponin I assay was raised in 13%. |  |
| 5 | A woman on dialysis has epigastric discomfort. Her troponin is 85 ng/L; her last stable value, same assay, was 80. A repeat two hours later is 84 and her ECG is unchanged. What does this suggest? | Chronic injury without a rise; keep assessing and report | Acute infarction needs a rise or fall with ischemia. Her symptom still needs a report. |  |
| 6 | Why are cardiac markers drawn before the session when possible? | Dialysis lowers some values, so a post-dialysis sample is not comparable | In one study, hs-cTnT fell from 69 to 61 ng/L after a session. |  |
| 7 | Why are BNP and NT-proBNP hard to interpret on dialysis? | They are nearly always raised, and cut-offs are inconsistent | Compare with the patient’s usual level and the clinical picture. |  |
| 8 | A man’s hemoglobin fell from 10.8 to 9.2 g/dL in a month. He feels well. What comes first? | A search for blood loss | Anemia is expected; a new fall is not. Look for bleeding and report. |  |
