| 9 | Why do dialysis patients bleed easily with a normal platelet count? | Uremia impairs platelet adhesion and aggregation, and anemia keeps platelets from the vessel wall | The defect is in function, not number. Heparin adds to it. |  |
| 10 | A patient’s calcium is 8.0 mg/dL with an albumin of 2.8 g/dL, so the corrected calcium is 9.2. She has tingling around her mouth. What should you do? | Request an ionized calcium per protocol, check the ECG, and call | Correction does not track ionized calcium on dialysis, and she has symptoms. |  |
| 11 | A woman with a tunneled catheter is quiet and slightly confused. Her temperature is 36.2 °C and her pressure is lower than usual. What is the best interpretation? | Possible sepsis despite a normal temperature | Uremia blunts fever. Examine the catheter site and call. |  |
| 12 | A man with diabetes on dialysis has an HbA1c of 6.0%, but his home glucose is mostly above 200 mg/dL. What best explains this? | Shorter red cell survival and ESA treatment lower HbA1c | HbA1c underestimates glycemia on hemodialysis. Report it; do not change treatment at the chair. |  |
| 13 | A dialysis patient has a raised D-dimer. What does it tell you? | Little, because nearly everyone with reduced kidney function has a raised value | D-dimer keeps its sensitivity but loses specificity. A normal value still helps. |  |
| 14 | Which drug is least likely to be removed by dialysis? | A highly protein-bound drug with a large volume of distribution | Only free drug in the plasma reaches the membrane. |  |
| 15 | A nurse asks you to prescribe an antibiotic for a dialysis patient’s skin infection. What comes first? | Check a renal dosing reference using the dialysis entry, ask about timing, and inform the nephrologist | Dosing depends on renal clearance and dialytic removal. eGFR is not valid on dialysis. |  |

## Module 5: Inside the Machine: How Blood Is Cleaned

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In a patient with a forearm fistula, what does the term “arterial line” mean? | The tubing that carries blood from the patient toward the dialyzer | The names describe direction in the circuit. Both needles are in the same vessel. |  |
| 2 | A connection comes loose between the arterial needle and the blood pump. What is the main danger? | Air is drawn into the circuit | The pump pulls on this segment, so air is sucked in. |  |
| 3 | Which process removes most of the urea and potassium in standard hemodialysis? | Diffusion down a concentration difference | Small molecules cross quickly by diffusion. Convection adds little in standard hemodialysis. |  |
| 4 | Why do blood and dialysis fluid run in opposite directions through the dialyzer? | To keep a concentration difference along the whole length of the fibers | Blood that is nearly clean meets the freshest fluid, so diffusion continues to the end. |  |
| 5 | A colleague says that a high-flux dialyzer “gives a better urea clearance.” What is the most accurate reply? | Flux describes how easily water and larger molecules pass, not urea removal | Flux depends on pore size. Urea removal depends mostly on surface area and flows. |  |
| 6 | What did the HEMO Study find when it compared high-flux with low-flux dialyzers in 1,846 patients? | No significant difference in death from any cause | The relative risk was 0.92, with a confidence interval that includes no effect. |  |
| 7 | The machine goes into bypass after a conductivity alarm. What is happening? | Dialysis fluid is sent to the drain and does not reach the dialyzer | Bypass keeps fluid of the wrong composition away from the blood. No dialysis takes place meanwhile. |  |
| 8 | Why can a correct conductivity reading not confirm that the potassium level of the dialysis fluid is right? | Conductivity reflects total salt content, most of which is sodium | The sensor cannot tell one salt from another, so staff check the concentrate label. |  |
| 9 | Mrs. Aquino is dialyzing through a new fistula. The high venous pressure alarm has sounded twice, and her arm hurts near the venous needle. What do you do first? | Uncover the arm and examine the venous needle site with the pump stopped | This suggests infiltration. Look at the arm before anything on the machine is changed. |  |
| 10 | Mr. Ocampo is asleep with his fistula arm under a blanket. The machine is quiet. Why is this unsafe? | A dislodged venous needle may not lower the venous pressure enough to trigger an alarm | Most of the venous pressure comes from the needle itself. A visible access is the safeguard. |  |
| 11 | The arterial pressure becomes steadily more negative while the displayed blood flow is unchanged. What is the best interpretation? | The access is not supplying blood easily, and the true blood flow may be lower than displayed | With strong suction the pump tubing partly collapses and delivers less than the screen shows. |  |
| 12 | The transmembrane pressure has climbed for an hour, and dark streaks are visible in the dialyzer. What is the most likely cause? | Clotting of the fibers | Clotted fibers reduce the working membrane, so more pressure is needed to remove the same water. |  |
| 13 | Twenty minutes after a temperature alarm was overridden, Mr. Salonga reports back pain and breathlessness. The blood in his venous line looks translucent, like red wine. What applies to the blood in the circuit? | It is not returned, because burst red cells release potassium | These signs suggest hemolysis. Dialysis is stopped, the blood is not returned, and the attending nephrologist is called at once. |  |
| 14 | A blood-leak alarm sounds. No blood is visible in the fluid tubing, and a technician offers to override the alarm. What is the best response? | Do not override; assess the patient, have the used fluid tested per protocol, and call the attending nephrologist | A small leak may be invisible. Torn fibers mean blood loss and contact with non-sterile fluid. |  |
| 15 | Which statement about the randomized evidence for hemodiafiltration is accurate? | One trial found fewer deaths with high-dose hemodiafiltration than with high-flux hemodialysis | The hazard ratio for death was 0.77 in selected patients able to reach a high convection volume. |  |

## Module 6: Water and Dialysate: The Hidden Half of the Treatment

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why can a contaminant that is harmless in drinking water harm a dialysis patient? | Dialysis fluid bypasses the gut and liver, the volume is large, and the kidneys cannot excrete what crosses | Three gates are lost, and the exposure runs to hundreds of liters a week. |  |
| 2 | A dialysis fluid flow of 500 mL per minute runs for 240 minutes, three times a week. About how much fluid meets the blood each week? | 360 L | 500 × 240 ÷ 1,000 = 120 L per session, and 120 × 3 = 360 L per week. |  |
| 3 | Which step of water treatment removes chloramine? | Activated carbon | Chloramine passes through reverse osmosis and deionization. Carbon removes it, which is why the chlorine test is taken after the first carbon bed. |  |
| 4 | Why are two carbon beds placed in series? | So that breakthrough after the first bed is detected before the second fails | A test between the beds catches exhaustion of the first while the second still protects. |  |
| 5 | Over two afternoon shifts, four patients in one room develop new anemia, and two have back pain during dialysis. The technician changed a carbon tank last week. Which contaminant is the leading suspect? | Chloramine | Hemolysis after a carbon change points to chloramine. A faulty tubing lot is the main mimic. |  |
| 6 | Several patients develop severe itching and nausea during dialysis, and one has ventricular fibrillation. All were treated in one room that uses a temporary deionizer. What is the likely cause? | Fluoride released from the deionizer | This matches the published outbreak in which a temporary deionizer released fluoride: 12 of 15 patients in one room became ill. |  |
| 7 | What did the Caruaru outbreak in Brazil show? | Untreated surface water carried cyanobacterial toxins that caused liver failure and death | Of 130 patients, 101 met the case definition of acute liver failure and 50 died. The water had not been treated. |  |
| 8 | What does the Limulus amebocyte lysate (LAL) test miss, according to Glorieux and colleagues? | Some smaller bacterial fragments that still provoke inflammation in a cell assay | About 1 in 10 pure or ultrapure samples still provoked a cytokine response. The clinical meaning is unknown. |  |
| 9 | What does conductivity in dialysis fluid mainly reflect? | Total dissolved salt, mostly sodium | Conductivity rises with charged particles. It cannot detect bacteria, endotoxin, or uncharged toxins. |  |
| 10 | Twenty minutes into the session, a patient complains of intense thirst, headache, and nausea, and the blood pressure has risen. The bicarbonate jug was changed before the start. What should you suspect first? | Dialysis fluid that is too concentrated, from a proportioning or concentrate error | High sodium pulls water out of cells and raises thirst and pressure. Check the concentrate, the conductivity, and the machine. |  |
| 11 | What did the factorial randomized trial by Asci and colleagues show about ultrapure dialysis fluid? | It did not reduce cardiovascular events; a benefit after more than 3 years on dialysis was post hoc | The hazard ratio was 0.90 with a confidence interval from 0.61 to 1.32. The subgroup finding needs confirmation. |  |
| 12 | The meta-analysis by Susantitaphong and colleagues found that ultrapure fluid raised hemoglobin and albumin and lowered C-reactive protein. How should you describe these findings? | Changes in surrogate markers, mostly from nonrandomized studies, without proof of benefit on hard outcomes | These are surrogates. The authors called for a large trial with hard outcomes. |  |
| 13 | At 3 p.m., two patients in different rooms on the same water loop develop chills and fever within an hour of starting. Neither has a catheter. What is the best next step? | Declare a possible cluster, follow the protocol, and call the Medical Director and the attending nephrologists | The same new symptom on one loop is a unit problem until proven otherwise. Early chills suggest endotoxin. |  |
| 14 | In a cluster, 6 of 10 patients on machines that received a new concentrate batch are ill, and 0 of 9 on the old batch are. What does this comparison tell you? | The new batch is the leading suspect, to be confirmed by testing | An attack rate of 60% against 0% points to the batch. It guides testing; it does not prove the cause. |  |
| 15 | At the start of your shift, the log shows no total chlorine result for today, and the first patients are about to start. What do you do? | Ask the technician to test now and follow the unit’s policy before patients start, and inform the charge nurse | The pre-shift chlorine test protects red cells. The policy states what happens when it is missing. |  |

## Module 7: Reading the Dialysis Order and Judging the Dose

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which change in the order raises small-solute removal without a ceiling set by the dialyzer? | A longer session time | Flow and dialyzer size have diminishing returns. Every minute adds dose. |  |
| 2 | A man on thrice-weekly dialysis has a pre-dialysis potassium of 3.3 mmol/L after three days of vomiting. His order lists a very low potassium bath. What should you do? | Hold the start and call the attending nephrologist | A steep gradient would lower his potassium fast. The bath belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 3 | What did Karnik and colleagues find about potassium baths in dialysis-unit cardiac arrests? | Patients who arrested were nearly twice as likely to have been on a 0 or 1 mEq/L bath | 17.1% of arrest patients were on a 0 or 1 mEq/L bath, against 8.8% of the dialysis population. The study was observational. |  |
| 4 | Why does digoxin make a low-potassium bath more dangerous? | Low potassium increases digoxin’s effect on the heart | Hypokalemia increases digoxin toxicity. This is established pharmacology. |  |
| 5 | What did the MyTEMP trial show about cooler dialysis fluid? | As a policy for all patients, it did not reduce cardiovascular death or hospitalization | In 84 centers and 15,413 patients, the adjusted hazard ratio was 1.00. |  |
| 6 | What does Kt/V measure? | Removal of small solutes such as urea, relative to body water | Kt/V is a surrogate for small-solute removal. It does not measure middle molecules, fluid, or nutrition. |  |
| 7 | In the HEMO Study, what happened when the dose was raised above the standard? | Mortality did not change | A single-pool Kt/V of 1.71 against 1.32 gave a relative risk of death of 0.96, with a confidence interval crossing 1. |  |
| 8 | Pre-dialysis BUN is 80 mg/dL and post-dialysis BUN is 24 mg/dL. What is the urea reduction ratio? | 70% | (80 − 24) ÷ 80 = 0.70, or 70%. |  |
| 9 | A nurse draws the post-dialysis sample from the arterial line with the pump at full speed through a fistula with recirculation. How does this affect the result? | The dose looks higher than it was | Cleaned blood lowers the post BUN, so the dose looks better. |  |
| 10 | Why is equilibrated Kt/V lower than single-pool Kt/V? | Urea moves back into the blood from tissues after the session | Urea rebound from slower compartments raises blood urea after the session. Equilibrated Kt/V accounts for it. |  |
| 11 | Mr. Ramos’s spKt/V has fallen from 1.45 to 1.15. His log shows full time and no alarms. On the adequacy day, a new nurse drew the post sample 45 minutes after the session ended. What is the most likely explanation? | A sample drawn after rebound, making the dose look lower | A late draw catches rebound, so the dose looks low. Report it and ask whether to repeat. |  |
| 12 | A patient lost 40 of 240 prescribed minutes in each session this month. Her usual spKt/V is 1.41. Using the simplified proportional estimate, what is her approximate spKt/V? | 1.18 | 1.41 × 200 ÷ 240 ≈ 1.18. The real relation is not linear, so a measured value is still needed. |  |
| 13 | What did the FHN Daily Trial show for six-times-weekly in-center dialysis? | Better composite outcomes of death or left ventricular mass, and death or physical health, with more access interventions | Hazard ratios were 0.61 and 0.70 for the two composites. Left ventricular mass is a surrogate. |  |
| 14 | In CONVINCE, death occurred in 17.3% with hemodiafiltration and 21.9% with hemodialysis over about 2.5 years. What is the approximate number needed to treat to prevent one death? | 22 | The absolute difference is 4.6 percentage points, and 1 ÷ 0.046 ≈ 22. This number was calculated from published proportions, not reported by the trial. |  |
| 15 | Ms. Lim is on online hemodiafiltration. Her fistula has been difficult and her blood flow is well below the order. The machine shows a low convection volume each session. What should you do? | Record the delivered blood flow and volume, examine the access, and report to the attending nephrologist | Convection volume depends on blood flow, and the access may be failing. |  |

## Module 8: Dry Weight: Finding the Right Amount of Fluid

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which statement best describes dry weight? | A clinical estimate of the lowest tolerated post-dialysis weight, which drifts as the patient changes | No device measures dry weight directly. It is a clinical estimate, and it must be revised when the patient gains or loses flesh. |  |
| 2 | A consensus group described pre-dialysis volume overload as the sum of two parts. What are they? | The weight gain since the last session and the overload left after the last session | The scale sees the gain. Overload left behind because the target is too high hides under the target. |  |
| 3 | In the LUST auscultation substudy, how well did crackles reflect lung water on ultrasound? | Poorly, explaining about 12% of the variation | Crackles shared about 12% of the variance with B-lines and edema about 4%. Their absence does not exclude lung water. |  |
| 4 | A man arrives 3.2 kilograms above target for a 4-hour session. His unit adds 0.2 liters for the rinse-back and normalizes to a target weight of 64 kilograms. What is his UF rate? | 13.3 mL/kg/h | Goal 3,400 mL. 3,400 ÷ (4 × 64) = 3,400 ÷ 256 = 13.3 mL/kg/h. The numbers are illustrative. |  |
| 5 | What is the strongest evidence currently available that UF rates above 13 mL/kg/h are harmful? | Large observational cohorts showing an association with mortality | The HEMO secondary analysis and a cohort of 118,394 patients found associations. No randomized trial has tested a ceiling. |  |
| 6 | According to the Mermelstein cohort, how does a fixed ceiling in mL/kg/h behave across body sizes? | It is relatively stricter for small patients and more lenient for heavy ones | Risk levels followed 3W plus a constant, so the same risk sits at a higher mL/kg/h in a small patient and a lower one in a heavy patient. A fixed ceiling restricts small patients more and heavy patients less. |  |
| 7 | Why does the blood pressure tend to fall late in a session in a patient near target? | Refilling slows as the tissue spaces empty, while removal continues | Refilling depends on an expanded tissue space. Near true dry weight it slows, and removal outruns it. |  |
| 8 | A nurse proposes removing the remaining 1.5 liters in the last hour because the patient was dizzy earlier. What is the problem with this plan? | It places the fastest removal where refilling is slowest | The late session is when refilling is slowest. Fast removal there invites a sharp drop. Call the attending about the remaining goal. |  |
| 9 | What did the LUST randomized trial show? | Ultrasound guidance relieved congestion more often but did not change the composite of death, myocardial infarction, and heart failure | Congestion was relieved in 78% versus 56%, but the primary outcome did not differ (hazard ratio 0.88). Post hoc benefits are hypotheses. |  |
| 10 | What was the main finding of the BISTRO trial? | Bioimpedance guidance did not change time to anuria compared with a standardized clinical protocol | The hazard ratio was 0.751 with a confidence interval crossing 1. The authors credit the standardized clinical protocol. |  |
| 11 | In the DRIP trial, probing the dry weight down by about 1 kilogram did what? | Lowered ambulatory systolic pressure by about 7 mm Hg, with more symptoms of low pressure during dialysis | DRIP showed a blood pressure benefit, a surrogate, over 8 weeks. It did not test hard outcomes. |  |
| 12 | A woman has had cramps in the last hour of most sessions for three weeks. She is dizzy at home after dialysis, and her urine output has fallen. She has been eating well since a hospital stay. What is the most likely issue? | Her target weight is now too low because she has gained flesh | Weight gained as flesh makes the old target too low. Report the pattern; the attending decides. |  |
| 13 | A man’s post-dialysis weights reach target every session, but his edema persists and his pre-dialysis pressure is climbing. He lost his appetite after pneumonia a month ago. What should you do? | Report the pattern and the four-session trend to the attending nephrologist and suggest a target review | Lost flesh may have been replaced by water. Proposing a review is your role; writing a new target is not. |  |
| 14 | A relative asks you to lower her father’s target weight because his ankles are swollen. What do you do? | Assess him, document the findings, and call the attending nephrologist with the request | Any proposed change in target weight, from any source, goes to the attending. Edema has causes other than overload. |  |
| 15 | A patient arrives 4.5 kilograms over target for a 3-hour session, and the rate would exceed your unit’s ceiling. What is the best action? | Call the attending nephrologist before starting, with the weights, goal, hours, rate, and minimum time | A goal that does not fit the ceiling is a prescription question. The attending chooses among the options. |  |

## Module 9: The Lifeline I: Fistulas and Grafts

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the main difference between a fistula and a graft? | A fistula uses the patient’s own vein, and a graft uses a synthetic tube | In a fistula the patient’s own vein is needled. In a graft a synthetic tube connects artery and vein and is needled. |  |
| 2 | Why is the thrill of a healthy access continuous? | The vein offers little resistance, so blood flows in systole and in diastole | The access is a low-resistance path, so flow and vibration continue through diastole. |  |
| 3 | You feel a hard, bounding pulse where a soft thrill used to be. What is the most likely cause? | Outflow narrowing beyond that point | Blood enters at arterial pressure and cannot leave easily, so pressure rises before the narrowing. |  |
| 4 | You raise the arm of a patient with a forearm fistula. The lower half stays full and the upper half flattens. What does this suggest? | An outflow narrowing between the two halves | A normal fistula flattens along its length. With an outflow narrowing, only the part beyond it drains. |  |
| 5 | In the pulse augmentation test, the pulse near the join barely strengthens. What does this suggest? | Inflow narrowing | With normal inflow the pulse strengthens clearly against the occluding finger. |  |
| 6 | Mr. Ramos has a looped forearm graft. Before his session the nurse finds no thrill and no bruit. He feels well. What do you do first? | Confirm the findings, hold needling, and call the attending nephrologist at once | No thrill and no bruit mean thrombosis. A trial needle adds harm, and earlier treatment is associated with better results. |  |
| 7 | Which statement about graft infection does the KDOQI guideline support? | It seldom resolves with antibiotics alone | A graft is foreign material, and bacteria that reach it are hard to clear. |  |
| 8 | Mrs. Aquino has a bulge on her fistula. Today the skin over it is shiny, cannot be pinched up, and has a small scab. The thrill is normal. What is the best action? | Hold needling and call the attending nephrologist today | Thin shiny skin and a scab mean a wall at risk of rupture. KDOQI advises emergency surgical assessment for erosion. |  |
| 9 | Why does repeated needling in one small area raise concern? | It weakens the wall and skin, and was associated with more access failure in a large cohort | In an observational study of 7,058 patients, area puncture was associated with a higher risk of access failure than rope-ladder needling. |  |
| 10 | Mr. Santiago has diabetes and a new upper-arm fistula. The hand on that side is cold, numb, and pale. The thrill is strong. What is the most likely problem? | Steal | Blood is taking the shortcut through the access, and diseased arteries cannot compensate. |  |
| 11 | A needle site is still bleeding after the session. Which technique is correct? | Two fingertips over the skin hole and the vessel hole, firm enough to stop bleeding while a thrill remains | Pressure belongs on the needle site. It should stop the bleeding without stopping the flow, and it should not be lifted early. |  |
| 12 | A scab over an aneurysm gives way during dialysis, and blood pours from the arm. Which response is correct? | Press hard on the bleeding point, call for help, and have the blood pump stopped | Direct pressure comes first and is not released. Blood in the circuit is never a reason to delay. |  |
| 13 | In the review of 88 fatal access bleeds, where did most deaths with a known location occur? | At home or in a nursing home | The figure was 78%. Patients and families need to know the warning signs and how to press. |  |
| 14 | A ward nurse is about to take a blood pressure on a fistula arm. What is the best explanation to give? | The cuff can stop the flow, and stopped flow can clot | The rule rests on physiology and expert opinion, and it costs nothing to follow. |  |
| 15 | What does KDOQI say about a narrowing that causes no clinical indicators? | It does not recommend opening it | KDOQI relies on regular examination and the treatment record to identify the narrowings that need action. |  |

## Module 10: The Lifeline II: Catheters

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does the cuff on a tunneled catheter do? | Anchors the catheter and helps block bacteria once tissue grows into it | The cuff sits inside the tunnel. Ingrown tissue holds the catheter and forms a barrier between skin and vein. |  |
| 2 | The chart shows that a non-tunneled neck catheter was inserted five weeks ago. It flows well. What should you do? | Report it to the attending nephrologist | Non-tunneled catheters are intended for short use because infection risk rises with time. The plan belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 3 | Why is a catheter examined before every connection? | An infection found before connection is still local | Once the pump starts, organisms at the exit site or in the lumen can be flushed into the blood. |  |
| 4 | Before connection, a ring of white fabric is visible at the exit site of a tunneled catheter. What comes next? | Secure the catheter, do not use it, and call the attending nephrologist | A visible cuff means the catheter has moved outward. The exposed part is not sterile and the tip may be out of position. |  |
| 5 | Which step, done before a cap is removed, protects most directly against air entry? | Confirming that the clamp is closed | Central venous pressure can fall below room pressure during a breath in. Scrubbing and masks protect against bacteria. |  |
| 6 | A ward nurse asks to run an overnight antibiotic through a patient’s dialysis catheter because the veins are difficult. What is the best answer? | Decline routine use and refer the request to the attending nephrologist | Each extra opening of the hub adds risk of infection and air entry. |  |
| 7 | What did the cluster-randomized study of catheter care compare? | Chlorhexidine exit-site care plus hub scrubbing, against usual care | Intervention facilities had fewer positive blood cultures per 1,000 catheter-days. |  |
| 8 | Why can a lumen accept a saline flush and still fail to give blood back? | Suction pulls the vein wall, a clot, or a fibrin sheath over the opening | Pushing lifts the obstruction away and pulling draws it back, like a flap. |  |
| 9 | A tunneled catheter placed three days ago flows poorly from the first session. Which cause is most likely? | A kink, a tight stitch, or a poorly placed tip | Early poor flow is usually mechanical. Clot and fibrin sheath are typical after weeks of good function. |  |
| 10 | A lumen resists a saline flush. Why should nobody push harder with a small syringe? | High pressure can damage the catheter and force material into the bloodstream | For the same push, a small syringe generates a higher pressure. Stop and call the attending nephrologist. |  |
| 11 | In the study of 102 patients with tunneled catheters, what happened when the blood lines were reversed? | Significant recirculation became common, with no gain in clearance | Recirculation above 10% was found in 86% with lines reversed and 3% in the usual position. |  |
| 12 | Forty minutes into dialysis, a 67-year-old man with a tunneled catheter has shaking chills. His exit site is clean. How do you interpret this? | A catheter-related bloodstream infection until proven otherwise | Bacteria inside the lumen are washed into the blood when the pump runs. The exit site may look normal. |  |
| 13 | For the same patient, what should happen before the first antibiotic? | Blood cultures per protocol, and a call to the attending nephrologist | Guidelines give priority to cultures before antibiotics. Forearm veins are saved for a future fistula. |  |
| 14 | At disconnection a hub is found open and unclamped. The seated patient coughs, becomes breathless, and her blood pressure falls. What do you do first? | Clamp the catheter, stop the pump, do not return the blood, give oxygen, and call for help | This is suspected air entry. Returning blood or flushing could push more air into the patient. |  |
| 15 | A patient eight months into catheter dialysis says a fistula operation is unnecessary because the catheter works. What is the most useful response? | Explain that the risks grow with time, and tell the attending nephrologist that the plan has stalled | Catheter use is associated with more infection and death than fistula use. The access plan belongs to the nephrologist. |  |

## Module 11: Safe to Start, Safe to Go Home

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is “do not start” never a complete decision for a dialysis patient? | Fluid, potassium, and acid keep building, so a plan for where and when to dialyze is still needed | Not dialyzing carries its own risk. |  |
| 2 | Mr. Ramos has a catheter. His blood pressure is far below that of his last six sessions, and he has felt cold and weak since yesterday. He has no fever. What fits best? | Delay, examine him, follow protocol, and call the attending nephrologist before connection | Fever is often absent in catheter bloodstream infection. |  |
| 3 | What is the purpose of the first minute of the three-minute sequence? | To observe how the patient walks, breathes, and speaks, and to ask how they have been | Walking, breathing, alertness, and speech give the fastest information. |  |
| 4 | A patient is 4 kilograms above dry weight and sleeps sitting up. Her chest is clear and her ankles are not swollen. What does the evidence suggest? | A normal examination does not exclude overload | In 1,106 paired examinations, crackles and edema agreed poorly with lung congestion on ultrasound. |  |
| 5 | Mrs. Bautista arrives for dialysis. Her speech has been slurred since breakfast and her right hand is clumsy. Vital signs are at her usual values. What is the best decision? | Do not connect, follow the emergency protocol, call the attending nephrologist, and prepare to transfer | A new neurologic deficit needs urgent imaging, and heparin could worsen a brain bleed. |  |
| 6 | Why is the outpatient dialysis chair the wrong place to begin for a patient with active bleeding? | Heparin can worsen bleeding, and the unit cannot transfuse rapidly or treat the source | The circuit usually needs a blood thinner. |  |
| 7 | In the study of suspected infection in catheter-dependent patients, what was found about fever? | It was a presenting symptom in fewer than half of catheter-related bloodstream infections | Fever was a presenting symptom in 47%, so its absence does not exclude infection. |  |
| 8 | Which question bears most directly on the safety of heparin today? | Any bleeding, black stools, falls, or a blow to the head | Heparin can worsen any bleeding, including a slow bleed inside the skull after a fall. Report these answers before connection. The nephrologist decides the heparin plan. |  |
| 9 | What do the large studies of missed dialysis sessions show? | Missed sessions are associated with more deaths and admissions, with possible confounding | The studies were observational, and most patients were on three sessions a week. |  |
| 10 | Mr. Santiago missed his last session and arrives 6 kilograms above dry weight, comfortable at rest. The nurse suggests removing all of it today. What is the best response? | Assess him, test as protocol allows, and call the attending nephrologist, who sets the fluid goal and time | Fast fluid removal is associated with harm. Dividing the work across sessions is a prescription decision. |  |
| 11 | A patient admits he skipped a session because he had no fare. Which response serves him best? | Thank him, explain the risk, and share the reason with the team so help can be arranged | Patients who feel blamed hide missed sessions. |  |
| 12 | Under the CDC recommendations, how is a patient positive for hepatitis B surface antigen dialyzed? | In a separate room on a dedicated machine, by staff not caring for susceptible patients at the same time | Hepatitis B survives on surfaces for at least seven days. |  |
| 13 | A patient transfers to your unit this morning with no hepatitis B result in the chart. He says, “I am negative.” What should you do? | Treat the status as unknown, tell the charge nurse, call the attending nephrologist, and follow unit policy | Unknown is not the same as negative. |  |
| 14 | After dialysis, Mrs. Cruz has her usual seated blood pressure. On standing she is dizzy and her pressure falls. What is the best next step? | Lay her down, follow the hypotension protocol, call the attending nephrologist, and keep her until stable | The fall on standing shows she cannot yet compensate for the fluid removed, so she is not safe to walk. Dry weight review belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 15 | A patient’s needle sites have bled longer than usual at three sessions in a row. Why should the nephrologist be told? | A pattern of prolonged bleeding is one clinical sign that the access may be narrowing | The KDOQI guideline lists it among the clinical signs of a failing access. Other causes exist. |  |

## Module 12: First Sessions and Unusual Sessions

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is a first-ever hemodialysis session usually shorter and less efficient than a maintenance session? | A fast fall in a very high urea can swell the brain | The brain has adapted to a high urea. Lowering it fast creates an osmotic gradient into the brain. |  |
| 2 | In the DOPPS analysis of 86,886 patients, what was the pattern of mortality after starting hemodialysis? | Mortality was highest in the first 120 days | Early mortality exceeded intermediate mortality in every country studied. The study was observational. |  |
| 3 | Why does urea pull little water across cell membranes in a steady state, but more during fast dialysis? | Urea crosses membranes, so it is equal on both sides until its plasma level falls faster than it can leave the cells | Urea is an ineffective osmole at equilibrium. It becomes effective when plasma urea falls faster than brain urea. |  |
| 4 | Using calculated osmolality = 2 × sodium + glucose ÷ 18 + BUN ÷ 2.8, what does a BUN of 140 mg/dL contribute? | 50 mOsm/kg | 140 ÷ 2.8 = 50. The urea share is large when the BUN is high. |  |
| 5 | Which statement about the mechanism of dialysis disequilibrium is best supported? | The reverse urea hypothesis remains viable, and other mechanisms may contribute | A critical review found the reverse urea hypothesis viable. Idiogenic osmoles and brain acidosis are also proposed. |  |
| 6 | A 70-year-old man is 90 minutes into his first session. He has a new headache, has vomited once, and is restless. His pressure is unchanged. What do you do first? | Have the treatment slowed or stopped per protocol, check glucose and a neurologic examination, and call the attending nephrologist | These are early signs of disequilibrium. Treatment after it is established is rarely successful, so you act early. |  |
| 7 | A maintenance patient returns after missing four sessions. Her urea is very high and she is drowsy. The nurse plans to run her usual order. What is the best response? | Call the attending nephrologist before connection, because missed sessions raise her disequilibrium risk | A patient whose urea has risen for weeks resembles a new patient. Her usual order may be too efficient today. |  |
| 8 | How strong is the evidence that a particular first-session prescription prevents disequilibrium? | Mechanism, reviews, and case series only | The syndrome is rare, so randomized trials are almost impossible. Prevention rests on physiology and expert practice. |  |
| 9 | A man with acute kidney injury after leptospirosis is in week 3 of outpatient dialysis. His urine output has risen from 200 to 900 mL per day. What should you do? | Record the trend and report it to the attending nephrologist the same day | Rising urine output may signal recovery. The decision to test or stop belongs to the attending nephrologist. |  |
| 10 | In the cohort by Jordan and colleagues, what share of patients discharged on outpatient dialysis after AKI recovered kidney function? | About 41% | 45 of 111 recovered, most within 30 days of discharge. The study was single-center and selected. |  |
| 11 | Why is low blood pressure during dialysis a particular concern in a patient with AKI? | It may reduce kidney perfusion and set back recovery | An injured kidney depends on blood flow to heal. Hypotension is a second injury. |  |
| 12 | A visiting patient arrives with an order from her home unit and hepatitis results dated seven months ago. Your unit accepts results only within a shorter interval. What is the correct plan? | Ask the attending nephrologist for a local order, and place her by the unit protocol for unknown status | The visiting order is information. Out-of-date serology is managed as unknown status. |  |
| 13 | Which document from a transferred patient tells you where and in which direction to needle? | The access type and cannulation map | The cannulation map and recent access problems guide the first needling (Modules 9 and 10). |  |
| 14 | A patient with heart failure and a high potassium is ordered isolated ultrafiltration first. What is true? | Isolated ultrafiltration removes water and salt but clears little potassium, so diffusive dialysis is still needed | Without dialysis fluid flow there is no diffusion. Potassium leaves only in the small volume removed. |  |
| 15 | Why is blood pressure often better tolerated during isolated ultrafiltration than during diffusive dialysis? | Plasma osmolality changes little, so refilling and vessel tone are better preserved | Diffusive dialysis lowers plasma osmolality as urea leaves, which slows refilling. Isolated ultrafiltration does not. |  |

## Module 13: When the Pressure Falls

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why do most patients tolerate the removal of several liters in one session? | Refilling, vessel tightening, and the heart’s response act together | The machine removes water only from the blood. The three defenses hold the pressure while it does. |  |
| 2 | Why does tightening of the veins help to maintain pressure during fluid removal? | Veins hold most of the blood volume, so tightening returns blood to the heart | When veins tighten, the heart stays filled. When they relax, blood pools and output falls. |  |
| 3 | A woman with long-standing diabetes has a pressure of 80/46 mm Hg in hour three. Her pulse is 64 per minute and regular. What does the pulse tell you? | Her heart rate response is blunted, so the pulse does not make the pressure safe | Autonomic neuropathy and reflex loss of sympathetic tone can both keep the pulse from rising. |  |
| 4 | In the analysis by Flythe and colleagues, which definition was most strongly associated with death? | A lowest systolic pressure below 90 mm Hg | Nadir-based definitions tracked mortality. Definitions built on symptoms or interventions did not. |  |
| 5 | Published estimates of how often intradialytic hypotension occurs vary widely. What is the main reason? | Studies use different definitions | A fall in pressure alone is very common. A fall with symptoms and an intervention is much less common. |  |
| 6 | A patient’s pressure has fallen in hour three and the nurse has not yet acted. The patient responds and is breathing. What comes first? | Lay the patient back with the legs raised and stop fluid removal | These two steps take seconds. Assessment and the call follow immediately. |  |
| 7 | How strong is the evidence for the head-down, legs-up position? | Expert opinion, with a note that its effect may be limited | The European guideline grades it as opinion. It is used because it is quick, carries little risk in a patient who is breathing comfortably, and fits the physiology. |  |
| 8 | A patient recovers after first steps. The nurse asks whether to restart fluid removal at the original rate. What is the best answer? | Ask the attending nephrologist how to manage the remaining goal | The remaining goal, the time, and the dry weight belong to the prescription. |  |
| 9 | Ten minutes into a session, a man becomes itchy and flushed, begins to wheeze, and his pressure falls. What happens to the blood in the circuit? | Do not return it. Stop the blood pump and clamp the lines | Onset in the first minutes with itching and wheeze suggests a dialyzer reaction. That blood is not returned. |  |
| 10 | A woman with a dialysis catheter has a pressure of 86/50 mm Hg forty minutes into her session. She is shivering and feels warm. What is the best interpretation? | Possible sepsis. Call the nephrologist and start the fever protocol | An early drop with chills and a catheter does not fit fluid removal. |  |
| 11 | Which finding makes a simple volume-related drop less likely? | A patient who arrived at or below the dry weight | A patient who is not overloaded has little fluid to lose. Look for another cause, or a dry weight set too low. |  |
| 12 | What did the MyTEMP trial show? | Cooler dialysis fluid as a center-wide policy did not reduce cardiovascular death or admission | The hazard ratio was 1.00. The trial tested a policy, not cooling for individual patients with frequent episodes. |  |
| 13 | What did the echocardiography studies by Burton and colleagues show? | Heart muscle function often falls during dialysis, and this is associated with later harm | Stunning occurred in 64% of 70 patients and was associated with higher mortality. The studies were small and observational. |  |
| 14 | A man has had low pressure in four of his last six sessions, each time after a full meal in hour two. What should the physician on duty do? | Report the pattern to the attending nephrologist and suggest moving the meal | Moving the meal addresses the trigger and protects nutrition. The plan belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 15 | During an episode, the blood in the venous line looks translucent, like red wine, and the patient has back pain. A second patient nearby is also unwell. What do you do? | Stop the blood pump, clamp the lines, do not return the blood, and call for help | These findings suggest hemolysis, possibly from the dialysis fluid. Hemolyzed blood is not returned. |  |

## Module 14: High Blood Pressure and the Breathless Patient

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In cohort studies of hemodialysis patients, which readings predicted death? | Home and ambulatory readings | Home and ambulatory systolic pressure predicted mortality; pre- and post-dialysis readings did not. |  |
| 2 | Why does a pre-dialysis reading on Monday tend to overstate a patient’s usual pressure? | It comes at the peak of fluid gain after the long gap, often with a white-coat effect and hurried technique | Peak volume, anxiety, and rushed technique push it up. |  |
| 3 | Mrs. Reyes has a pre-dialysis reading of 178/96 mm Hg. She feels well and has no headache, chest pain, or neurologic signs. Her home log averages 138 mm Hg systolic. What is the best action? | Recheck after rest with a correct cuff, proceed per protocol, and report the pattern | A high reading without organ damage is not an emergency, and her home log suggests the unit number overstates her usual pressure. |  |
| 4 | What did the DRIP trial show? | Lowering target weight by about 1 kg lowered ambulatory systolic pressure without worsening quality of life | Ambulatory systolic pressure fell by about 7 mm Hg; low-pressure symptoms rose. Death was not studied. |  |
| 5 | A man takes four blood pressure drugs, and his home pressure stays high. He says he takes them all. What is the most important treatable cause to raise with the attending nephrologist? | Hidden volume overload | After white-coat effect and missed doses are excluded, volume is the most important treatable cause of resistance. |  |
| 6 | What did the BID pilot trial of blood pressure targets in dialysis show? | A possible safety signal in the intensive arm, in a trial too small to conclude | Four deaths versus one, with nonsignificant differences in other outcomes. |  |
| 7 | In the HDPAL trial, which statement is accurate? | Serious cardiovascular events were more frequent with lisinopril, and the trial was stopped early | Pressure improved similarly; events were more frequent with lisinopril. |  |
| 8 | A patient’s pressure rises from 150 to 172 mm Hg during most sessions. He takes lisinopril each morning. Which drug fact is most relevant to report? | Lisinopril is removed by dialysis, so its effect may fall during the session | Most ACE inhibitors are dialyzable; the nephrologist may consider a non-dialyzable agent. |  |
| 9 | Which statement about intradialytic hypertension is best supported? | Its link with death is observational and may largely reflect high pressure between sessions | In one cohort the association lost significance after adjustment for 44-hour ambulatory pressure. |  |
| 10 | During a session, Mr. Santos’s pressure rises to 196/108 mm Hg. He now has a severe headache and is confused. What do you do? | Treat it as a possible hypertensive emergency: follow the emergency protocol, call the nephrologist, and prepare for transfer | Headache with confusion suggests brain injury. Organ damage defines the emergency. |  |
| 11 | Which finding defines a hypertensive emergency? | High pressure with new or worsening target-organ damage | The number alone does not define the emergency; organ damage does. |  |
| 12 | Mrs. Dela Cruz becomes breathless ten minutes after connection. She is itchy and wheezing, and has little fluid on board. What is the most likely door? | Reaction | Early onset with itching and wheeze suggests a dialyzer reaction (Module 16). |  |
| 13 | In a cohort of 392 hemodialysis patients, what proportion of those with moderate to severe lung congestion on ultrasound had no or only slight symptoms? | About 70% | 71% were asymptomatic or slightly symptomatic. |  |
| 14 | Mang Tonyo has no urine output and arrives with pulmonary edema. A colleague suggests a high dose of a loop diuretic instead of dialysis. What is the best reply? | With no filtration, a diuretic has no urine to increase; fluid can be removed only by the dialyzer | A loop diuretic acts on the tubule. Without filtration there is no urine for it to increase. |  |
| 15 | A patient with pulmonary edema is connected and dialysis is running. His saturation keeps falling and he develops chest pain with new ST depression. The nephrologist advises transfer. What happens to the blood in the circuit? | Return it per protocol, since the circuit is sound and the cause lies in the patient | The cause lies in the patient and the circuit is sound. Blood is not returned with air, hemolysis, a reaction, or a contaminated or clotted circuit. |  |

## Module 15: Heart Emergencies in the Chair: Chest Pain, Arrhythmia, and Cardiac Arrest

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Ten minutes into a session with a new dialyzer type, a patient develops itching, wheeze, and a falling blood pressure. What is done with the blood in the circuit? | Clamp the lines and do not return it | A severe early reaction may be caused by the dialyzer. Returning the blood delivers more of the trigger. |  |
| 2 | Why is a single raised troponin a poor guide to heart attack in a dialysis patient? | Most dialysis patients have a chronically raised value, so the change over time matters | Chronic elevation is common. A rise or fall with evidence of ischemia defines an acute heart attack. |  |
| 3 | A 70-year-old woman with diabetes becomes breathless and sweaty in the third hour. Her blood pressure has fallen. She has no chest pain. After the first protocol steps, what comes next? | A 12-lead ECG and a call to the attending nephrologist | Fewer than half of dialysis patients with a heart attack had chest pain in a large registry. Treat these signs as possible ischemia. |  |
| 4 | According to the ERC 2025 guidance, who operates the dialysis machine during a cardiac arrest? | A trained dialysis nurse or technician assigned to it | Assigning the machine to trained staff leaves the leader free to direct the code. |  |
| 5 | A patient collapses during dialysis, and the nurse sees foam in the venous line. What is correct for the circuit? | Stop the pump, clamp the venous line, and do not return the blood | Air in the venous line is a do-not-return condition. Returning the blood would push more air into the patient. |  |
| 6 | What does the ERC 2025 guidance say about the dialysis access during a cardiac arrest? | Leave it open and use it for drugs | The access is already in the circulation and is faster than a new cannula. |  |
| 7 | When must a patient be disconnected from the dialysis machine before a shock? | Whenever the machine is not rated defibrillation-proof | The ERC advises disconnection unless the machine is defibrillation-proof. Know your machines’ rating before a code. |  |
| 8 | A man who missed a session arrests in the waiting area before dialysis. The monitor shows a slow, wide rhythm. Which reversible cause leads your list? | High potassium | Arrest before a session after a long gap points to hyperkalemia. The other three need a running circuit. |  |
| 9 | Why do arrhythmias cluster around the first session after the long gap? | Potassium and fluid peak, then fall fastest in that session | Both the peak and the rapid correction stress the rhythm. |  |
| 10 | Studies disagree on whether the first rhythm in dialysis unit arrests is usually shockable. What follows in practice? | Attach the defibrillator early in every arrest and let the rhythm decide | Either type of rhythm may be present, so early rhythm analysis is needed alongside immediate compressions. |  |
| 11 | A patient develops new atrial fibrillation in the second hour. He is stable and feels well. A colleague suggests starting a blood thinner today. What is the best response? | Record an ECG, look for triggers, and call the attending nephrologist | Evidence for long-term AF treatment in dialysis patients is limited. The decision belongs to the nephrologist and cardiologist. |  |
| 12 | How do the ERC and AHA 2025 guidelines compare on intravenous calcium for cardiac arrest from high potassium? | The ERC recommends it, and the AHA says its effectiveness is not well established | The guidelines differ, and both rest on limited evidence. Your unit’s protocol decides. |  |
| 13 | A patient arrests in a half-reclined dialysis chair. What is the first step for positioning? | Recline the chair flat and start compressions | Compressions start where the patient is, on the back, on as firm a surface as can be achieved without delay. |  |
| 14 | A session is stopped for chest pain with new ST depression. The patient is stable. How does he travel to the emergency room? | By ambulance with monitoring and an escort | For suspected acute coronary syndrome, the chest pain guideline recommends transfer by emergency medical services. |  |
| 15 | Which statement about the debrief after a code is most accurate? | An immediate debrief and a later review are both reasonable, and the focus is performance | The AHA supports performance-focused debriefing, both immediate and delayed. |  |

## Module 16: When the Circuit Fails: Air, Hemolysis, Reactions, and Blood Loss

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is the circuit blood not returned when hemolysis is suspected? | It contains potassium released from broken red cells | Returning it can cause severe hyperkalemia in a patient who cannot excrete potassium. |  |
| 2 | Twelve minutes into a first treatment with a new dialyzer, a patient has itching, a hoarse voice, wheeze, and a falling blood pressure. What happens to the blood in the circuit? | It is not returned. Dialysis stops and the lines are clamped | This is a severe early (type A) reaction. The circuit blood carries the trigger. |  |
| 3 | Why may the venous pressure alarm stay silent when the venous needle comes out? | The reading falls only by the pressure inside the access, which is often low in a fistula | Tubing and needle resistance remain, so the pressure can stay within the alarm limits. |  |
| 4 | Mr. Aquino, 78, has dementia and keeps pulling at his blanket. The machine is quiet. He is now pale and drowsy. What do you do first? | Lift the blanket and look at the access and lines | A covered access in a restless patient is the usual setting for a dislodged venous needle. A silent machine does not exclude it. |  |
| 5 | A patient coughs and becomes breathless, and there is foam below the venous chamber. The pump is stopped and the line clamped. Which position do published reviews advise for venous air embolism? | Lying on the left side | The left lateral position tends to hold air in the right heart. Sitting upright lets air rise toward the brain. |  |
| 6 | Why does an opening in the circuit before the blood pump draw air in? | That segment is under negative pressure because the pump is pulling | Pressure there is below atmospheric, so an opening admits air instead of leaking blood. |  |
| 7 | Which pair describes a type B dialyzer reaction? | Later in treatment; chest and back pain, usually mild | Type B reactions come later and are usually mild. Dangerous causes of chest pain are excluded first. |  |
| 8 | Three patients in one shift develop abdominal pain and high blood pressure within 30 minutes. What is the best next step? | Declare a possible cluster event, stop treatments per protocol, and have the water and dialysis fluid checked | The same new symptoms in several patients point to a shared cause. Blood is not returned if hemolysis is possible. |  |
| 9 | The venous chamber holds a large clot and the dialyzer shows dark streaks. A staff member suggests pushing saline hard to return the blood. What do you advise? | Do not return blood through the clotted parts, record the loss, and call the nephrologist | Clot must never be pushed toward the patient. |  |
| 10 | Which part of the water system removes chloramine? | The carbon tanks | Chloramine passes through reverse osmosis. Exhausted carbon has led to outbreaks of hemolysis. |  |
| 11 | A conductivity alarm returns after each reset and the fluid is in bypass. A staff member proposes overriding it to finish the last hour. What is correct? | Do not override. Call the technician and the nephrologist | Fluid that is too dilute can break red cells. The bypass is protecting the patient. |  |
| 12 | What did investigators find in the 1998 multistate outbreak of hemolysis? | A lot of blood tubing with a narrowed opening | The one common factor across three states was a single lot of tubing. |  |
| 13 | Ms. Lim has her first dialysis with a very high urea. Near the end she has a headache and becomes confused. The lines and blood look normal. Which statement fits best? | Dialysis disequilibrium is possible. The treatment stops and the nephrologist is called | New neurologic symptoms in a first treatment with a high urea suggest disequilibrium, which is not a circuit fault. |  |
| 14 | What does a confirmed blood leak alarm mean? | The dialyzer membrane has torn | The detector sits in the used dialysis fluid. Blood return then follows unit protocol and the nephrologist. |  |
| 15 | You cannot tell whether sudden chest pain is hemolysis, a reaction, or cardiac. The pump is stopped and the lines clamped. What is the safest position on blood return? | Do not return it until you have spoken to the nephrologist | Giving up the circuit blood costs less than returning air, broken cells, or an allergen. |  |

## Module 17: The Shivering Patient: Fever and Bloodstream Infection

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why can a catheter infection return after a full course of antibiotics? | Bacteria in a biofilm on the catheter wall are shielded from antibiotics and white cells | Biofilm shields bacteria, which then reseed the blood. |  |
| 2 | In United States surveillance, how did a central venous catheter compare with a fistula for S. aureus bloodstream infection? | It was associated with a rate about six times higher | The adjusted rate ratio was 6.2, from observational data. |  |
| 3 | Three patients on machines of the same model begin shivering within the first hour of the morning shift. All were well on arrival. What is the first priority? | Declare a possible cluster, follow the cluster protocol, and inform the Medical Director at once | Several patients with one new symptom point to a shared source. Each patient is still assessed and cultured. |  |
| 4 | Why are blood cultures still drawn when a pyrogenic reaction is suspected? | Contaminated fluid can carry live bacteria and cause true bacteremia | In one outbreak, bad water and poor machine disinfection caused both pyrogenic reactions and gram-negative bacteremia. |  |
| 5 | A man with a tunneled catheter starts shaking minutes after his catheter is flushed and connected. He felt well on arrival. What does the timing suggest? | Bacteria in the catheter lumen entering the blood, which cultures must confirm | The timing is a clinical clue, not a validated test. |  |
| 6 | In a dialysis patient with a catheter and suspected catheter infection, where should the culture sets usually come from? | The catheter hub and the dialysis circuit, per the unit’s protocol | KDOQI draws from the hub and circuit, and circuit cultures were the most accurate in a diagnostic study. |  |
| 7 | What is the main reason to avoid arm-vein punctures for cultures in a dialysis patient? | Damaged veins may not be usable for a future fistula | IDSA advises that peripheral samples in hemodialysis patients come from vessels not intended for future access (A-III). |  |
| 8 | A woman on dialysis has a qSOFA score of 1. How should you interpret it? | It does not exclude sepsis; judge her by the change from her baseline | qSOFA was derived to flag risk, not to rule sepsis out. The current guideline recommends against using it as the only screen. |  |
| 9 | Why can a SOFA score not rise in the kidney component for a patient on maintenance dialysis? | The kidney component already scores its maximum | A rise of 2 or more must come from breathing, clotting, liver, circulation, or the brain. |  |
| 10 | A febrile man with a catheter has a systolic pressure of 92 mm Hg, down from 150 at the start, while fluid removal continues. What do you do first? | Stop fluid removal per protocol, reassess, and call the attending nephrologist | Part of the fall may be fluid removal. Stop it, reassess, and call. |  |
| 11 | What does the 2026 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guideline say about the timing of antimicrobials in septic shock? | Immediately, ideally within 1 hour of recognition, strong recommendation with very low certainty | The recommendation is strong despite very low certainty of evidence. |  |
| 12 | A patient’s blood cultures grow S. aureus from a tunneled catheter. What does the IDSA guideline advise about the catheter? | Remove it and place a temporary catheter at another site | Removal is advised for S. aureus, Pseudomonas, and Candida (A-II). A guidewire exchange is an option if no other site exists (B-II). |  |
| 13 | A dialysis patient treated for S. aureus bacteremia a month ago has new back pain and a low fever. What is the best action? | Examine the spine and legs, ask about weakness and bladder change, and call the attending nephrologist the same day | Spine infection is often diagnosed late, and about one quarter of reported patients developed deficits. |  |
| 14 | In the international registry, how did endocarditis outcomes in hemodialysis patients compare with other patients? | In-hospital mortality was higher, 30.4% versus 17% | Six-month mortality and relapse were also higher. The data are observational. |  |
| 15 | A patient’s session ends early for an isolated catheter infection. The circuit is intact and unclotted. Another patient that shift had a suspected contaminated solution. What applies to each circuit? | Return the blood in the isolated infection per protocol; in the suspected contamination, clamp and ask the attending nephrologist first | When the fluid or circuit may be the cause, the blood is not returned without the nephrologist’s advice. |  |

## Module 18: The Confused or Seizing Patient: Potassium, Sugar, and the Brain

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is the predialysis potassium usually highest on the first session of the week? | The long gap gives about three days of intake with almost no kidney excretion | Intake continues while the exit is nearly closed. |  |
| 2 | A patient’s predialysis potassium is 7.0 mmol/L and the ECG looks normal. What is the best interpretation? | The heart may still be at risk, because the ECG is insensitive for high potassium | The ECG is insensitive for high potassium. |  |
| 3 | What does intravenous calcium do in high potassium? | It raises the firing threshold of heart muscle without lowering potassium | Calcium protects the membrane; potassium does not change. |  |
| 4 | A man on dialysis has a potassium of 6.8 mmol/L in hour one, with peaked T waves. He is otherwise stable. The nurse asks whether to stop dialysis while the order set is started. What is the best answer? | Keep dialysis running, because it removes potassium, and call the attending nephrologist | Dialysis is the step that removes potassium from the body. |  |
| 5 | What did the 2025 systematic review of acute hyperkalemia treatment find? | Insulin with glucose, salbutamol, or both lowered potassium on average, with very low to low certainty | Average falls were 0.7 to 1.2 mmol/L. No clinical benefit was shown for calcium or bicarbonate. |  |
| 6 | A woman without diabetes receives insulin with glucose for high potassium at 9:00 a.m. Her starting glucose was 92 mg/dL. When is she most likely to become hypoglycemic, based on published data? | Around 2 hours later, and the low may last about 2 hours | Median onset was 2 hours, median duration 2 hours. Patients without diabetes were at higher risk. |  |
| 7 | What did DIALIZE-Outcomes show about sodium zirconium cyclosilicate in hemodialysis patients? | It improved potassium control but did not reduce arrhythmia-related events | Normokalemia 74.0% versus 47.0%; events 8.8% versus 8.9%. |  |
| 8 | Which statement about potassium binders in dialysis is best supported? | They lower a surrogate value; benefit on patient outcomes is not shown | No trial has shown fewer deaths or arrhythmias. |  |
| 9 | Two hours after a session with a low-potassium dialysis fluid, a patient on digoxin reports palpitations. What is the best first step? | Obtain an ECG and electrolytes, and call the attending nephrologist | Low potassium with digoxin raises arrhythmia risk. |  |
| 10 | Why does blood sugar tend to fall during dialysis? | Glucose leaves into the dialysis fluid, insulin is cleared slowly, and the failed kidney makes little new glucose | Several links act together. |  |
| 11 | A woman with type 2 diabetes becomes quiet and drowsy in hour three. Her pressure is normal. What do you check first? | Capillary glucose | It takes one minute, and lows in the chair are often silent. |  |
| 12 | A man on dialysis started a new medicine for shingles from another doctor three days ago. Today he is confused and hallucinating in the chair. Glucose, pressure, and oxygen are normal. What is the most likely cause? | Accumulation of acyclovir or valacyclovir | These drugs accumulate in kidney failure and cause confusion and hallucinations. |  |
| 13 | A patient has a generalized seizure during dialysis. Which sequence fits this module? | Protect the patient and the access, stop the blood pump per protocol, airway and oxygen, glucose, and call | Protect first. The antiseizure drug comes from the unit’s protocol. |  |
| 14 | At 9:45 a.m. a nurse notices a new facial droop. She last saw the patient normal at 9:10 a.m. Heparin was given at 7:30 a.m. From what time does the stroke clock run? | 9:10 a.m. | The clock starts at last known well. |  |
| 15 | What do the registry data on thrombolysis in dialysis patients show? | Similar brain hemorrhage rates, but higher in-hospital death, in observational data | Hemorrhage 5.2% versus 6.1%; death 22% versus 11%. The stroke team decides eligibility. |  |

## Module 19: Bleeding and Heparin

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How does heparin prevent clotting in the dialysis circuit? | It makes antithrombin switch off thrombin and factor Xa much faster | Heparin works through antithrombin. It prevents new fibrin but does not dissolve existing clot. |  |
| 2 | Why does anemia add to bleeding in a dialysis patient? | With fewer red cells in the center of the stream, fewer platelets are pushed toward the vessel wall | Red cells push platelets toward the vessel wall. |  |
| 3 | What did the low-dose heparin study by Murea and colleagues show? | Heparin per session fell by more than half while the urea reduction ratio was maintained, in a before-and-after comparison | A nonrandomized study that did not test bleeding or survival. |  |
| 4 | A man arrives for dialysis and mentions that a skin lesion was removed from his back this morning. The wound is dry under a dressing. What do you do before the heparin bolus? | Treat the procedure as a yes on the gate and call the attending nephrologist before heparin | A recent procedure is a yes even when the wound looks dry. |  |
| 5 | In the USRDS cohort studied by Wasse and colleagues, which factor was associated with upper GI bleeding after adjustment? | Cardiovascular disease | Cardiovascular disease, smoking, and inability to walk independently were associated with bleeding. |  |
| 6 | In the cohort of 12,468 patients described by Shen and colleagues, how was heparin-free dialysis related to bleeding? | It was not associated with less bleeding, but patients went heparin-free because they were already at risk | The bleeding hazard ratio was 1.15, and confounding limits any conclusion. |  |
| 7 | A woman is on a heparin-free session after a biopsy. In hour two the venous pressure has risen steadily, and dark streaks appear in the dialyzer during a flush. What is the best next step? | Call the attending nephrologist now, before the circuit clots, and follow the unit’s protocol | These are early signs of clotting. An early call saves blood and treatment time. |  |
| 8 | What did the EvoCit trial show about a heparin-grafted dialyzer with citrate-enriched dialysis fluid and no systemic heparin? | It was noninferior for Kt/V, but 4.2% of sessions ended early for clotting | Kt/V is a surrogate, and clinical outcomes were not measured. |  |
| 9 | At the end of Mrs. Lim’s session, her arterial needle site keeps oozing after removal. How should it be pressed? | Two fingertips and a small gauze over the vessel hole and the skin hole, held for the set time without lifting | Lifting early breaks the forming clot. |  |
| 10 | For three sessions in a row, a man’s venous needle site in his graft has bled long after correct pressure. What does this pattern most suggest? | A narrowing downstream that raises pressure in the graft | This points to outflow stenosis. Report it for access assessment. |  |
| 11 | A 70-year-old woman on dialysis has a pressure that falls faster than her fluid removal explains, and her pulse has risen from 80 to 110 per minute. She mentions black stools this week. What do you do? | Stop heparin per protocol, assess, call the attending nephrologist, and prepare for transfer | This suggests upper GI bleeding. Do not wait for laboratory results to call. |  |
| 12 | Why should the Glasgow-Blatchford score be read with care in a dialysis patient? | Its blood urea item is high in every dialysis patient, so the score overstates risk | The score is context, not a decision. |  |
| 13 | A man started dialysis 9 days ago after a hospital stay with heparin injections. His platelets have fallen from 230 to 95 × 109/L, and his catheter clotted. The 4Ts score is high. What happens to heparin? | Every source stops, including catheter locks and flushes, and the nephrologist is called the same day | When HIT is possible, all heparin stops. |  |
| 14 | In the study by Lo and colleagues, what did a low 4Ts score show? | It ruled HIT out well in both settings | Low-score patients almost never had significant antibodies. |  |
| 15 | What is the best summary of the evidence on oral anticoagulants for hemodialysis patients with atrial fibrillation? | Small, inconclusive trials and favorable observational data | The trials were underpowered; the 2026 analysis was observational. |  |

## Module 20: Prescribing Without Kidneys

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a kidney-cleared drug build up in a hemodialysis patient even though dialysis removes it? | Between sessions there is no kidney clearance, so each dose adds to what remains | Dialysis runs a few hours a few times a week. Between sessions an anuric patient has no route out. |  |
| 2 | Which drug properties predict good removal by hemodialysis? | Small, little protein binding, mostly in the blood | Only free molecules in plasma water cross the membrane. |  |
| 3 | A nurse asks you to double a new antibiotic dose “because dialysis will wash it out.” What is the best response? | Check the hemodialysis line in the unit’s reference and call the attending nephrologist first | Many drugs are barely removed, and removed drugs still accumulate between sessions. |  |
| 4 | Why does an eGFR from serum creatinine mislead when dosing for an anuric dialysis patient? | Creatinine is not at steady state and reflects the dialysis schedule | The equations assume a steady creatinine. In dialysis it rises between sessions and falls during them. |  |
| 5 | Mrs. Reyes, 76, on hemodialysis, became drowsy and confused three days after starting a new medicine. Which is the most likely culprit? | Valacyclovir, started three days ago for shingles | Confusion a few days after a kidney-cleared drug is started is the classic pattern. Published cases had a mean onset of 3.1 days. |  |
| 6 | A hemodialysis patient on cefepime for four days now has jerking arms and is hard to rouse. What is the key consideration? | Cefepime neurotoxicity, including possible nonconvulsive seizures; call the attending nephrologist now | In one review, a quarter of those with an EEG had nonconvulsive status epilepticus. |  |
| 7 | What did Ishida and colleagues find about gabapentin in hemodialysis patients? | Even low doses were associated with a higher hazard of altered mental status | Low doses carried 31% to 41% higher hazards. The study was observational, so it shows association, not proof of cause. |  |
| 8 | In older adults with CKD, 1.9% on a higher versus 1.5% on a lower starting gabapentinoid dose had a serious event within 30 days. What is the best reading? | A small absolute difference, about one extra event per 250 patients, in people not on dialysis | The risk ratio was 1.27, but the absolute difference was 0.40%. Dialysis patients were excluded. |  |
| 9 | Mr. Dizon, 63, on hemodialysis, has metformin on a list from a new clinic doctor. What do you do? | Flag it to the attending nephrologist; the ADA-KDIGO consensus calls it contraindicated with dialysis | The KDIGO 1B recommendation applies to an eGFR of 30 or more. |  |
| 10 | A woman on hemodialysis takes glibenclamide bought by a relative. Her glucose is low in hour two and recovers with treatment by protocol. What is the main concern? | Her glucose may fall again for hours; arrange repeated checks and call the attending | A long-acting sulfonylurea with active metabolites can cause recurrent low glucose. |  |
| 11 | In the apixaban study by Mavrakanas and colleagues, how much of the drug did a hemodialysis session remove? | About 4% | Apixaban is largely protein-bound. The study had seven patients and measured drug levels, a surrogate. |  |
| 12 | Why is an antibiotic loading dose usually not reduced in a dialysis patient? | It fills the volume of distribution, which kidney failure does not shrink | Clearance falls in kidney failure, so maintenance changes. The volume the first dose fills does not. |  |
| 13 | A dialysis patient with good residual urine asks for ibuprofen for knee pain. What is the best first step? | Examine the knee, use non-drug measures and paracetamol per protocol, and avoid repeated NSAIDs | NSAIDs threaten residual function and raise potassium. Ketorolac is on the Beers list to avoid. |  |
| 14 | Which over-the-counter product most often raises potassium dangerously? | A potassium chloride salt substitute | Salt substitutes replace sodium with potassium, which the patient cannot excrete. |  |
| 15 | Mr. Santos, 68, on hemodialysis, takes an unlabeled herbal capsule from abroad. How do you handle it? | Ask without judgment, identify the ingredients if possible, record it, and report it to the attending nephrologist | KDIGO advises reviewing and limiting such remedies. Scolding drives them out of sight. |  |

## Module 21: Blood and Bone: Anemia and Mineral Disorders

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does inflammation cause anemia in a dialysis patient even when iron stores are full? | It raises hepcidin, which traps iron in storage cells | Hepcidin closes the iron exit channel, so stored iron cannot reach the marrow. |  |
| 2 | A patient has ferritin 900 µg/L, TSAT 12%, and a high C-reactive protein. Which pattern is this? | Functional iron deficiency | Iron is stored but not moving. Inflammation is the usual lock. |  |
| 3 | A patient has ferritin 60 µg/L and TSAT 11%. What should you look for first? | A source of blood loss | Empty stores in a dialysis patient usually mean blood loss. |  |
| 4 | What did PIVOTAL show for proactive high-dose intravenous iron compared with reactive low-dose iron? | Fewer cardiovascular events and deaths, with lower ESA doses | 29.3% vs. 32.3% (hazard ratio 0.85), with equal infection rates. |  |
| 5 | What grade does KDIGO 2026 give to intravenous rather than oral iron in hemodialysis? | 2D | A suggestion on very low certainty evidence. |  |
| 6 | Which statement about hemoglobin targets do the trials support? | Aiming for normal increased strokes or cardiovascular events without better quality of life | Normal Hematocrit, CHOIR, and TREAT all pointed to harm. KDIGO 2026 sets a ceiling below 11.5 g/dL (1D). |  |
| 7 | What did ASCEND-D and INNO2VATE show about HIF-PHIs in dialysis? | Noninferior to ESAs for major cardiovascular events | Noninferior is not better. KDIGO 2026 still suggests an ESA first (2D). |  |
| 8 | Mr. Reyes’s hemoglobin fell from 10.2 to 8.4 g/dL in a month. He reports black stools, and his pulse is 108 at the start of the session. What do you do? | Treat it as possible bleeding and call the attending nephrologist now | A fast fall with black stools and a fast pulse is bleeding until proven otherwise. |  |
| 9 | Mrs. Dizon’s hemoglobin has drifted down over three months despite rising ESA doses. Her catheter exit site is red and C-reactive protein is high. What is most likely? | Catheter inflammation blocking the ESA response | Inflammation is a leading cause of poor response. Report the exit site today. |  |
| 10 | Mr. Cruz, on the transplant waiting list, has a hemoglobin of 7.8 g/dL, no symptoms, normal vital signs, and no bleeding. The nurse asks about transfusion. What is best? | Call the attending nephrologist first, noting his transplant plans and lack of symptoms | Transfuse for symptoms, and avoid transfusion when possible in transplant candidates. |  |
| 11 | An intact PTH is 520 pg/mL; the assay’s upper limit is 65 pg/mL. Where does it sit against KDIGO 2017? | At 8 times, within about 2 to 9 times | 520 ÷ 65 = 8 (2C suggestion). |  |
| 12 | What did EVOLVE show? | No significant reduction in death or major cardiovascular events in the primary analysis | 48.2% vs. 49.2% (hazard ratio 0.93, 0.85 to 1.02). |  |
| 13 | What did the HiLo phosphate target trial conclude? | No inference was possible | It stopped early with poor enrollment and separation. |  |
| 14 | Mrs. Bautista takes cinacalcet and reports tingling around her mouth and finger cramps. What do you do? | Suspect low calcium: follow protocol, check calcium and an ECG, and call | Calcimimetics lower calcium. Symptoms make it a same-day call. |  |
| 15 | Mrs. Santos, 61, has diabetes and obesity, takes warfarin, and reports a very painful, firm purple patch on her thigh. What is best? | Inspect, describe, and photograph it with consent, and call the attending nephrologist the same day | This suggests calciphylaxis. Medicine changes belong to the attending nephrologist. |  |

## Module 22: Diabetes on Dialysis

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a patient’s insulin requirement often fall as kidney function is lost? | The kidney clears less insulin, and kidney glucose production falls | Insulin lingers and the kidney adds less glucose between meals. |  |
| 2 | A man on premixed insulin skipped breakfast. At hour two he is sweaty and his pressure has fallen. What do you check first, beside the pressure? | A capillary glucose | A low and a falling pressure look alike and can occur together. A fingerstick takes two minutes. |  |
| 3 | In the sensor study by Hayashi and colleagues, dialysis fluid contained 100 to 150 mg/dL glucose. What did they find? | One in five patients had a low related to dialysis, and none had symptoms | 21% had silent lows despite a glucose-containing bath. |  |
| 4 | When may a low related to dialysis occur, according to the sensor data? | During the session or after it, before the next meal | The study counted lows between the end of dialysis and the next meal. The ride home is part of the risk. |  |
| 5 | Which change in a dialysis patient tends to make HbA1c falsely low? | A rising dose of an erythropoiesis-stimulating agent | An ESA releases young, lightly glycated red cells. |  |
| 6 | A woman on insulin has an HbA1c of 5.6%. Her meter log shows readings from 50 to 320 mg/dL. Which interpretation is best? | The HbA1c may be biased low and may also reflect frequent lows. Report the discordance | In dialysis a low HbA1c can mean young red cells or frequent lows. The readings come first. |  |
| 7 | What does KDIGO 2022 Recommendation 2.2.1 (1C) say about HbA1c targets? | An individualized target from below 6.5% to below 8.0%, for patients not treated with dialysis | The recommendation excludes dialysis. No guideline gives an HbA1c target for dialysis patients. |  |
| 8 | In the DOPPS cohort, how was HbA1c associated with death in hemodialysis patients? | The association was U-shaped, lowest at 7.0% to 7.9% | Risk rose at both ends. The data are observational. |  |
