| 9 | A patient’s sensor shows 62 mg/dL, but he looks well and is chatting. What do you do? | Confirm with a fingerstick and follow the protocol | Sensors differ in accuracy in dialysis, and lows are often silent. Confirm, then act per protocol. |  |
| 10 | A mean sensor glucose is 190 mg/dL and the laboratory HbA1c is 6.4%. Using GMI = 3.31 + 0.02392 × mean glucose, what is the discordance? | About 1.5 percentage points, with the GMI higher | The GMI is about 7.9%. Report the gap. |  |
| 11 | A new patient’s list includes metformin. What does KDIGO 2022 say? | Recommendation 4.1.1 (1B) applies at eGFR 30 or more, and the algorithm stops metformin below 30 | Flag it for the attending nephrologist. You do not stop it yourself. |  |
| 12 | A patient who has started dialysis is still taking empagliflozin. What is the best action? | Flag it for the attending nephrologist, citing the practice point to continue until kidney replacement therapy starts | Practice point 1.3.6 says continue until kidney replacement therapy starts. |  |
| 13 | Which diabetes medicine on a dialysis patient’s list is most likely to cause a prolonged low? | Glibenclamide | Glibenclamide and its active products build up without kidneys. Lows can return hours after treatment. |  |
| 14 | During a foot check you find a dusky blue second toe on the left. The left foot is cooler than the right. What do you do? | Call the attending nephrologist now | A dusky toe and a cold foot suggest a threatened blood supply. Hours matter, so escalate at once; prompt review can save the limb. |  |
| 15 | A woman with long-standing diabetes feels full and bloated after small meals. She is low in the chair and high in the evening. What is the likely link? | Gastroparesis delays the meal while insulin and the dialyzer lower glucose | A slow stomach mismatches food and insulin. Report the pattern. |  |

## Module 23: Food, Fluid, and Muscle

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why do protein needs rise once a patient starts hemodialysis? | Amino acids are lost into the dialysis fluid, and inflammation and acidosis speed muscle breakdown | Each session removes amino acids, and inflammation and acidosis speed breakdown. |  |
| 2 | In the study by Ikizler and colleagues, about how much amino acid was lost per session? | About 6 to 8 grams | About 6 to 8 grams, depending on the membrane: real but modest. |  |
| 3 | What does the KDOQI 2020 guideline recommend for protein intake in metabolically stable adults on maintenance hemodialysis? | 1.0 to 1.2 g/kg per day, Grade 1C | A strong recommendation (1) on low-certainty evidence (C). |  |
| 4 | A 66-year-old woman on dialysis has an albumin of 3.1 g/dL. She has had a fever for two days and a catheter in her neck. Her weight is stable and she eats well. What is the best reading of her albumin? | Inflammation is a likely cause, so look for infection before labeling her malnourished | Inflammation lowers albumin. Alone, it does not diagnose wasting. |  |
| 5 | Under the ISRNM criteria, what is needed to diagnose protein-energy wasting? | Findings in at least three of four categories: chemistry, body mass, muscle mass, and intake | The panel required findings across categories. Inflammation markers are clues, not criteria. |  |
| 6 | A pooled analysis of 90 dialysis studies found that the middle half of studies reported protein-energy wasting in what share of patients? | 28% to 54% | The 25th to 75th percentile range was 28% to 54%. |  |
| 7 | Which tool does the KDOQI 2020 guideline describe as valid and reliable for nutritional assessment in adults on dialysis, with Grade 1B? | The 7-point Subjective Global Assessment | The MIS carries a weaker 2C statement. |  |
| 8 | A man’s post-dialysis weight fell from 70 to 65 kilograms in three months. He eats less, his temples are hollow, and he has no edema. The nurse asks whether to lower his dry weight. What do you do? | Leave the target as it is, run the nutrition screen, and call the attending nephrologist to review it | About 7% lost, with signs of tissue loss. The dry weight belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 9 | Why can a patient who loses muscle but keeps the same dry weight become overloaded? | The same target weight now holds less tissue and more fluid | If tissue falls and the target does not, water fills the gap. |  |
| 10 | A woman’s pre-dialysis potassium is high for the second month. She has stopped eating fruit, has not moved her bowels in four days, and missed a session last week. What is the best next step? | Report the constipation and missed session as likely causes to the attending nephrologist and ask the dietitian to review her intake | Missed dialysis and constipation explain it better than her diet. |  |
| 11 | What did the cluster randomized trial by Sullivan and colleagues show? | Teaching patients to avoid phosphorus additives lowered serum phosphorus more than usual care | The fall was 0.6 mg/dL larger at three months, a surrogate outcome. |  |
| 12 | A tricycle driver brings instant noodles and a cola to every session, and his phosphate stays high. What advice fits current guidance? | Keep the fresh protein, cut the processed foods, and see the dietitian | Additive phosphate is absorbed more completely than phosphate in fresh food. |  |
| 13 | What did the Cochrane review of oral protein-based supplements in dialysis find? | Supplements probably raise serum albumin a little, a surrogate outcome | The mean difference in albumin was 0.19 g/dL, with moderate certainty. Albumin is a surrogate. |  |
| 14 | In the randomized trial by Cano and colleagues, what did adding intradialytic parenteral nutrition to oral supplements do? | Did not improve two-year mortality, admissions, or nutritional markers | IDPN added nothing over oral supplements in 186 malnourished patients. |  |
| 15 | An 80-year-old man falls asleep with rice in his mouth during hour two and his pressure falls soon after. What is the best response after you clear his mouth and manage the drop? | Report the episode and suggest the team review whether and when he eats in the chair | Eating in the chair helps many and endangers a few. The team decides who eats, and when. |  |

## Module 24: Symptoms in the Chair and at Home: Cramps, Itch, Sleep, Pain, and Fatigue

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the systematic review by Murtagh and colleagues, which symptom had the highest weighted prevalence among dialysis patients? | Fatigue | Fatigue was reported by about 71% of patients, ahead of itch at 55% and pain at 47%. |  |
| 2 | What did DOPPS show about itch and the medical directors of dialysis units? | Most directors underestimated how common itch was in their units | 69% of directors underestimated prevalence, and 17% of very itchy patients had not told staff. |  |
| 3 | A man asks to stop at hour three because of calf cramps. His pressure is 82/50 mm Hg; it was 146/84 at the start. What is the best first interpretation? | A hypotension episode that needs the Module 13 response | A cramp with a low pressure is not minor. Fluid removal has outrun refilling. |  |
| 4 | A woman has calf cramps near the end of almost every session, and she reaches her target weight each time. What should you do? | Report the pattern to the attending nephrologist so the target weight and fluid plan can be reviewed | A recurring pattern near target weight suggests the target may be too low. The prescription belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 5 | How strong is the evidence for drugs to prevent dialysis cramps? | Low certainty: trials are small and heterogeneous, and no drug is established | Vitamins and L-carnitine show signals in small trials only. |  |
| 6 | Forty minutes into a session, a man reports a sudden, severe headache, unlike any before. He vomits once and is slow to answer. What do you do? | Call for help and the attending nephrologist now, check vital signs and neurologic status, and start the emergency protocol | A sudden, worst-ever headache with vomiting and slowed responses is a neurologic emergency until proven otherwise. |  |
| 7 | In the Cochrane review of itch treatments, which drug class had high-certainty evidence and the largest reduction in itch? | Gabapentinoids | Gabapentinoids reduced itch by about 4.95 cm on a 10-cm scale. Kappa-opioid agonists helped less. |  |
| 8 | In KALM-1, 51.9% of patients on difelikefalin and 30.9% on placebo had at least a 3-point fall in worst itch. About how many patients need treatment for one more responder? | About 5 | The absolute difference is 21 percentage points. 100 divided by 21 is about 4.8, rounded up to 5. |  |
| 9 | A man started gabapentin for itch two weeks ago. Today he is drowsy with jerking limbs. What is the likely explanation? | Gabapentin accumulation in kidney failure | Gabapentin is cleared by the kidneys. Drowsiness and myoclonus suggest toxicity. Call the nephrologist. |  |
| 10 | A woman says her legs feel crawly and must move when she sits still in the evening, and moving eases it. What should you check first? | Her iron studies | These are features of restless legs syndrome. Iron deficiency is a treatable contributor. |  |
| 11 | What did the randomized trial of insomnia treatments in hemodialysis patients find? | Neither CBT-I nor trazodone beat placebo, and serious cardiovascular events were more frequent with trazodone | The trial was modest, but it argues against reflex sedatives. |  |
| 12 | A patient on hemodialysis has chronic knee pain and asks for “something stronger.” Which response fits this module? | Ask about the cause, the score, and all current analgesics, then report to the nephrologist | Morphine and codeine metabolites accumulate in kidney failure. The nephrologist chooses any drug. |  |
| 13 | In the HOPE trial, pain interference fell 0.49 points more with coping skills training; meaningful improvement occurred in 50.9% versus 36.6%. What is the best reading? | A modest average effect hid a useful benefit in a subset, with about 7 trained per extra responder | Averages and responder rates tell different stories. The effect diminished by 36 weeks. |  |
| 14 | In DOPPS, what was a recovery time longer than 12 hours associated with, compared with 2 to 6 hours? | Higher hospitalization and mortality, in observational data | The adjusted hazard ratio for death was 1.47. The study was observational and cannot show cause. |  |
| 15 | A patient has signed off 50 minutes early every Friday for a month on a 240-minute, three-times-weekly prescription. What is the best action? | Find the cause, offer a fix, record the time lost, and inform the nephrologist | He has lost 200 minutes over four weeks, close to one full session. The prescription belongs to the nephrologist. |  |

## Module 25: Special Patients: Frailty, Complex Illness, and Pregnancy

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a patient with heart failure tolerate fast fluid removal poorly? | The failing heart has a narrow band between congestion and low output | A failing heart sits on a low, flat Frank-Starling curve, so a small change in filling matters. |  |
| 2 | A man with cirrhosis becomes drowsy and confused in hour two. What is the best first interpretation? | Confusion with several possible causes, including liver failure, low sugar, bleeding, and infection | Hepatic encephalopathy can look like uremia. Check sugar and vital signs, look for bleeding and infection, and call. |  |
| 3 | Frailty prevalence in hemodialysis ranged from 6% to 82% across studies. What best explains the range? | Studies used different tools in different populations | The pooled estimate was 34.3%, but the answer depends on the tool and the population. |  |
| 4 | What does a Clinical Frailty Scale score of moderately frail tell you about one dialysis patient? | That the patient needs closer watching and has a higher average risk of death | Each one-point rise was associated with a hazard ratio of 1.22 in a dialysis cohort. It describes group risk, not one person’s prognosis. |  |
| 5 | An 84-year-old woman stands after dialysis and says the room is spinning. Her standing pressure is much lower than sitting. What do you do first? | Sit or lay her down, recheck pressures, and apply the unit’s discharge criteria | A patient who is dizzy on standing is not ready to go. The fluid plan belongs to the nephrologist. |  |
| 6 | A frail man fell at home yesterday and struck his head. He takes an anticoagulant and seems well. What is the right step? | Call the attending nephrologist now, before the session proceeds | A head strike on an anticoagulant is a stop-and-call trigger, even when the patient looks well. Session heparin adds bleeding risk; the nephrologist decides how to proceed. |  |
| 7 | In the study by Murray and colleagues, what share of older hemodialysis patients had a documented diagnosis of cognitive impairment? | Fewer than 3 in 100 | Only 2.9% had a documented diagnosis, although most had moderate or severe impairment on testing. |  |
| 8 | What did Chiu and colleagues find about pill burden? | A median of 19 pills a day, with higher burden associated with lower physical quality of life | The study was cross-sectional. It shows an association, not that removing pills helps. |  |
| 9 | A daughter asks whether dialysis is still helping her frail mother. What is the best response? | Acknowledge the question, ask the patient what matters most, record it, and tell the nephrologist | You open the door without leading. The goals-of-care conversation belongs to the attending nephrologist. |  |
| 10 | A 19-year-old from a children’s unit keeps missing Saturday sessions. His mother answers for him. What is the best approach? | Speak to him first, ask what makes Saturday hard, and report the pattern without blame | The ISN and IPNA consensus asks for an individualized transition that builds self-management skills. |  |
| 11 | Why should you ask a woman on dialysis about contraception? | Fertility is reduced but not absent, and pregnancy changes the treatment at once | The UK guideline recommends contraception advice for all women of reproductive age with CKD (3.1.1, grade 1D). |  |
| 12 | Ms. Bautista, 29, on dialysis for two years, reports three missed periods and morning nausea. What is the first priority? | Tell the attending nephrologist the same day and arrange testing per protocol | These symptoms are common in kidney failure, which is why pregnancy is recognized late. Act the same day. |  |
| 13 | A non-pregnant woman on dialysis has a raised serum pregnancy hormone. What explains this possibility? | The kidney normally clears part of the hormone, so levels can rise without pregnancy | The evidence is case-level. A positive serum result needs specialist confirmation. |  |
| 14 | In the comparison by Hladunewich and colleagues, what was associated with more live births? | More weekly hours of hemodialysis | Live births were 48% at 20 hours a week or fewer and 85% above 36 hours. The comparison was observational. |  |
| 15 | A pregnant dialysis patient takes an ACE inhibitor. What do you do? | Flag it to the nephrologist and obstetrician the same day, without stopping it yourself | The guideline asks for a plan to stop or convert ACE inhibitors (3.3.5, grade 1B). Stopping without a replacement leaves her pressure uncontrolled. |  |

## Module 26: The Person in the Chair: Mood, Burden, and Choices About Care

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why can a depression symptom score overestimate depression in a patient on dialysis? | Fatigue, poor sleep, and poor appetite can come from uremia as well as from depression | Scales count somatic items that kidney failure can cause; interview-based prevalence was lower in the Palmer meta-analysis. |  |
| 2 | A man on dialysis scores 4 on the PHQ-9, but answers 1 on item 9. What do you do? | Start the safety pathway now, whatever the total | Any positive answer to item 9 starts the safety pathway. The total does not override it. |  |
| 3 | In the Watnick validation study in dialysis, what did a PHQ-9 score of 10 or more show? | Sensitivity and specificity of about 92%, in a small single-center sample | The study had 62 patients. It shows good accuracy against interview, not that screening improves outcomes. |  |
| 4 | A woman’s PHQ-9 total is 12. Her highest items are sleep, energy, and appetite; interest, mood, and item 9 are 0. What is the best interpretation? | Uremic symptoms may be driving the score; report it and look for physical causes as well | The somatic items overlap uremia. Report it, look for causes such as itch, anemia, or underdialysis, and let the attending nephrologist decide on referral. |  |
| 5 | What does the Cochrane review of antidepressants versus placebo in dialysis show? | Very low certainty evidence, no quality-of-life benefit shown, and more nausea | Four small trials with 170 participants could not show benefit with confidence. Nausea was more common. |  |
| 6 | The ASCEND trial found modestly lower depression scores with sertraline than with chairside CBT. What can you conclude? | Sertraline did better than CBT on a rating scale, but neither was compared with no treatment | ASCEND compared two active treatments. It had no placebo or no-treatment arm, and its outcome was a surrogate score. |  |
| 7 | A patient with a positive screen asks you to start him on an antidepressant today. What is the best response? | Report the screen and his preference to the attending nephrologist, who chooses and doses any medicine | Drug choice and dosing in kidney failure belong to the attending nephrologist. Your job is to record and refer. |  |
| 8 | A man has missed three Mondays. The head nurse wants you to warn him that he will die. What is the better first step? | Ask an open question about what happened and sort the barrier | A named barrier can be fixed. A warning without understanding rarely changes behavior. |  |
| 9 | In the DOPPS data, which factor was associated with fewer skipped sessions? | A higher share of highly trained staff hours | Each 10% more highly trained staff hours was associated with lower odds of skipping. Larger facility size showed only a borderline association with more skipping. |  |
| 10 | A patient was prescribed 13 sessions of 240 minutes. He missed 1 and the other 12 ran full time. What percent was delivered? | 92.3% | Delivered 12 × 240 = 2,880 minutes. Prescribed 3,120. 2,880 ÷ 3,120 = 92.3%. |  |
| 11 | A daughter who brings her mother to every session looks exhausted and has stopped working. What do you do? | Ask how she is managing, and route money, schedule, and her own strain to their owners | Caregiver strain threatens the patient’s care. Route each part to its owner and never promise coverage from memory. |  |
| 12 | A calm, oriented woman says she wants to stop dialysis. She has no thoughts of self-harm. What is your role today? | Listen, check capacity in outline, look for treatable drivers, and call the attending nephrologist the same day | The decision belongs to the patient with the attending nephrologist and family. Documents are covered in Module 31. |  |
| 13 | A man asks to stop dialysis and also says his family would be better off if he were dead. What comes first? | Start the safety pathway: stay with him, call the attending nephrologist and his emergency contact | Self-harm risk comes before the withdrawal question. He is not left alone or sent home alone. |  |
| 14 | A son asks you to stop his father’s dialysis. The father, who is alert, has said nothing about stopping. What do you do? | Listen to the son, then ask to hear from the father, record both, and call the attending nephrologist | While the patient can decide, his voice leads. A family request he has not voiced is a stop-and-call trigger. |  |
| 15 | What did the SPIRIT trial of advance care planning in dialysis clinics show? | Better agreement between patients and surrogates on end-of-life goals, and less decisional conflict | Its outcomes were preparation measures at two weeks. Surrogate confidence did not differ, and bereavement results were mixed. |  |

## Module 27: Other Roads: Transplant, Peritoneal Dialysis, and Conservative Care

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which function does a working kidney transplant provide that neither hemodialysis nor peritoneal dialysis provides? | Production of erythropoietin and activation of vitamin D | Both forms of dialysis replace part of filtration only. A transplant also restores hormone production. |  |
| 2 | Why did Wolfe and colleagues compare transplant recipients with patients on the waiting list rather than with all dialysis patients? | Waitlisted patients were also judged fit, which reduces the effect of selecting healthier patients | Comparing like with like reduces, but does not remove, selection bias. |  |
| 3 | In the waitlist comparison, how did the risk of death after transplant change over time? | It was higher in the first two weeks, then much lower by 18 months | Surgery carries early risk. The relative risk at 18 months was 0.32, and long-term mortality was 48% to 82% lower. |  |
| 4 | A 38-year-old man has been on hemodialysis for four years. His chart shows no transplant discussion and no documented contraindication. What should you do? | Flag the absent discussion to the attending nephrologist and document it | KDIGO asks that patients be informed and considered (1D). The referral is the nephrologist’s. |  |
| 5 | What is the grade of the KDIGO 2020 recommendation not to exclude patients from transplant because of age alone? | 1A | Statement 2.1.1 is graded 1A. |  |
| 6 | A waitlisted woman is stable, with a hemoglobin lower than last month. A transfusion has been ordered for today’s session. What is the best first step? | Hold it as your unit’s protocol allows and call the attending nephrologist, noting that she is waitlisted | A transfusion may sensitize her. When she is stable, there is time to call. |  |
| 7 | How does a blood transfusion reduce a patient’s chance of finding a compatible donor? | It exposes the immune system to foreign HLA, and the antibodies formed can rule out donors | Antibodies against HLA persist and can attack a donor kidney that carries the same antigens. |  |
| 8 | What drives water removal in peritoneal dialysis? | The osmotic gradient created by glucose or icodextrin in the fluid | Glucose draws water osmotically. The gradient fades as glucose is absorbed. |  |
| 9 | A woman on peritoneal dialysis arrives for backup hemodialysis. She has abdominal pain and her drained bag is cloudy. What do you do? | Keep the bag, check vital signs, follow the unit protocol, and call the attending nephrologist | Cloudy effluent with pain suggests peritonitis. Treatment is directed by the nephrologist and the PD team. |  |
| 10 | A PD program records 4 episodes of peritonitis over 120 patient-months. What is its peritonitis rate? | 0.40 episodes per patient-year | Patient-years at risk are 120 ÷ 12 = 10. The rate is 4 ÷ 10 = 0.40, at the ISPD target of no more than 0.40. |  |
| 11 | What did the 2025 Cochrane review conclude about conservative kidney management compared with dialysis in older people? | Effects on death and quality of life were uncertain, with very low certainty evidence from non-randomized studies | No randomized trials exist, and prognostic imbalances made the certainty very low. |  |
| 12 | In the Dutch cohort of patients aged 70 or older, what was found in those aged 80 or older? | The survival difference was smaller and not statistically significant | Median survival was 1.4 versus 2.1 years (P=0.08) in a retrospective single-center study. |  |
| 13 | An 85-year-old man in the unit for his wife’s appointment has an eGFR of 7 mL/min/1.73 m². His chart shows a documented conservative plan and an advance care plan. His son says nobody offered dialysis. What is the best response? | Recognize a planned path, avoid suggesting an error, and ask the attending nephrologist to speak with the family | A documented conservative plan is a choice. The family’s question goes to the nephrologist. |  |
| 14 | A man with a failed transplant is now on hemodialysis. A nurse asks whether to stop his tacrolimus because the graft no longer works. What do you say? | Do not change it. Ask the attending nephrologist and the transplant team | Stopping risks rejection of the old graft, and continuing raises infection risk. The transplant team decides. |  |
| 15 | A transplant recipient dialyzing temporarily has a temperature of 38.2 °C, tenderness over the graft, and less urine than yesterday. Which statement is correct? | Call the attending nephrologist at once and ask that the transplant team be informed | Graft tenderness and falling urine raise concern for infection or rejection. |  |

## Module 28: Outside the Unit: Surgery, Contrast, Hospital Stays, and Travel

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a long fast before surgery raise the potassium of a dialysis patient? | Insulin falls, so less potassium is driven into cells | With little insulin during a fast, less potassium moves into cells, and no kidney removes it. |  |
| 2 | In the meta-analysis by Palamuthusingam and colleagues, what happened to the excess postoperative mortality of dialysis patients after adjustment for age and other illness? | It was reduced but persisted | Adjustment attenuated the excess, but it remained in every surgical field. |  |
| 3 | A patient on Monday-Wednesday-Friday dialysis is booked for elective surgery on Monday at 8 AM. What is the main concern about the timing? | The operation falls at the end of the longest gap, when potassium and fluid peak | Potassium and fluid peak after the weekend gap. The nephrologist decides on any session change. |  |
| 4 | In the Medicare study of dialysis timing before surgery, what was the 90-day mortality when the last session was 3 days before surgery, compared with 1 day? | 5.2% compared with 4.2% | The absolute difference was 1.0 percentage point. The study was observational, and the authors warned of residual confounding. |  |
| 5 | Using the published absolute risk difference of 1.0 percentage point, about how many procedures done 3 days rather than 1 day after dialysis go with one additional death in that association? | 100 | 1 divided by 0.010 is 100. This is a number needed to harm for an association, not a proven effect of moving a session. |  |
| 6 | A surgeon asks you to add a heparin-free session tonight before a morning operation. A chair is free. What do you do? | Gather the facts and call the attending nephrologist, who decides on the session and the heparin | Both are changes to the prescription. Your facts make the decision quick. |  |
| 7 | An emergency physician asks whether an anuric patient on maintenance hemodialysis needs dialysis right after a CT scan with iodinated contrast. What is the best answer? | No emergency session is needed for the contrast alone, and the scan should not be delayed | Keep the usual schedule. Delaying a needed scan can cause more harm. |  |
| 8 | The ACR and NKF consensus statement advises IV saline prophylaxis before iodinated contrast in which group? | Patients with acute kidney injury or eGFR below 30 who are not on maintenance dialysis | The advice is for patients not on dialysis. It is a consensus statement, not a graded recommendation. |  |
| 9 | A woman on dialysis still passes about 500 mL of urine a day and is scheduled for a contrast CT. What should the physician on duty do? | Tell the attending nephrologist, who weighs the scan against her residual function | Pooled data show no significant loss, from small studies. The nephrologist weighs it. |  |
| 10 | What does the 2021 ACR and NKF statement say about dialysis after a group II gadolinium-based agent? | Starting or changing dialysis is likely unnecessary | Zero NSF cases were found after 4,931 doses in patients with eGFR below 30. It is a consensus statement. |  |
| 11 | A ward team plans a PICC in the right arm of a patient whose left arm fistula works and whose right arm was mapped for a future access. What do you do? | Ask them to hold and discuss the route with the attending nephrologist, and write an access-protection note | A prior PICC is associated with lack of a working fistula. KDOQI considers vein preservation reasonable (Expert Opinion). |  |
| 12 | In the case-control study by El Ters and colleagues, what was the adjusted association between a prior PICC and lack of a working fistula? | OR 2.8, an association that cannot prove cause | The adjusted odds ratio was 2.8 (1.5 to 5.5). The study was retrospective and observational. |  |
| 13 | An orthopedic resident asks you to write “cleared for surgery” for a dialysis patient. What is the best response? | Write a facts-only peri-procedure note with the questions for the attending nephrologist and the nephrologist’s contact | The surgical team and the nephrologist decide fitness. You supply the facts. |  |
| 14 | A patient returns from two weeks of dialysis in a unit abroad with weak infection control. What does the evidence from United Kingdom units suggest? | New hepatitis C infection can occur, and early infection may show RNA before antibody | In one study, 4 of 153 travel episodes led to new hepatitis C. Test per your unit’s protocol. |  |
| 15 | A woman comes for her first session after a week in hospital. She weighs 3 kg less than her old dry weight and has new medicines but no discharge summary. What comes first? | Obtain the discharge information and call the attending nephrologist before the fluid goal is set | The nephrologist sets the dry weight and medicines. You bring the facts. |  |

## Module 29: A Clean Unit: Hepatitis, HIV, Tuberculosis, and Vaccines

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the outbreaks described, how did hepatitis C usually spread? | Hands, gloves, shared items, and contaminated surfaces | Cases clustered at neighboring stations, and a shared machine explained only 1 of 11 cases in Vietnam. |  |
| 2 | Why does hepatitis B spread more easily at a dialysis station than HIV? | Hepatitis B reaches very high levels in blood and survives for days on surfaces | CDC describes very high blood levels and survival for at least 7 days on surfaces. |  |
| 3 | Under the CDC recommendations, which arrangement is correct for a patient positive for HBsAg? | A separate room and dedicated machine, with staff who do not care for susceptible patients at the same time | The room and equipment rule comes with a staff rule. |  |
| 4 | What does KDIGO 2022 recommend about dedicated machines for patients with hepatitis C? | Not using dedicated machines (1D) | KDIGO relies on standard infection control. Grade D means very low certainty, and some units keep stricter rules. |  |
| 5 | A new patient’s panel shows HBsAg negative, anti-HBs 150 mIU/mL, and anti-HBc negative. What does it mean? | Immune by vaccination | Anti-HBs without anti-HBc is the footprint of the vaccine. |  |
| 6 | After a month of dialysis abroad, a patient is anti-HCV negative and HCV RNA positive. What is most likely? | A recent infection in the window before antibody appears | Antibody appears weeks after infection, so this pattern marks new infection. |  |
| 7 | A patient has positive anti-HBc with negative HBsAg and negative anti-HBs. What does CDC advise? | Retest, because isolated anti-HBc is often a false positive; isolation is not needed | Most isolated anti-HBc results are false positives, and HBsAg is not detectable. |  |
| 8 | A unit follows 60 hepatitis C-negative patients for 5 months and finds 1 new conversion. What is the rate per 100 patient-months? | 0.33 | Patient-months = 60 × 5 = 300. Rate = 1 ÷ 300 × 100 = 0.33. |  |
| 9 | At 9:00 a.m., a patient at hour one in the general area has a new reactive HBsAg. What comes first? | Call the attending nephrologist and separate her nurse from the susceptible neighbors | Act as if the result is true until confirmed; disclosure happens in private. |  |
| 10 | Why can a dialysis patient with active tuberculosis have a negative interferon-gamma release assay? | The test measures a T-cell response that kidney failure weakens | Both tests depend on T-cell memory, blunted in kidney failure. |  |
| 11 | A man arrives with three weeks of cough, night sweats, and weight loss. What do you do first? | Mask and separate him, and call the attending nephrologist before he is placed on the floor | These features suggest active tuberculosis; the nephrologist and Medical Director decide where he dialyzes. |  |
| 12 | In 3,509 vaccine-naive dialysis patients, what share reached protective anti-HBs after a four-dose recombinant series? | About 63% | It was 62.9%, which is why the antibody is checked after every series. |  |
| 13 | What is the main limitation of the registry linking pneumococcal and influenza vaccination to lower mortality? | It was observational, so healthier patients may have been more likely to be vaccinated | A healthy-vaccinee bias can make a vaccine look more protective than it is. |  |
| 14 | A nurse with documented anti-HBs of 90 mIU/mL is stuck by a needle from an HBsAg-positive patient. What hepatitis B management is needed? | None for hepatitis B, although the exposure is still reported and managed for other viruses | Documented protection means no hepatitis B postexposure management. The HIV and hepatitis C pathway still applies. |  |
| 15 | A nurse is splashed in the eye with blood during disconnection. What is the correct first sequence? | Flush the eye, report at once, gather both serology results, and get the nurse to the designated clinician | A mucous-membrane splash is an exposure. The 2025 guideline stresses prompt reporting and expert consultation. |  |

## Module 30: When the Power or Water Fails

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a missed session carry more danger than its length suggests? | Potassium can reach a dangerous level before the patient feels breathless | Potassium can disturb the rhythm while the patient still looks well. |  |
| 2 | What did the Hurricane Sandy study of early dialysis show? | An association with lower odds of admission and death, in retrospective claims data | The study could not tell who was offered early dialysis and declined, so healthier patients may have come early. |  |
| 3 | The power fails at 2:15 p.m. with ten patients on the machines. What do you do first? | Check every patient’s airway, consciousness, and access | Patients come before machines. Generator status is the second question. |  |
| 4 | Why does the blood become harder to return the longer the power is off? | Standing blood in the tubing and dialyzer begins to clot | Moving blood resists clotting. A clotted circuit is not returned. |  |
| 5 | During manual blood return without power, what replaces the venous air detector? | A staff member watching the venous drip chamber and line until the clamp is closed | Without power the detector and clamp may not act. The operator’s eyes do their job. |  |
| 6 | Mrs. Cruz’s session is being ended by hand after a power failure. The nurse sees foam in the venous line. What is right? | Clamp the lines and do not return the blood | Air in the venous line means no return. Clamp and keep the circuit. |  |
| 7 | Smoke enters the treatment area and the incident lead orders immediate evacuation. How are patients disconnected? | By the unit’s clamp-and-cut method, without returning the blood | When the building must be left at once, there is no time to return the blood. |  |
| 8 | Two patients in one room report back pain and chest tightness at 9:20 a.m.; one patient’s plasma looks pink. Both share a water loop. What do you do? | Stop the affected treatments, do not return the blood, keep the circuits, and call the nephrologist and the Medical Director | This is a water event until proven otherwise. Possible hemolysis means no return. |  |
| 9 | Why can a substance harmless in drinking water poison a dialysis patient? | It crosses the dialyzer membrane straight into the blood, and the failed kidney cannot clear it | The patient meets a very large volume of fluid each session, separated from the blood by a thin membrane. |  |
| 10 | Over two weeks, hemoglobin falls in many patients in one unit, with no change in medicines. What should you consider? | Chloramine reaching the dialysis fluid from exhausted carbon | Anemia across a unit is a pattern. A chloramine outbreak showed exactly this. |  |
| 11 | A monthly water test fails. Under Department of Health Administrative Order 2013-0003, what must the facility do? | Temporarily stop operations until a repeat test complies, disinfect the product water loop, and notify patients | The order requires a temporary stop, disinfection, and notification of patients. |  |
| 12 | A water laboratory report shows a bacterial count of 150 CFU/mL in product water. How does it read? | It meets the DOH regulatory limit of less than 200 CFU/mL, but not the stricter PSN target if the unit’s water policy has adopted it | The DOH order sets the regulatory limit at less than 200 CFU/mL, and testing laboratories report against it. The PSN standard gives a stricter target of less than 100 CFU/mL, which a unit may adopt in its water policy; the physician on duty does not choose. |  |
| 13 | Only half the chairs can run tomorrow. Mr. Lim has no urine, last dialyzed four days ago, and had a high potassium. Mrs. Uy dialyzed two days ago and passes urine. What is best? | Report both to the nephrologist; under a priority list Mr. Lim usually comes first | The longest gap, no residual function, and high potassium usually come first. The list and the nephrologist set the order. |  |
| 14 | A patient kept away by a flood asks what to eat until the unit reopens. What do you give? | The unit’s approved emergency diet and medicine advice, and a call to the nephrologist if breathless or weak | Numeric limits come from the unit dietitian and the nephrologist. |  |
| 15 | Why does a unit review its response after a typhoon closure? | Reviewing each response is how the next one goes better | Disaster reviews name the after-event review as a core step that feeds back into the plan. |  |

## Module 31: Rules, Records, and Quality

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which list matches what DOH AO 2012-0001 expects of a physician on duty? | Present during hours of operation, PRC-licensed with a listed credential, experienced with end-stage kidney disease, ACLS updated, and supervised by the Medical Director | These are the order’s requirements. |  |
| 2 | The PSN standard and the DOH order differ on a staffing point. How should the unit handle the difference? | Treat the DOH order as the legal floor and let the Medical Director set the unit’s rule within it | The order is law. The standard is good practice. The Medical Director sets the unit’s rule, and it cannot fall below the order. |  |
| 3 | At 6:30 p.m., a nurse hands you a medical certificate stating that a patient “completed dialysis today.” The patient left at hour two. What do you do? | Write a certificate that states the true times of attendance, or refer it per unit policy | Every statement on a certificate must be true. |  |
| 4 | In Li v. Spouses Soliman, which element must a patient prove in an informed-consent claim? | That the patient would not have consented had the material risks been disclosed | Duty to disclose, failure to disclose, consent otherwise not given, and injury. |  |
| 5 | Mr. Reyes, alert and oriented, wants to stop his session at hour one to attend a wake. His potassium this week was high. What comes first after you explain the risks? | Ask his reason and look for a way to meet it, then call the attending nephrologist | A competent adult may refuse. The reason is often solvable. |  |
| 6 | Which refusal note best holds up? | “5:10 p.m. Explained risk of dangerous heart rhythm from high potassium. Patient: ‘My ride is leaving.’ Witnessed by two staff. Attending nephrologist called 5:15 p.m.; advised session tomorrow.” | It has the time, explanation, the patient’s words, witnesses, call, and plan. |  |
| 7 | A nurse posts a photo of a treatment sheet with the patient’s name in a staff group chat that includes a supplier. What do you do? | Report it to the data protection officer and the Medical Director at once | Report at once so the breach team has its full time to decide on notification. |  |
| 8 | Under the Data Privacy Act, how is a dialysis patient’s hepatitis B status classed? | Sensitive personal information | Health information is sensitive personal information. |  |
| 9 | You forgot to write a note about a 2:15 p.m. episode and remember at 5:00 p.m. What is correct? | Write it now, labeled as a late entry with the current time | Inserting it to look contemporaneous makes the record untrue. |  |
| 10 | A unit of 50 patients had 2 bloodstream infections in one month. What is the rate per 100 patient-months? | 4.0 | 2 ÷ 50 × 100 = 4.0. |  |
| 11 | Monthly catheter percentages for 12 months show the last six points all below the median. What does this mean? | A shift: a signal that something changed, which the team should explain | Six or more consecutive points on one side of the median is a shift. |  |
| 12 | A unit’s standardized hospitalization ratio is 1.2. Which statement is correct? | Admissions were 20% above the number expected for this case mix, and the confidence interval decides if this is a signal | Observed divided by expected. The interval decides. |  |
| 13 | What did the study of the U.S. End-Stage Renal Disease Quality Incentive Program show? | Penalization was not associated with improvement in total performance scores | No association was found across 5,830 centers. |  |
| 14 | After a medication omission, a review recommends “remind staff to check orders.” Why do critics call this a weak fix? | It relies on people remembering and leaves the system that allowed the error unchanged | Reviews often settle for administrative fixes instead of changing system causes. |  |
| 15 | A team wants to cut catheter infections. Which plan best fits a well-run PDSA cycle? | Test one change on one shift, measure adherence and the monthly rate, and decide at the next CQI meeting | Small tests and data over time are what published PDSA projects most often lack. |  |

## Module 32: Putting It Together: Bedside Skills and a Simulated Shift

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In a descriptive report from four hemodialysis units, which medication error was most common? | Omission of an ordered medicine | Omission accounted for 24 of 35 medication errors. |  |
| 2 | At 2 a.m. you order a medicine across the floor during an emergency. What makes this verbal order safe? | The nurse reads it back, and you write it in the chart as soon as the emergency allows | Read-back catches mishearing; writing makes it part of the record. |  |
| 3 | Why should “units” be written in full on an order? | A handwritten U can be read as a zero, multiplying the dose | A misread U turns a dose into ten times the intended amount. |  |
| 4 | A nurse asks you to lower a patient’s fluid goal for the rest of the session because his pressure keeps falling. What do you do? | Follow the unit’s protocol for the falling pressure and call the attending nephrologist about the goal | The fluid goal belongs to the prescription. |  |
| 5 | What did the I-PASS handoff study by Starmer and colleagues show? | A handoff program was associated with fewer medical errors and preventable adverse events, without longer handoffs | Errors fell from 24.5 to 18.8 per 100 admissions, in a before-after design. |  |
| 6 | A patient with a left arm fistula is transferred for chest pain. Which letter item most directly protects the access? | The arm to protect, with no cuffs, needles, or lines on it | A blood pressure cuff or an intravenous line on the fistula arm can damage or clot the access. |  |
| 7 | A patient collapses in cardiac arrest at the chair. When do you call the attending nephrologist? | During the first steps, through a team member who calls while you lead the resuscitation | Calling yourself first would delay compressions. Module 15 has a team member call the attending nephrologist while you lead. |  |
| 8 | What is a Critical step on a station scoring sheet? | A step that must be done for the station to pass, whatever the total score | Critical steps are gates, not points. |  |
| 9 | A learner completes 11 of 12 steps (92%) at the hypotension station but does not screen for a dangerous cause, a Critical step. The unit’s standard is 80%. What is the result? | Repeat the station | A missed Critical step means the station is not passed, whatever the percentage. |  |
| 10 | What did the meta-analysis by Cook and colleagues find for simulation compared with no training? | Large effects on knowledge and skills, and a moderate effect on patient-related outcomes | Pooled effect sizes were about 1.1 to 1.2 for knowledge and skills and 0.50 for patient outcomes. |  |
| 11 | What is the main limitation of the study by Barsuk and colleagues on central line infections? | It was a before-and-after study at one hospital, so other changes could have contributed | The 74% reduction is an association within one site, not proof of cause. |  |
| 12 | Four events arrive together: a coughing patient with foam in the venous line, a falling pressure, a feverish catheter patient, and a stable patient who wants to leave. Where do you go first? | The coughing patient with foam in the venous line | Possible air embolism is an immediate threat to life. |  |
| 13 | In the same shift, for which patient must the blood not be returned? | The patient with possible air in the venous line | Returning blood from a line that may contain air could send more air into the patient. |  |
| 14 | A colleague scores 95% on the written test. What does this show? | That the colleague has studied the material, not that the colleague can perform under pressure | Entrustment is a separate judgment by people who watched the work. |  |
| 15 | An in situ practice code in your unit finds that the suction unit on the emergency cart does not work. How should you view this? | As a success: a hidden hazard found before a real patient needed the equipment | Finding latent safety threats is one purpose of in situ simulation. |  |

# Renal Dietitian

## Module 1: The Renal Dietitian&rsquo;s Role and the First Consult

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | An eGFR of 34 mL/min/1.73 m² with a UACR of 420 mg/g, both confirmed over four months, is classified as: | G3b A3 | An eGFR of 30 to 44 is G3b, and a UACR above 300 mg/g is A3. |  |
| 2 | What establishes that kidney disease is chronic? | Abnormal results present for at least three months | Chronicity requires abnormality of structure or function for at least three months. One result may reflect a temporary problem. |  |
| 3 | A patient has an eGFR of 75 and a normal urine test and ultrasound. Does she have CKD? | No; G1 and G2 need another marker of kidney damage | G1 and G2 are CKD only when a marker of kidney damage, such as albuminuria, is also present. |  |
| 4 | Why can a frail, thin patient have a misleadingly good creatinine-based eGFR? | Creatinine comes from muscle, and low muscle mass lowers serum creatinine | Creatinine is produced by muscle. With little muscle, serum creatinine is low and the formula overestimates kidney function. |  |
| 5 | During a call, a patient reports new palpitations, and the laboratory flags her potassium as critical. What is the first action? | Activate the urgent pathway and call the responsible physician | A critical result with symptoms needs immediate medical assessment. Diet teaching comes after stabilization. |  |
| 6 | Which referral should be seen within days, not at the next routine clinic? | A patient who started hemodialysis last week and is eating half his usual intake | A new dialysis start with falling intake is a high-risk transition and should be expedited. |  |
| 7 | A patient asks whether she can take two phosphate binder tablets with a large meal. The prescription says one. The dietitian should: | Reinforce the prescribed dose and ask the prescriber about adjusting it | Changing a dose belongs to the prescriber. The dietitian reports the question and reinforces the written instruction. |  |
| 8 | In SBAR, where does “spironolactone was added last month” belong? | Background | Background holds the relevant history, including recent medicine changes. |  |
| 9 | A handoff is “closed” when: | The receiver has repeated the message and accepted responsibility | Closing the loop means read-back and acceptance. A message nobody acknowledged has not been delivered. |  |
| 10 | Which document requires Philippine hospitals to record nutrition care as assessment, diagnosis, intervention, and monitoring? | Department of Health Administrative Order No. 2019-0033 | The Department of Health order sets out the Nutrition Care Process in hospitals. |  |
| 11 | Why did guidelines move away from fixed lists of forbidden foods? | Blanket restriction lowered intake, and many abnormal results had causes other than food | Restriction for everyone risks wasting, and non-diet causes are common. KDOQI 2020 recommends adjusting intake to the individual’s serum levels. |  |
| 12 | Which item is NOT one of the six things to collect before a first consult? | The patient’s favorite restaurant | The six are the referral question, stage or modality, recent admissions, weight and intake trend, key results with dates, and medicines. |  |
| 13 | The UACR mainly tells you: | How leaky the kidney filter is | Albuminuria reflects damage to the filtering barrier and predicts progression and cardiovascular risk. |  |
| 14 | You cannot reach the attending physician about a critical result. What next? | Call the next person on the unit’s escalation list | The escalation list exists for this. Move to the next contact within the time your policy sets. |  |
| 15 | After an urgent handoff, what should the note contain? | Value and units, time, symptoms, recipient, directions, and acceptance | The record must let another clinician see what was found, who was told, and who took responsibility. |  |

## Module 2: How the Kidney Handles Nutrients

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which nutrient is the source of urea? | Protein | Urea is made by the liver from the nitrogen in amino acids. |  |
| 2 | A patient with CKD eats less for two weeks, and his urea rises. The most likely explanation is: | His own muscle is being broken down, or there is another source such as bleeding | With low intake, a rising urea points to catabolism or another nitrogen source. |  |
| 3 | After a salty meal, what does the body defend first? | The concentration of sodium in the blood | Thirst and antidiuretic hormone restore concentration by retaining water, which expands volume. |  |
| 4 | Why does telling a hemodialysis patient to “drink less” often fail on its own? | Thirst is driven by sodium intake, so salt must be reduced first | Sodium raises the blood concentration and triggers thirst. Lowering sodium lowers the drive to drink. |  |
| 5 | In the Cochrane review of salt reduction in CKD, the blood pressure fell by about: | 7/4 mm Hg | Lowering sodium by about 1,700 mg a day reduced blood pressure by about 7/4 mm Hg; trials were short. |  |
| 6 | About how much of the body’s potassium is inside cells? | 98% | Nearly all potassium is intracellular, so small shifts change the blood level. |  |
| 7 | Which hormone moves potassium into cells within minutes of a meal? | Insulin | Insulin, with adrenaline, drives potassium into cells. Fasting and insulin deficiency weaken this. |  |
| 8 | Which of these lowers kidney potassium excretion? | Spironolactone | Spironolactone blocks aldosterone, which the kidney needs to excrete potassium. |  |
| 9 | Why does constipation matter for potassium in advanced CKD? | The colon excretes a growing share of potassium as kidney function falls | Colonic excretion becomes more important as eGFR falls, and constipation reduces it. |  |
| 10 | Which source of phosphorus is absorbed most completely? | Inorganic phosphate additives | Additives are nearly fully absorbed; plant phytate is the least available. |  |
| 11 | In early CKD, serum phosphorus is usually normal because: | FGF23 and PTH rise and force the remaining nephrons to excrete more | Hormonal compensation keeps the level normal for years, at the cost of high PTH and low active vitamin D. |  |
| 12 | Which foods add the most acid load? | Animal protein and grains | Animal protein and grains produce acid; fruits and vegetables supply alkali. |  |
| 13 | Chronic metabolic acidosis in CKD contributes to: | Muscle and bone loss | Acid is buffered by bone and promotes muscle protein breakdown. |  |
| 14 | The Maroni formula estimates protein intake from: | 24-hour urine urea nitrogen and body weight | It uses urine urea nitrogen plus a weight-based estimate of non-urea nitrogen, in a stable patient. |  |
| 15 | In a large cohort on hemodialysis, higher reported dietary potassium was: | Not associated with serum potassium or death | The association was null; the study was observational, so it informs but does not settle practice. |  |

## Module 3: Nutrition Assessment, Diagnosis, and Monitoring

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A patient’s post-dialysis weight was 62 kg six weeks ago and is 58 kg now. Today’s pre-dialysis weight is 61 kg. The percent loss is: | 6.5% | Compare post-dialysis with post-dialysis: (62 − 58) ÷ 62 × 100 = 6.5%. |  |
| 2 | Why is the pre-dialysis weight a poor measure of tissue change? | It includes fluid gained since the last session | Interdialytic fluid can add several kilograms and hide tissue loss. |  |
| 3 | A stable weight with rising blood pressure and new ankle edema may mean: | Tissue loss replaced by fluid; the dry weight may need review | Fluid can replace lost tissue at the same scale weight. Report it to the nephrologist. |  |
| 4 | Which intake method does KDOQI prefer for estimating energy and protein intake? | A three-day food record | A three-day record, including a dialysis day and a non-dialysis day, reduces day-to-day error. |  |
| 5 | The nPCR is unreliable as a measure of protein intake when the patient is: | Catabolic or anabolic | Urea then reflects tissue breakdown or building, not intake alone. |  |
| 6 | Which is NOT a cause of low serum albumin? | High handgrip strength | Albumin falls with inflammation, dilution, losses, and liver disease. |  |
| 7 | Which tool does KDOQI firmly recommend (grade 1B) for assessing nutritional status in adults on dialysis? | 7-point Subjective Global Assessment | The 7-point SGA is recommended as valid and reliable in CKD stage 5 on dialysis. |  |
| 8 | On which arm should handgrip be measured in a hemodialysis patient with a fistula? | The arm without the fistula | Protect the access arm; measure the other side the same way each time. |  |
| 9 | How many of the four ISRNM categories must be met to diagnose PEW? | Three | At least one item in three of the four categories. |  |
| 10 | The GLIM diagnosis requires: | One phenotypic and one etiologic criterion | GLIM combines a phenotype (weight loss, low body mass index, or low muscle) with a cause (reduced intake or inflammation). |  |
| 11 | Which etiology makes a PES statement actionable? | Related to pain on chewing | The etiology should be something the nutrition plan can change. |  |
| 12 | Unintentional weight loss that meets the ISRNM body-mass criterion is: | 5% in 3 months or 10% in 6 months | These thresholds are part of the PEW body-mass category. |  |
| 13 | A small-framed, well, active patient has a stable body mass index of 21. On the PEW body mass index item alone you should: | Not diagnose PEW; one item in one category is not enough, and the cut-off came from other populations | PEW needs three categories. The 23 kg/m² cut-off is applied with judgment in small-framed adults. |  |
| 14 | Intake has halved and weight has fallen 6% in six weeks. Besides starting a plan, you should: | Report it to the nephrologist and nurse the same day | Combined intake decline and weight loss needs prompt team review. |  |
| 15 | A good monitoring plan states: | One measure, one date, and who is responsible | A plan another person could carry out names the measure, the time, and the owner. |  |

## Module 4: Energy, Protein, and Eating Patterns in Stable Chronic Kidney Disease

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Under KDIGO 2024, the suggested protein intake for an adult with CKD G4 who weighs 60 kg is: | 48 g/day | 0.8 g/kg/day × 60 kg = 48 g/day (a weak suggestion, grade 2C). |  |
| 2 | Under KDOQI 2020, the low-protein range for a metabolically stable 60 kg adult with CKD stage 4 without diabetes is: | 33 to 36 g/day | 0.55 to 0.60 g/kg/day × 60 kg = 33 to 36 g/day, under close clinical supervision (a firm recommendation, grade 1A). |  |
| 3 | The KDOQI energy recommendation for metabolically stable adults with CKD is: | 25 to 35 kcal/kg/day | 25 to 35 kcal/kg/day, individualized (a firm recommendation, grade 1C). |  |
| 4 | Why must energy be adequate before protein is lowered? | Without enough energy, dietary and body protein are used as fuel | Protein is spared for tissue maintenance only when energy needs are met. |  |
| 5 | Which patient should NOT be offered a low-protein diet? | A 79-year-old with G3b CKD, weight loss, and slow gait | Frailty with weight loss calls for protecting intake; KDIGO suggests considering higher targets in such patients. |  |
| 6 | In the ketoanalogue trial by Garneata and colleagues, what limits applying the result widely? | Only 14% of screened patients were randomized after a run-in selecting adherent, well-nourished people | The trial shows what is achievable in highly selected patients. |  |
| 7 | The Cochrane review of low-protein diets in adults without diabetes concluded that: | Very low-protein diets probably reduce progression to kidney failure; effects on death are probably nil | The benefit was seen for very low-protein diets in stages 4 to 5; mortality was probably not affected. |  |
| 8 | An observational study finds that older adults with CKD who eat more protein die less often. The best reading is: | The association may be partly explained by healthier people eating more; it supports not restricting frail older adults | Association is not causation; confounding by health status is likely. |  |
| 9 | “Metabolically stable” excludes a patient who: | Was discharged from hospital five days ago after pneumonia | A recent hospital stay for acute illness means the patient is not stable. |  |
| 10 | KDIGO’s advice to avoid more than 1.3 g/kg/day in adults at risk of progression is a: | Practice point without a formal grade | It is an ungraded practice point. |  |
| 11 | One cup of cooked rice provides about how much protein? | 4 g | About 4 g per cup; three cups add about 12 g a day. |  |
| 12 | Which first change addresses protein, sodium, and phosphate additives together? | Replacing processed meats with fresh fish, egg, or tofu | Processed meats carry all three; fresh sources lower them without lowering energy. |  |
| 13 | A patient on a supervised low-protein diet develops pneumonia and eats poorly. You should: | Stop the restriction, return to at least 0.8 g/kg/day, and inform the nephrologist | Restriction is for stable patients; illness raises needs and the risk of wasting. |  |
| 14 | The plant-dominant low-protein diet is best described as: | An idea built on physiology, not yet tested in an outcome trial | It rests on physiology; outcome trials have not yet been done. |  |
| 15 | For an adult with diabetes and CKD not on dialysis, the American Diabetes Association and KDIGO Diabetes guideline advise aiming for: | 0.8 g/kg/day | Both advise 0.8 g/kg/day; lower intakes are not recommended in diabetes. |  |

## Module 5: Sodium, Fluid, and Volume

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A label lists 720 mg of sodium per serving and two servings per packet. A patient eats the whole packet. Sodium eaten is: | 1,440 mg | Two servings × 720 mg = 1,440 mg. |  |
| 2 | 2 g of sodium is about how much salt? | 5 g | Sodium × 2.5 = salt; 2 g of sodium is about 5 g of salt, one level teaspoon. |  |
| 3 | KDIGO 2024 suggests a sodium intake for people with CKD of: | Less than 2 g/day | Less than 2 g of sodium a day (a weak suggestion, grade 2C). |  |
| 4 | The KDOQI 2020 sodium statement is: | Less than 2.3 g/day | Less than 2.3 g (100 mmol) a day; grade 1B in CKD stages 3 to 5 and 1C on dialysis. |  |
| 5 | What did the Cochrane review of salt reduction in CKD establish with high certainty? | Lower blood pressure | Blood pressure fell by about 7/4 mm Hg. Trials were too short for hard outcomes. |  |
| 6 | Pre-dialysis weight 66.5 kg; previous post-dialysis weight 64.0 kg. The gain is: | 2.5 kg (3.9%) | 66.5 − 64.0 = 2.5 kg; 2.5 ÷ 64.0 × 100 = 3.9%. |  |
| 7 | Why should sodium be taught before fluid? | Sodium drives thirst; lowering it makes a fluid limit achievable | Thirst follows sodium intake. |  |
| 8 | A patient on twice-weekly dialysis has the same daily intake as one on thrice-weekly dialysis. His weight gain per interval will be: | Larger, because the interval is longer | Gain accumulates over more days. |  |
| 9 | A salt substitute lists potassium chloride. For a patient with CKD G4 on losartan, you should: | Not recommend it; refer the question to the nephrologist | Potassium chloride is fully absorbed, and the trial evidence excluded people with serious kidney disease. |  |
| 10 | The large salt-substitute trial showed fewer strokes in: | Older adults with hypertension or prior stroke, excluding serious kidney disease | The benefit applies to the population studied, which excluded those at risk of hyperkalemia. |  |
| 11 | Which patient most likely does NOT need a fluid limit? | CKD G3a with normal urine output and no edema | Patients who pass urine normally and have no fluid retention should drink to thirst. |  |
| 12 | Very low weight gains with falling post-dialysis weight suggest: | Poor food intake | Little food means little sodium and fluid; check the food record. |  |
| 13 | Which counts toward a fluid allowance? | Lugaw and soup | Liquids and foods that are liquid at room temperature count. |  |
| 14 | Which finding makes a visit urgent? | New breathlessness at rest | Resting breathlessness may be pulmonary edema and needs immediate assessment. |  |
| 15 | Who sets the dry weight and the fluid prescription? | The nephrologist | The dietitian contributes intake and weight data; the nephrologist decides. |  |

## Module 6: Potassium, Acid&ndash;Base Balance, and Fiber

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A stable patient has a mildly high potassium on two checks. What comes before changing whole-food intake? | Review of sample quality, medicines, bowel habit, acidosis, glucose, dialysis, and concentrated sources | Hyperkalemia is usually multifactorial, and diet is seldom the only cause. |  |
| 2 | Which ingredient most deserves attention on a “low-sodium salt” label? | Potassium chloride | Potassium chloride is absorbed almost completely. |  |
| 3 | Why is a glass of buko juice a different load from a serving of vegetables with similar potassium? | It is absorbed quickly and without fiber | Liquids deliver potassium rapidly; whole foods bring fiber and alkali. |  |
| 4 | A hemolyzed blood sample can cause: | Falsely high potassium | Potassium leaks from broken red cells: pseudohyperkalemia. |  |
| 5 | Which over-the-counter medicine can raise potassium and harm kidney function? | Ibuprofen | Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs reduce kidney potassium excretion. |  |
| 6 | A patient asks whether to stop her angiotensin receptor blocker because of potassium. You should: | Explain that the physician decides, and report the other causes you found | RAAS inhibitors are protective; the dietitian helps remove other causes. |  |
| 7 | How does constipation raise potassium in advanced CKD? | It reduces colonic potassium excretion | The colon is an important exit route when eGFR is low. |  |
| 8 | KDOQI’s statement on dietary potassium is: | Adjust intake to maintain serum potassium in the normal range (expert opinion) | It is an opinion-based statement that calls for individualization. |  |
| 9 | In the trial of fruits and vegetables for acidosis in stage 4 CKD, potassium did not rise. An important limit is that: | Patients were selected for low hyperkalemia risk | The result applies to selected, monitored patients. |  |
| 10 | KDIGO suggests considering treatment to prevent acidosis with clinical consequences at a bicarbonate below about: | 18 mmol/L | A practice point gives below 18 mmol/L in adults as an example. |  |
| 11 | Which change is most likely to lower potassium at the least nutritional cost? | Stopping the salt substitute and juices | Removing concentrated, fully absorbed sources costs little nutritionally. |  |
| 12 | Boiling cut root crops in plenty of water and discarding the water: | Lowers potassium and also removes some vitamins | Leaching lowers potassium content, with loss of water-soluble vitamins. |  |
| 13 | In which patient is potassium restriction usually wrong? | Peritoneal dialysis with a potassium of 3.3 mmol/L | Low potassium is common in peritoneal dialysis and should be reported, not restricted. |  |
| 14 | An old potassium restriction should be lifted when: | Potassium is normal on repeated checks or a binder has been started | Restrictions should end when the reason ends. |  |
| 15 | The observational finding that dietary potassium was not associated with serum potassium on hemodialysis means: | Blanket bans lack outcome support, but individuals with repeated high values still need assessment | It weakens the case for universal restriction without proving intake is irrelevant. |  |

## Module 7: Phosphorus, Calcium, Vitamin D, and Bone

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why do most patients on hemodialysis need binders despite dialysis? | Weekly absorption usually exceeds what three sessions remove | A session removes on the order of 800 mg, less than typical absorption over the days between. |  |
| 2 | Which source of phosphorus is absorbed most completely? | Phosphate additives in processed food | Inorganic additives are about 90% absorbed or more. |  |
| 3 | Which ingredient indicates a phosphate additive? | Sodium tripolyphosphate | Look for “phos” in the ingredient list. |  |
| 4 | Which protein food has the lowest phosphorus-to-protein ratio? | Egg white | Egg white has about 1 to 2 mg of phosphorus per gram of protein. |  |
| 5 | A patient with low protein intake and high phosphorus should first: | Reduce additive-containing foods and drinks | Remove phosphorus that carries no protein; protect protein intake. |  |
| 6 | When should a phosphate binder be taken? | With food, as the prescription states | A binder acts on the food that is in the gut with it. |  |
| 7 | A patient asks to take an extra binder with a fiesta meal. You should: | Reinforce the prescription and refer the question to the prescriber | Dose changes belong to the prescriber. |  |
| 8 | KDIGO suggests that decisions about phosphate-lowering treatment be based on: | Progressively or persistently elevated phosphate, with calcium and PTH considered together | Serial measurements and the three values together guide treatment. |  |
| 9 | The HiLo trial showed that: | No inference about targets could be drawn; it stopped early with poor separation | Enrollment and phosphate separation were insufficient. |  |
| 10 | How much elemental calcium is in a 500 mg calcium carbonate tablet? | 200 mg | Calcium carbonate is 40% elemental calcium. |  |
| 11 | KDOQI suggests a total elemental calcium intake in CKD stages 3 to 4 (not on active vitamin D) of about: | 800 to 1,000 mg/day | 800 to 1,000 mg a day from all sources (a weak suggestion, grade 2B). |  |
| 12 | Which is a medicine for secondary hyperparathyroidism and not a nutritional supplement? | Calcitriol | Calcitriol is active vitamin D; it raises calcium and phosphorus absorption. |  |
| 13 | Phosphorus rises after a dose increase of an active vitamin D analog. The likely explanation is: | Increased intestinal phosphorus absorption from the medicine | Active vitamin D raises absorption of calcium and phosphorus. |  |
| 14 | Three sachets of 3-in-1 coffee a day matter for phosphorus because: | The creamer commonly contains phosphate salts | Non-dairy creamers often contain phosphate additives. |  |
| 15 | Boiling meat and discarding the liquid: | Lowers phosphorus somewhat while keeping most protein | Some phosphorus moves into the cooking liquid. |  |

## Module 8: Diabetes, Obesity, and Cardiometabolic Risk

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does the risk of hypoglycemia rise as CKD advances? | Insulin is cleared more slowly and the kidney releases less glucose | Slower insulin clearance and less kidney glucose release both lower glucose. |  |
| 2 | A patient received an erythropoiesis-stimulating agent two months ago. Her HbA1c may be: | Falsely low | Young red cells carry less glucose, so HbA1c reads low. |  |
| 3 | KDIGO’s HbA1c target in diabetes with CKD is: | Individualized from below 6.5% to below 8.0% | A firm recommendation (grade 1C) to individualize the target. |  |
| 4 | Which data best explain recurrent lows before dinner? | Time-stamped meter readings with meal and medicine times | Times link each low to a missed meal or a dose. |  |
| 5 | Two symptomatic lows on insulin this week. The dietitian should: | Tell the diabetes team the same day | Recurrent hypoglycemia is a safety issue for the prescribing team. |  |
| 6 | One cup of cooked rice contains about how much carbohydrate? | 40 to 45 g | About 40 to 45 g; keeping the portion steady helps with fixed insulin doses. |  |
| 7 | Why is a cola a poor routine drink but sometimes used to treat a low? | It is fast sugar, but it also carries phosphate additives and empty calories | Fast sugar treats a low; glucose tablets do so without the phosphorus. |  |
| 8 | An SGLT2 inhibitor causes glucose to be: | Passed in the urine | It blocks glucose reabsorption in the kidney. |  |
| 9 | Who gives sick-day instructions for pausing an SGLT2 inhibitor? | The prescriber | The dietitian checks that the patient knows the instruction exists. |  |
| 10 | In the FLOW trial, semaglutide reduced the main kidney and heart outcome by about: | 24% | Hazard ratio 0.76 over a median 3.4 years in 3,533 patients. |  |
| 11 | What should the dietitian watch for in a patient starting a GLP-1 receptor agonist? | A fall in intake below protein needs, nausea, and dehydration | Appetite can drop sharply; protein intake must be protected. |  |
| 12 | A patient cuts rice for glucose, meat for her kidneys, and fruit for potassium. The main risk is: | Too little energy and protein overall | Stacked restrictions can shrink intake below need. |  |
| 13 | Sarcopenic obesity means: | Excess fat with low muscle mass and strength | The scale can hide low muscle. |  |
| 14 | During weight loss in CKD, what should be tracked besides weight? | Grip strength or a chair-stand test | Function shows whether muscle is being kept. |  |
| 15 | Which change belongs to the diabetes team, not the dietitian? | Changing the insulin dose | Medicine doses belong to the prescriber. |  |

## Module 9: Medical Nutrition Therapy in Maintenance Hemodialysis

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The KDOQI protein advice for stable adults on HD is: | 1.0 to 1.2 g/kg/day | 1.0 to 1.2 g/kg/day, expert opinion. |  |
| 2 | For a stable 60 kg patient on HD, the protein range is: | 60 to 72 g/day | 1.0 to 1.2 × 60 = 60 to 72 g/day. |  |
| 3 | About how many grams of amino acids are lost in one HD session? | 6 to 12 g | About 6 to 12 g pass into the dialysis fluid each session. |  |
| 4 | Why is a dialysis day often the worst nutrition day? | Early travel, the session, and tiredness can remove one or two meals | Ask for a dialysis-day recall as well as a non-dialysis-day recall. |  |
| 5 | A patient started HD six weeks ago and still follows his 0.8 g/kg/day plan. The dietitian should: | Raise the target to 1.0 to 1.2 g/kg/day and explain why | A leftover pre-dialysis restriction is a common cause of early wasting. |  |
| 6 | An nPCR is a reliable estimate of protein intake when the patient is: | Metabolically stable | In unstable patients urea reflects tissue breakdown, not intake. |  |
| 7 | A main risk of eating during HD is: | A fall in blood pressure after eating in some patients | Post-meal hypotension is a reason for timing and selection, not a blanket ban. |  |
| 8 | When is eating during dialysis safest? | Early in the session, in patients without frequent low pressure | Blood pressure is usually lowest late in the session. |  |
| 9 | In the large U.S. supplement program, why does the result not prove the supplement saved lives? | It was observational; patients who kept taking it may have been healthier | Healthy-adherer effects can explain part of the association. |  |
| 10 | In the French IDPN trial, adding IDPN to oral supplements: | Did not change two-year survival | Survival and admissions did not differ. |  |
| 11 | The German IDPN trial showed improvement in: | Prealbumin, a laboratory marker | A marker improved; patient outcomes were not shown. |  |
| 12 | KDOQI suggests a trial of oral supplements, when counseling alone fails, for at least: | Three months | A minimum three-month trial, a weak suggestion. |  |
| 13 | Who decides whether a patient receives IDPN? | The nephrologist with the nutrition support team | The dietitian assesses and recommends; the team decides. |  |
| 14 | A patient on incremental twice-weekly HD reports much less urine. The diet may need to: | Tighten for potassium and fluid, with the nephrologist informed | Allowances depended on urine output. |  |
| 15 | Which finding should go to the nephrologist the same day? | Falling post-dialysis weight with poor intake | Weight loss with poor intake needs prompt team review. |  |

## Module 10: Medical Nutrition Therapy in Peritoneal Dialysis

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A PD patient absorbs 80 g of glucose a day. At 3.4 kcal/g, the energy from dialysate is: | 272 kcal | 80 × 3.4 = 272 kcal. |  |
| 2 | Total energy need is 1,800 kcal and dialysate supplies 272 kcal. Food should supply about: | 1,528 kcal | 1,800 − 272 = about 1,528 kcal. |  |
| 3 | Why do protein needs stay high on PD? | Protein is lost into the dialysis fluid every day | About 5 to 15 g a day, more with peritonitis. |  |
| 4 | What should happen first when a PD patient reports cloudy fluid? | Immediate contact with the PD team | Cloudy fluid is presumed peritonitis and needs urgent assessment. |  |
| 5 | Potassium on PD is often: | Low, because fluid without potassium removes it all day | Continuous removal makes low potassium common. |  |
| 6 | In the Thai trial, keeping potassium at 4 to 5 mmol/L in hypokalemic PD patients was associated with: | Fewer peritonitis episodes | Hazard ratio 0.47 for peritonitis in one open-label trial. |  |
| 7 | A PD patient with potassium 3.4 mmol/L asks whether to keep avoiding bananas. You should: | Report the result and lift the ban | Low potassium calls for more, not less, with the physician informed. |  |
| 8 | Which meters may falsely read high with icodextrin? | Those using glucose dehydrogenase with pyrroloquinoline quinone | Maltose is read as glucose by these meters. |  |
| 9 | Why is a falsely high glucose reading dangerous? | Insulin may be given for a high that does not exist, causing a severe low | Treating a false high can cause real hypoglycemia. |  |
| 10 | Early fullness on PD is mainly caused by: | Fluid in the abdomen pressing on the stomach, and steady glucose absorption | Both reduce how much the patient can eat. |  |
| 11 | Which advice helps early fullness? | Small frequent meals with protein first | Smaller meals and protein first make room for what matters. |  |
| 12 | Who changes the PD fluid strengths or schedule? | The nephrologist and PD team | The dietitian reports eating problems; the team decides. |  |
| 13 | Stronger PD fluid (for example 4.25%) gives: | More glucose absorbed | Higher dextrose removes more water and adds more glucose energy. |  |
| 14 | After peritonitis, protein losses: | Rise for several days | Inflammation of the membrane increases protein loss. |  |
| 15 | In a patient with diabetes on PD, absorbed glucose may: | Raise glucose, weight, and blood fats | Dialysate glucose is real energy and real glucose. |  |

## Module 11: Acute Kidney Injury and Inpatient Nutrition Support

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A patient on CKRT weighed 69 kg before illness and 78 kg now with edema. Which weight is the better basis? | 69 kg, with the source documented | Resuscitation fluid inflates today’s weight. |  |
| 2 | ESPEN’s protein reference for a critically ill patient on continuous KRT is: | 1.5 to 1.7 g/kg/day | Continuous therapy removes amino acids and illness raises needs. |  |
| 3 | At 69 kg, 1.5 to 1.7 g/kg/day is about: | 104 to 117 g | 1.5 × 69 = 103.5; 1.7 × 69 = 117.3. |  |
| 4 | Should protein be reduced to delay dialysis in a hospitalized patient with AKI? | No; ESPEN advises against it | Muscle loss is the greater harm; dialysis can support the kidney. |  |
| 5 | An outpatient low-protein order is still active in the ICU. The dietitian should: | Flag it to the team for cancellation | Outpatient restrictions do not fit critical illness. |  |
| 6 | Which is a refeeding risk factor? | Little or no intake for five days or more | Prolonged starvation depletes phosphate, potassium, magnesium, and thiamine. |  |
| 7 | Why does phosphate fall when feeding restarts? | Insulin drives it into cells | New tissue building and energy production pull phosphate into cells. |  |
| 8 | Who orders thiamine and electrolyte replacement? | The physician | The dietitian flags the risk and asks for the protocol. |  |
| 9 | Propofol supplies about: | 1.1 kcal per mL as fat | It is given in a fat emulsion; count it in energy intake. |  |
| 10 | Why might a standard formula suit a patient on CKRT better than a renal formula? | It contains more potassium and phosphate, which CKRT removes | CKRT removes electrolytes; renal formulas restrict them. |  |
| 11 | A patient was prescribed 1,800 kcal but received 900 kcal because feeds were stopped for scans. You should: | Record delivered intake and work with the team to reduce interruptions | Delivered intake is what the patient gets. |  |
