| 12 | In the first days of critical illness, ICU teams usually: | Build energy up over several days | Gradual build-up avoids overfeeding when the body makes its own glucose. |  |
| 13 | Phosphate is falling in a patient on CKRT. Possible causes include: | Refeeding and CKRT losses | Both can act together. |  |
| 14 | At discharge after AKI, the diet should: | Be reassessed with the latest kidney function at follow-up | Kidney recovery varies; the plan must follow it. |  |
| 15 | The ESPEN protein ranges are best described as: | Expert-based reference values to adjust to tolerance | They guide practice where trials are limited. |  |

## Module 12: Nutrition Before and After Kidney Transplantation

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which party dish is the immediate food-safety risk for a recent transplant recipient? | Kinilaw | Raw fish in vinegar is not reliably safe. |  |
| 2 | Why should transplant recipients avoid pomelo? | It may block the enzyme that breaks down tacrolimus, like grapefruit | The mechanism is shared with grapefruit; evidence is mainly laboratory and animal. |  |
| 3 | A recipient brings a herbal tonic. You should: | Ask him not to start it and refer it to the transplant pharmacist or physician | Herbs can raise or lower tacrolimus levels. |  |
| 4 | Before transplant, the goal of weight management is: | Less fat with muscle kept | Rapid loss on dialysis often takes muscle. |  |
| 5 | Which result should be checked before lifting a potassium restriction after transplant? | Current serum potassium | Tacrolimus and slow graft function can keep potassium high. |  |
| 6 | Tacrolimus commonly causes: | High potassium and low magnesium | Monitor both after transplant. |  |
| 7 | Phosphate after transplant often: | Falls, sometimes very low | The new kidney and high PTH pass phosphate into urine. |  |
| 8 | Afternoon high glucose after transplant is most often linked to: | Prednisone | Steroids raise glucose later in the day. |  |
| 9 | The four food safety steps are: | Clean, separate, cook, chill | Taught by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration. |  |
| 10 | Fiesta leftovers should be chilled within: | Two hours | Bacteria grow quickly at room temperature. |  |
| 11 | Who sets the transplant patient’s specific food rules and their duration? | The transplant program | Programs differ; follow theirs. |  |
| 12 | KDIGO advises that transplant candidates with obesity be: | Not excluded on body mass index alone | Assessment should be broader than one number. |  |
| 13 | Long-term risks after transplant include: | Weight gain, high blood pressure, high blood fats, diabetes, and bone loss | Manage them as cardiovascular risk factors. |  |
| 14 | A recipient asks if he must still drink only a liter a day. You should: | Check with the transplant team; many are asked to drink more early on | Fluid advice changes with the graft; the team decides. |  |
| 15 | Why use teach-back for food safety? | It confirms that the patient can apply the advice to real dishes | Understanding is shown, not assumed. |  |

## Module 13: Supportive Care, Frailty, and Special Populations

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In conservative kidney management, the first nutrition question is: | What matters most to you now? | Goals shape every later decision. |  |
| 2 | A patient’s goal is to sit with family on Sundays. Which outcome fits it best? | Enough strength and tolerable symptoms to join the family meal | The plan serves the person’s goal. |  |
| 3 | A neighbor told the family that all protein is harmful. The patient is losing weight. You should: | Explain that some protein is needed and bring back small portions of favored foods | Mistaken restrictions are a common cause of wasting. |  |
| 4 | For metallic taste, which suggestion is useful? | Sour and aromatic flavors such as calamansi and ginger | Acid and aromatics can mask metallic taste. |  |
| 5 | KDIGO 2024 advises considering, in older adults with frailty or sarcopenia: | Higher protein and energy targets | Function outweighs slowing progression in this group. |  |
| 6 | Which is a short frailty or sarcopenia screen? | SARC-F questionnaire | SARC-F takes minutes; grip strength and chair-stand also help. |  |
| 7 | For calcium stone formers, the AUA advises dietary calcium: | At normal levels, about 1,000 to 1,200 mg/day | Dietary calcium binds oxalate in the gut. |  |
| 8 | The AUA fluid goal for stone formers is enough to make urine of at least: | 2.5 liters a day | Dilute urine lowers the chance of crystals forming. |  |
| 9 | Before advising a high fluid intake for stones, check: | Stone type and whether heart, sodium, or kidney problems make it unsafe | High fluid can harm some patients. |  |
| 10 | Who should set the fluid plan in ADPKD? | The kidney team, considering function and medicines | Tolvaptan and kidney function change the advice. |  |
| 11 | An 11-year-old with CKD and slowing growth needs: | Referral to a pediatric renal team | Children need growth-based specialist care. |  |
| 12 | A woman on dialysis becomes pregnant. The best action is: | Prompt referral for joint nephrology, obstetric, and dietetic care | Pregnancy changes every target. |  |
| 13 | Which is an acute change requiring urgent medical review? | New confusion | Acute changes are not frailty progression. |  |
| 14 | Why ask about the caregiver? | Because the plan depends on who shops and cooks, and caregivers can burn out | A plan the family cannot sustain will fail. |  |
| 15 | In supportive care, a restriction should stay only if: | It makes the person feel better or prevents a crisis | Restrictions must earn their place. |  |

## Module 14: Vitamins, Minerals, Supplements, and Foods That Harm

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which vitamins are lost in dialysis? | Water-soluble vitamins such as B vitamins and C | Small water-soluble molecules pass into the dialysis fluid. |  |
| 2 | Why avoid vitamin A supplements in kidney failure? | It builds up and can become toxic | The failing kidney cannot clear it. |  |
| 3 | Why avoid 1,000 mg vitamin C tablets? | Excess vitamin C turns into oxalate | Oxalate can deposit in tissues and vessels. |  |
| 4 | A sensible vitamin C intake for a dialysis patient is about: | 75 to 90 mg | The usual daily intake, not high doses. |  |
| 5 | Star fruit is dangerous in kidney disease because it contains: | Oxalate and caramboxin | Oxalate injures the kidney; caramboxin is a nerve toxin. |  |
| 6 | A dialysis patient ate two star fruits this morning and feels well. You should: | Call the physician now | Symptoms can be delayed; early assessment matters. |  |
| 7 | Symptoms of star fruit poisoning include: | Hiccups, confusion, and seizures | Caramboxin affects the nervous system. |  |
| 8 | Kamias is a risk because it is rich in: | Oxalate | Case reports describe acute oxalate kidney injury after kamias juice. |  |
| 9 | Noni juice is a problem in CKD mainly because of its high: | Potassium | It can raise serum potassium. |  |
| 10 | Which item belongs in the “duplicate” group? | Calcium in both a binder and an over-the-counter supplement | The same ingredient in two products. |  |
| 11 | Who stops a prescribed medicine? | The prescriber | The dietitian reports; the prescriber decides. |  |
| 12 | KDOQI considers supplementing water-soluble vitamins reasonable in: | Patients with poor intake or on dialysis | Expert opinion in KDOQI 2020. |  |
| 13 | The evidence on star fruit poisoning comes mainly from: | Case reports and case series | Case reports show the harm, not how common it is. |  |
| 14 | A “kidney detox” tea: | Has no proven benefit and may contain harmful substances | No product cleanses the kidneys. |  |
| 15 | The best response to a patient’s remedy is: | Explain the risk, and offer something helpful instead | Respect, explain, redirect. |  |

## Module 15: Counseling and Filipino Food Skills

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A bouillon cube has 1,200 mg sodium and patis adds 600 mg; the pot makes four servings. Sodium per serving from these is: | 450 mg | 1,800 ÷ 4 = 450 mg. |  |
| 2 | With an extra half bowl of broth, the serving rises to about: | 675 mg | 450 × 1.5 = 675 mg, assuming even distribution. |  |
| 3 | Which makes a recipe estimate less certain? | Uneven servings and extra broth | Sodium follows the broth. |  |
| 4 | Which question is teach-back? | “Show me how you will measure the patis tomorrow.” | The patient demonstrates the plan. |  |
| 5 | Teach-back checks: | Whether the explanation was clear | It tests the teaching. |  |
| 6 | In elicit, offer, check, the step before offering is: | Asking what the person knows and wants | Start with the person’s view. |  |
| 7 | Why let the patient choose the change? | A self-chosen change is more likely to happen | Ownership supports action. |  |
| 8 | “Less than 2,000 mg of sodium” is clearer to most patients as: | One level teaspoon of salt for the whole day, from everything | Household measures beat numbers. |  |
| 9 | One meat exchange provides about: | 8 g of protein | From the Philippine Food Exchange Lists. |  |
| 10 | Pinggang Pinoy fills half the plate with: | Vegetables and fruits | Half vegetables and fruits, a quarter energy food, a quarter protein. |  |
| 11 | Why check potassium and phosphorus separately when using exchanges? | Exchange lists were not designed for kidney disease | Use PhilFCT and labels for these. |  |
| 12 | Which is affordable protein? | Eggs, tofu, monggo, small fish | Local staples serve the budget. |  |
| 13 | For a fiesta, the best approach is: | Plan portions, leave out broth and sawsawan, take binders, and keep dialysis | Planning works better than banning. |  |
| 14 | Who often needs the teaching most? | The person who cooks | The cook decides what is served. |  |
| 15 | When a patient’s first language is Kapampangan, you should: | Use Kapampangan, an interpreter, or a family member the patient chooses | Use the patient’s language. |  |

## Module 16: Transitions of Care, Quality Improvement, and Capstone

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Comparable post-dialysis weights were 63 kg and now 60 kg. The loss is about: | 4.8% | (63 − 60) ÷ 63 × 100 = 4.8%. |  |
| 2 | At 60 kg on stable HD, protein should be about: | 60 to 72 g/day | 1.0 to 1.2 × 60. |  |
| 3 | 9 of 12 eligible new patients were assessed in time. The measure is: | 75% | 9 ÷ 12 = 75%. |  |
| 4 | Which is a balancing measure for faster new-patient assessments? | Assessment time per patient or completeness of monitoring plans | It shows whether speed came at a cost. |  |
| 5 | The denominator in the example is: | All adults who started outpatient HD in the period, by the written rule | The patients who should have met the standard. |  |
| 6 | A run chart is used to: | See whether a measure changes over time after a change | Look for sustained shifts. |  |
| 7 | With small numbers, a change from 9 of 12 to 10 of 12: | Is one patient and may be chance | Small samples swing widely. |  |
| 8 | Which item belongs in every nutrition handoff? | Who follows up and when | Ownership and timing make a handoff work. |  |
| 9 | A low-protein order from the clinic is still active after a new HD start. This is an example of: | A plan lost or mis-carried at a transition | Transitions are where plans fail. |  |
| 10 | In the capstone, the binder question should be: | Handed to pharmacy to confirm prescribed timing | Timing is clarified by the prescriber or pharmacist. |  |
| 11 | What should happen first when a critical potassium with weakness is reported mid-session? | Activate the urgent pathway and confirm the handoff | Routine work gives way to urgent care. |  |
| 12 | PDSA stands for: | Plan, Do, Study, Act | A small test-of-change cycle. |  |
| 13 | A vague aim becomes a measure when it has: | A numerator, denominator, period, and data source | Definition makes it countable. |  |
| 14 | Why add a stability check before finalizing the HD protein range? | Acute illness changes needs and the meaning of urea-based estimates | The stable range assumes metabolic stability. |  |
| 15 | The patient-facing part of a handoff should include: | The next step in plain words and the next check date | Patients need to know what to do next. |  |

# Dialysis Clinical Head

## Module 1: Before You Sign: Authority and Appointment

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does the clinical head own? | The clinical system: policies, competency arrangements, the quality program, and holding or restarting unsafe operation | Prescriptions belong to the attending nephrologist and technical release to the technical leads. |  |
| 2 | Why does the long weekend gap matter when you judge coverage? | Mortality and heart failure admissions are highest the day after it | In a large cohort, deaths and cardiovascular admissions peaked on the day after the two-day gap. |  |
| 3 | You already head one hemodialysis unit. An owner offers you three more. How many may you accept? | Two, for a total of three | DOH, PhilHealth, and PSN cap a nephrologist at three dialysis units in total, counting every unit already headed, under any owner. |  |
| 4 | An old DOH form and the PSN guideline disagree on a qualification pathway. What resolves it? | Written clarification from the RLED and from PSN, both filed | Each authority answers its own question; get both answers in writing. |  |
| 5 | Who owns the decision that a particular patient can safely wait for a later session? | The attending nephrologist or physician on duty | It is a medical judgment about that patient. |  |
| 6 | Who signs the technical release of a water system after a repair? | The designated technical lead | The clinical head signs clinical authorization after the technical release exists. |  |
| 7 | The letter names “the company’s dialysis units.” What should you ask for? | A schedule listing each site, its stations, and its license | You cannot oversee a site you have not been told about. |  |
| 8 | Which of these must be written into the appointment before you sign? | Authority to hold or restrict an unsafe operation | Without it, you carry accountability without control. |  |
| 9 | A site has no named alternate physician. What is a reasonable response? | Defer that site until an alternate has accepted | Alternates are part of the coverage you are signing for. |  |
| 10 | United States rules assign the dialysis medical director which responsibilities? | Quality assessment, staff education, and policy oversight | The comparator shows the same pattern: named person, defined scope, accountability to governance. |  |
| 11 | What is the purpose of rehearsing three scenarios with the owner? | To agree in advance who stops, who tells patients, who verifies, and who restarts | The middle of a crisis is too late to negotiate authority. |  |
| 12 | A site is due to open before its licensing approvals are complete. What should you do? | Decline to be named as its head until licensing is complete | Your name on an unlicensed site is an endorsement you cannot support. |  |
| 13 | What is an authority matrix? | A table of each decision, its owner, its alternate, and its escalation route | It turns “who decides?” into a lookup. |  |
| 14 | The same document is offered as proof for the DOH, PSN, and PhilHealth. What is the problem? | Each authority asks a different question and needs its own evidence | Keep separate evidence for each authority. |  |
| 15 | In a hospital-based unit, what should the clinical head align with? | Medical staff bylaws and the hospital incident command structure | Hospital governance shapes who holds which decision. |  |

## Module 2: Licensing and Inspection Readiness

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which DOH order sets the baseline rules for licensing dialysis facilities? | AO 2012-0001 | AO 2013-0003 covers dialysis water. |  |
| 2 | What should you do before applying a 2026 interim licensing circular you have seen only by title? | Read its full text, annexes, and transition clauses, and ask the RLED how it applies | A title is not a rule. |  |
| 3 | The administrator wants two chairs in an unapproved bay. What is your first step? | Keep the bay closed and ask the RLED in writing | Changes to stations and rooms may need approval first. |  |
| 4 | A machine returned from repair has no signed release. Who closes that gap? | The designated technical lead by documenting checks and signing | Clinical authorization follows technical release. |  |
| 5 | What are the three sources in a tracer? | Document, observed practice, staff interview | Gaps live between what is written and what is done. |  |
| 6 | A policy is signed but staff describe a different workflow. What completes the tracer? | Observing practice and records, then recording the discrepancy | The discrepancy is the finding. |  |
| 7 | Which of these closes a finding? | Objective evidence that the control works, checked by a named person on a date | Completion is not effectiveness. |  |
| 8 | A finding last closed with “staff retrained” recurs. What does that suggest? | The fix was weak and its effect was never checked | Training alone fades; choose a stronger control and measure it. |  |
| 9 | What is containment? | The immediate step that prevents harm while the real fix is made | Tagging a machine out of service is containment. |  |
| 10 | What should happen to obsolete versions of a controlled document? | Withdraw them from use and archive one copy | The version in use should match the version in the binder. |  |
| 11 | Which registry must dialysis clinics report their patients to? | The Philippine Renal Disease Registry | PRDR participation is part of the licensing order. |  |
| 12 | A finding scores likelihood 4 and severity 4 on the teaching scale. What happens? | It goes to leadership review because it is 16, above the teaching threshold of 15 | Use your facility’s own thresholds in practice. |  |
| 13 | Who should own an inspection finding that needs money to fix? | A named owner, with the request escalated to the governing body | Resource-dependent risks go to governance with a request. |  |
| 14 | Why walk the route from the entrance to a chair with the floor plan? | The walk shows which records to check and whether the space matches the approval | Space and records should describe the same unit. |  |
| 15 | What is the clinical head’s role in an inspection? | To review the clinical criteria and make sure findings have owners and closure evidence | The compliance owner obtains forms; the head owns clinical criteria and follow-through. |  |

## Module 3: PhilHealth: Accreditation, the Package, and the Claim

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How many hemodialysis sessions a year does the current PhilHealth benefit cover for eligible patients? | 156 | 156 sessions, three a week for a year. |  |
| 2 | What is the package rate per covered session under Circular 2024-0023? | 6,350 pesos | The rate rose in steps during 2024 to 6,350 pesos. |  |
| 3 | Which circular sets quality standards PhilHealth uses as its reference for maintenance hemodialysis? | 2024-0029 | 2024-0023 sets the benefit; 2024-0029 the quality reference. |  |
| 4 | Where does a chart-to-claim trace start? | The record made at the time of treatment | The contemporaneous record is least likely to have been edited. |  |
| 5 | The statement shows a separate charge for a test inside the package. What is it? | A billing error to reverse or refund if confirmed | No-copay rules make included items non-billable. |  |
| 6 | A session appears on the schedule but the treatment record is missing. What do you do with the claim? | Hold it until the source evidence is reconciled | Claims follow records. |  |
| 7 | How should an administrative error in a claim be corrected? | With an audit trail showing original, correction, author, and time | Never change a clinical record to fit a claim. |  |
| 8 | A patient had two sessions at another unit in October. Why does it matter? | They count toward the same annual allocation | Count sessions across units. |  |
| 9 | A family asks whether dialysis will stop while a charge is disputed. What do you say? | Treatment continues while the charge is checked | Coverage never decides whether a patient is dialyzed today. |  |
| 10 | Which is the PRO? | The PhilHealth Regional Office | Confirm accreditation cycles with your PRO. |  |
| 11 | Twelve claims are returned for the same missing signature. What is this? | A process defect to fix at the step that let them leave | Returned claims are a quality signal. |  |
| 12 | A patient on four sessions a week for six weeks after a hospitalization will likely: | Run short of covered sessions near year end | Six extra sessions come out of the same 156. |  |
| 13 | What must be in place before the first claim for a new patient? | PDD registration | Registration is a condition of the benefit. |  |
| 14 | Who should approve submission when the claim date and the treatment date disagree? | Nobody until the records are reconciled and the claims supervisor reviews it | Route uncertainty to the claims supervisor and hold. |  |
| 15 | Why keep the license, accreditation, and signatory dates on one calendar? | A lapse in any one can make delivered sessions unpayable | Status is a prerequisite for every claim. |  |

## Module 4: Clinical Oversight: Coverage, Pathways, and Rights

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which patients carry the highest risk at the start of a Monday shift? | Those after the long gap, after a missed session, or just back from hospital | Risk concentrates after the longest gap, missed sessions, and hospitalization. |  |
| 2 | In DOPPS, missing at least one session in four months was associated with what? | Higher mortality and more hospitalization | Hazard ratio 1.68 for death; observational. |  |
| 3 | A roster is fully named but the only catheter-competent nurse is on leave. What is the shift? | Not safe for catheter patients until a validated nurse is assigned | Competency, not attendance, decides. |  |
| 4 | What does KDOQI recommend as the target spKt/V for thrice-weekly hemodialysis? | 1.4 | Target 1.4, with a minimum delivered dose of 1.2. |  |
| 5 | A post-dialysis sample is drawn at full blood flow after rinse-back. What happens to the dose result? | It looks better than it was | Diluted urea overstates the dose. |  |
| 6 | The physician on duty is absent at opening. What is the first decision? | Secure physician coverage within the protocol’s time, and hold stations that cannot be covered | Coverage comes before connection. |  |
| 7 | A patient arrives breathless after a missed session. What is it? | A medical emergency needing immediate assessment | Treat it as an emergency. |  |
| 8 | What makes a huddle useful? | It can change who starts, who waits, and who is reassigned | A huddle that changes nothing is a ritual. |  |
| 9 | A critical potassium was filed without anyone being called. What is the system fix? | A named owner for each critical result type with a time to decision | Pathways leak where nobody owns the joint. |  |
| 10 | When is a transfer handoff complete? | When the receiver repeats back and accepts, and the outcome is recorded | Close the loop. |  |
| 11 | Which study linked patient-to-nurse ratios to transplant access? | A national United States cohort of young adults | Highest ratios: 14% lower incidence of transplant. |  |
| 12 | What is the strength of the evidence that staffing affects dialysis outcomes? | Consistent observational associations | A reasonable inference, not proof of a ratio. |  |
| 13 | Who decides whether a specific waiting patient can be delayed? | The attending nephrologist or physician | It is an individual medical judgment. |  |
| 14 | What should a missed session trigger? | A same-day call by a named person and a confirmed next session | Ask what happened, then secure the next treatment. |  |
| 15 | Which is a patient right your system must protect? | A complaint route that does not threaten the patient’s slot | Fear of losing a slot silences complaints. |  |

## Module 5: Infection, Water, and Machines: The Invisible Barriers

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How did the S. aureus bloodstream infection rate in hemodialysis patients compare with other adults in United States surveillance? | About 100 times higher | 4,248 versus 42 per 100,000 person-years. |  |
| 2 | What was the catheter-related bloodstream infection rate in the Philippine cohort? | 6.72 per 1,000 catheter-days | Gram-negatives predominated, and nearly half were multidrug-resistant. |  |
| 3 | Which vascular access carries the highest bloodstream infection risk? | Central venous catheter | About six times the fistula risk in national surveillance. |  |
| 4 | What makes an infection prevention audit actionable? | A written definition, an observed denominator, feedback, and re-observation | Measure, feed back, and check again. |  |
| 5 | What happened when facilities adopted the CDC core interventions in a collaborative? | Bloodstream infection fell 32% and access-related infection 54% | No control arm, so interpret with care. |  |
| 6 | Why must chlorine be tested every shift? | Chloramine passes reverse osmosis and can break through carbon within hours, causing hemolysis | Carbon is the only reliable barrier. |  |
| 7 | Why place two carbon tanks in series? | So a breakthrough is caught after the first tank before the second fails | Test between the tanks. |  |
| 8 | What maximums does ISO 23500-3 (2024) set for dialysis water? | Below 100 CFU/mL and 0.25 EU/mL | Action levels are set lower so problems are found earlier. |  |
| 9 | A culture result is labeled only “RO room.” What is it worth for a release decision? | Nothing until it is traced to a named port; repeat it | A result you cannot place cannot support a release. |  |
| 10 | The vendor says yesterday was normal; today’s test is not logged. What do you do? | Treat it as a failed check: test now and hold the loop | Reassurance is not evidence. |  |
| 11 | Who signs technical verification, and who signs clinical authorization? | The technical lead, then the clinical head | Two signatures, two questions. |  |
| 12 | What happened at Caruaru in 1996? | Microcystins in inadequately treated water caused liver failure; 50 patients died | Water treatment is life support. |  |
| 13 | What did a systematic review conclude about dialyzer reuse and mortality? | Mostly very low-quality evidence and no consistent difference | Safety depends on how reuse is done. |  |
| 14 | How much higher was tuberculosis incidence among CKD stage 5 patients in Singapore than in the general population? | Roughly 7 to 15 times higher | 279 to 630 versus 33 to 41 per 100,000. |  |
| 15 | A transfer record shows a reactive hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg). What is the right sequence? | Confirm the result, apply the hepatitis B pathway, and arrange timely dialysis | Delaying dialysis is a risk too. |  |

## Module 6: Risk Management and Proving Improvement

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which is a usable risk statement? | Because two similar names share a shift, an order may enter the wrong chart and a patient may receive the wrong dialysate | Cause, failure, consequence. |  |
| 2 | A rare risk has a catastrophic consequence but a modest score. What should happen? | It still goes to leadership review | Catastrophic consequences merit review regardless of score. |  |
| 3 | What is a latent condition? | A weakness built into the system that waits for an active error | Sort orders, staffing patterns, and noisy alerts are latent conditions. |  |
| 4 | Which fix is strongest? | Making the error impossible or forcing a check before the step | Remove or force sits at the top of the hierarchy. |  |
| 5 | What did a single-center FMEA redesign of deterioration alerts report? | A 71% fall in hazard scores | A before-and-after report: useful, not definitive. |  |
| 6 | A unit had 3 bloodstream infections in 150 patient-months. What is the rate? | 2.0 per 100 patient-months | 3 divided by 150, times 100. |  |
| 7 | Infections went from one to two this month. What is the right response? | Plot the rate with its denominator and look for a real signal, unless patients are at risk now | Small numbers move by chance. |  |
| 8 | What is a balancing measure? | A measure that checks whether an improvement caused harm elsewhere | Treatment-start delay balances a new check. |  |
| 9 | What distinguishes completing an action from proving it worked? | Implementation shows it was done; later observation and outcomes show whether risk fell | Close actions on evidence of effect. |  |
| 10 | A vendor wants treatment-level data to compare outcomes before and after its device. What is this? | Research needing ethics review and privacy determination first | Data leaving the unit for a comparison is research. |  |
| 11 | What should an investigation look for first? | What made the error easy and catching it hard | Systems, not scapegoats. |  |
| 12 | Which run chart pattern is a signal rather than noise? | Six or more consecutive points on one side of the median | Use rules, not impressions. |  |
| 13 | Why are the infection rate and missed-treatment rate in the example different measures even though both are 2 per 100? | They use different events and denominators and reflect different risks | Label every denominator. |  |
| 14 | When does residual risk go to the governing body? | When it stays above the threshold or needs resources beyond the unit | Escalate with options and costs. |  |
| 15 | A pop-up alert fires on every screen and staff click through it. What is the problem? | Alert fatigue makes the alert a weak, noisy layer | Alerts used sparingly stay meaningful. |  |

## Module 7: The Dialysis Clock: Disasters and Continuity

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does a disaster raise the risk for dialysis patients so quickly? | It lengthens the gap between sessions, during which potassium and fluid accumulate | Risk is already highest after the usual two-day gap. |  |
| 2 | Before Hurricane Sandy, early dialysis was associated with what? | Fewer emergency visits and hospitalizations, and lower 30-day mortality | Observational, but consistent with the mechanism. |  |
| 3 | What share of surveyed Manhattan patients missed dialysis after Sandy? | About 26% | A median of two sessions. |  |
| 4 | Your backup unit is on the same flood-prone road. What is the issue? | A shared dependency: both may fail together | Find a second site in a different direction. |  |
| 5 | The receiving unit says it has four chairs. Is that a confirmed transfer? | No: confirm acceptance by patient name, time, staffing, transport, and handoff | A chair is not an accepted patient. |  |
| 6 | Who sets each displaced patient’s clinical priority? | The attending nephrologist or physician on duty | Priority is a clinical judgment. |  |
| 7 | Power is restored by generator. Can you restart? | Not until water, machines, staffing, supplies, and records are verified and signed | Power is one dependency of several. |  |
| 8 | When may you stand down? | When every patient on the tracker has an outcome or a named owner | Account for everyone first. |  |
| 9 | What should a patient emergency card include? | Diagnosis, access, dry weight, medicines, last results, and unit contacts | It is the handoff when a patient arrives at a strange unit. |  |
| 10 | Which law sets the Philippine disaster coordination framework? | RA 10121 | RA 10121, the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act. |  |
| 11 | Why keep an offline roster and contact list? | Power or network loss can disable the electronic system | Protect it like any record. |  |
| 12 | What was found about post-traumatic stress after Katrina among hemodialysis patients? | About 24% had symptoms, more if they missed three or more sessions | Missed sessions are psychological as well as physical harm. |  |
| 13 | A breathless patient who missed Tuesday calls during the flood. What first? | Arrange urgent physician assessment or emergency department referral | Urgent symptoms need urgent assessment, not a slot. |  |
| 14 | What should a receiving agreement settle about billing? | Who claims for the session, confirmed with the claims lead and PhilHealth | Prevent duplicate claims and protect access. |  |
| 15 | What makes an after-action review useful? | Owners, dates, and a repeat drill that tests the fix | Show the change in the next drill. |  |

## Module 8: Safe Capacity, Supply, and the Board Brief

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Ten stations, two quarantined, staffing for seven per shift. How many safe slots per shift? | 7 | Staffing is the narrowest gate. |  |
| 2 | With seven safe slots and two shifts, how many patients can be treated today? | 14 | 7 times 2. |  |
| 3 | Demand is 18 sessions and safe capacity is 14. What happens to the other 4? | Physicians prioritize them and each gets a confirmed plan and owner | No patient leaves without a plan. |  |
| 4 | What is the reorder point at 30 kits a day, 4 days lead time, and 60 kits safety stock? | 180 | 30 times 4, plus 60. |  |
| 5 | If lead time doubles to 8 days with the same buffer, what is the reorder point? | 300 | 30 times 8, plus 60. |  |
| 6 | What sets safe capacity? | The narrowest of the gates | Released machines, staffing, coverage, restrictions. |  |
| 7 | Why reserve capacity for some patients? | Patients after a missed session, back from hospital, or needing isolation cannot easily move | Plan for time-sensitive needs first. |  |
| 8 | What should a board brief do? | Ask for one decision with options, cost, effect, and owner | Owners act on choices. |  |
| 9 | Why include equity in a transfer decision? | Transfers can fall hardest on patients without transport or money | The cheapest option on paper can harm most in practice. |  |
| 10 | When should you recalculate safe capacity? | Whenever a gate changes | It moves daily. |  |
| 11 | A supplier warns of delay. When should you act? | Now, by recalculating the reorder point and ordering the difference | The math changes before the shelf does. |  |
| 12 | What should the board approve? | A specific action, owner, resources, deadline, and how impact will be checked | Make it verifiable. |  |
| 13 | When targets conflict with safety, what should the clinical head do? | State the lost capacity and activate the escalation or transfer plan | Make the trade-off visible. |  |
| 14 | Which is a balancing concern of repeated overtime? | Staff fatigue and burnout | Overtime fixes today and can break next month. |  |
| 15 | What should happen weekly for supplies? | Stock compared with reorder points and lead-time changes | A weekly check catches drift. |  |

## Module 9: Leading the Workforce Under Pressure

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A float nurse lacks validated catheter competency. Which assignments may she take? | Only those within her validated competency, with required supervision | Count competencies, not bodies. |  |
| 2 | An order reads “UF 3.5” with no units or time. What do you do? | Hold that treatment until the responsible or covering physician confirms it | Ambiguous orders are not executed on assumption. |  |
| 3 | A senior staff member asks the charge nurse to backdate a competency sign-off. What is it? | Falsification of a record | Refuse, assess properly, and escalate. |  |
| 4 | Why keep the safety track and the personnel track separate? | So reporting a problem does not automatically lead to discipline, and people keep reporting | Separate patient protection from employment decisions. |  |
| 5 | What should happen to the person who raised the concern? | Thanks, information about what will happen, and protection from retaliation | Protect the reporter. |  |
| 6 | In a national United States survey, about what share of dialysis staff met burnout criteria? | One third | 33%, driven by work exhaustion. |  |
| 7 | Which is a sign of hidden overload? | The same few people absorbing every short shift | The most reliable staff burn out first. |  |
| 8 | Who decides which patients can safely move to a later shift? | A qualified physician | Operations coordinates; the physician decides. |  |
| 9 | What should a return-to-duty decision cite? | Defined competencies, observed performance, reviewer approval, and any continued supervision | Objective criteria. |  |
| 10 | How should violence against staff be treated? | As a safety event with reporting and response | Violence remains frequent in renal units. |  |
| 11 | What is the most useful thing to tell an owner who wants every chair filled? | The safe number, the limiting resource, the plan for each patient, and the decision that would restore capacity | Give a choice with a cost. |  |
| 12 | You have a personal conflict in a staff investigation. What should you do? | Arrange an alternate reviewer | Conflicts need a neutral reviewer. |  |
| 13 | What is supernumerary status? | Present but not counted toward staffing | Use it for orientees and unvalidated staff. |  |
| 14 | Which action helps break the overload loop? | Protecting breaks and spreading difficult shifts | Break the loop where you can. |  |
| 15 | Can the owner authorize a nurse’s competency by approving the roster? | No; competency is shown by observed, signed performance | Authority over money is not authority over competence. |  |

## Module 10: Access, Conflict, and Transitions

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In DOPPS, which factor was associated with missed sessions? | Longer travel time to the unit | Also younger age, shorter treatments, and depression. |  |
| 2 | How did the 4-month missed-treatment risk vary across DOPPS countries? | From under 1% to 24% | A more than 50-fold variation. |  |
| 3 | A patient has missed four sessions. What is the first question? | What is getting in the way? | Ask before you label. |  |
| 4 | A family member blocks an exit and shouts. What comes first? | Making the area safe through the facility process | Safety first. |  |
| 5 | What comes second? | Securing the patient’s next treatment | Continuity before administration. |  |
| 6 | A patient becomes suddenly confused and aggressive during dialysis. What should be considered? | A medical cause such as low glucose, uremia, or stroke | Behavior change can be clinical. |  |
| 7 | What did a structured second-chance program achieve after involuntary discharge? | 7 of 12 patients placed without recurrence | Debriefing, coaching, and a formal handoff. |  |
| 8 | Which is a defensible input to scheduling? | Clinical urgency and verified capacity | Scheduling follows clinical need and capacity. |  |
| 9 | What must be in place before any involuntary transfer? | Leadership and compliance review, accommodations considered, and a confirmed continuity plan | Never leave a patient without treatment. |  |
| 10 | Which DSWD program can help with dialysis costs? | Assistance to Individuals in Crisis Situation (AICS) | Subject to assessment and current rules. |  |
| 11 | What is a Malasakit Center? | A one-stop office in government hospitals that coordinates financial assistance | Established by RA 11463. |  |
| 12 | Should treatment wait for a pending assistance request? | No; keep the care plan separate from the assistance request | Treatment never waits for a grant. |  |
| 13 | When is a transplant referral complete? | When received, accepted, and the next step recorded with an owner | Track referrals to outcome. |  |
| 14 | Who reviews a disputed charge? | The claims lead, with compliance, returning the outcome to the patient | Give a date and a route. |  |
| 15 | Why record clinical and administrative decisions separately? | So each can be reviewed on its own basis and clinical care is not driven by administrative motives | Keep the reasons visible. |  |

## Module 11: Money, Procurement, and Conflicts of Interest

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Stock lasts 3 days and the verified delivery lands on day 5. What is the gap? | 2 days | 5 minus 3. |  |
| 2 | The verified product costs 120 pesos more per unit for 80 units. What is the incremental cost? | 9,600 pesos | 120 times 80. |  |
| 3 | What belongs in a full cost comparison? | Purchase, qualification, downtime, transfers, patient access, and consequences of delay | Hidden costs often dominate. |  |
| 4 | Can an owner authorize a product’s clinical suitability by approving payment? | No; clinical and technical suitability need their own reviews | Each gate has its own owner. |  |
| 5 | Which is a qualification gate for a substitute dialyzer? | Technical compatibility with your machines and reprocessing | Also registration, clinical review, training, traceability. |  |
| 6 | A vendor is the owner’s nephew. What should happen? | Written disclosure, recusal of the conflicted person, and evaluation by others through the usual gates | Disclose, then step back. |  |
| 7 | The owner proposes removing the second carbon tank to save money. What do you do? | Escalate in writing that it removes a safety control, with the risk and alternatives | Some costs are the price of operating at all. |  |
| 8 | If a qualified product is unavailable in time, what is the fallback? | Restrict to verified capacity, prioritize clinically, and arrange confirmed transfers | Time-bound and physician-led. |  |
| 9 | Why use ranges in a budget brief? | To show honestly how results depend on uncertain assumptions | Ranges are more useful than false precision. |  |
| 10 | What is the first step when two nephrologists dispute coverage? | Make sure every patient has a covering physician | Stabilize coverage, then mediate. |  |
| 11 | Who should mediate the dispute? | A neutral medical staff leader or governance designee under the bylaws | Recuse anyone with a material conflict. |  |
| 12 | Collections are late but demand is stable. Why separate the two? | They need different responses | Cash timing and demand are different problems. |  |
| 13 | What should compliance review in a vendor offer? | Inducements, bundled charges, and payments that could look like referral incentives | Look for incentives that distort care. |  |
| 14 | Why record lot numbers for dialyzers? | So a recall can find every patient exposed | Traceability is a safety gate. |  |
| 15 | What is a variable cost? | A cost that rises with each session, such as dialyzers | Fixed costs do not change with volume. |  |

## Module 12: When the System Goes Dark: Downtime and Privacy

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What happened at adjacent emergency departments after a ransomware attack on a nearby health system? | Census and ambulance arrivals rose sharply | Harm spreads beyond the target. |  |
| 2 | At untargeted adjacent hospitals during the attack, what happened to cardiac arrests? | They increased | Observational, single event. |  |
| 3 | A new order written yesterday cannot be retrieved. What do you do? | Ask the prescriber to confirm directly before treatment | Treat only from verified information. |  |
| 4 | Which belongs in the minimum safe data set? | Current order, dry weight, access, allergies, infection status, last key results | What you must verify before treating. |  |
| 5 | Reception proposes sending the census to a personal group chat. What is it? | An unauthorized disclosure of sensitive personal information | Use the approved offline roster. |  |
| 6 | Within how many hours must the NPC be notified when notification is required? | 72 | From knowledge or reasonable belief that a breach occurred. |  |
| 7 | Who assesses whether a breach requires notification? | The designated privacy lead | The unit contains and escalates at once. |  |
| 8 | After restoration, three patients appear twice. What must happen before billing? | Reconcile against the paper source and hold claims for the period | Duplicate claims are billing errors. |  |
| 9 | Why use a second checker during reconciliation? | Discrepancies with clinical or billing consequences need independent verification | Two sets of eyes on consequential changes. |  |
| 10 | How should downtime entries be carried into the restored record? | From the paper source, preserving original timestamps and provenance | Provenance matters. |  |
| 11 | What makes an offline roster useful? | Current version, authorized access, necessary fields, and a tested retrieval process | And destroy old copies. |  |
| 12 | How long might recovery from a major cyberattack take? | Weeks to months | A cancer center cleared its backlog by day 210. |  |
| 13 | Under the Data Privacy Act, health information is: | Sensitive personal information | It needs a lawful basis to be processed and shared. |  |
| 14 | The census was already sent. What first? | Contain: ask for deletion and record confirmations, then notify the privacy lead | Contain, preserve, escalate. |  |
| 15 | Who reconciles orders after downtime? | The physician on duty or designated prescriber | Each item needs a named owner. |  |

## Module 13: Serious Events, Families, and Outside Scrutiny

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A second patient on the same shift becomes unwell. What question should you ask? | What do the two patients share? | Look actively for a shared exposure. |  |
| 2 | What should you do with the dialyzers, lines, and vials used for the affected patients? | Keep them with lot numbers as possible evidence | Preserve before you clean. |  |
| 3 | What should happen to new connections on a possibly shared water loop? | Hold until the technical lead and clinical head review the evidence | Act on plausible shared exposure. |  |
| 4 | A daughter asks whether the clinic caused her father’s collapse. What is the best answer now? | We do not yet know; we are checking everything, and I will call you at a set time | Honest about uncertainty, specific about follow-up. |  |
| 5 | What did communication-and-resolution programs show in Massachusetts hospitals? | Improved trends in some liability measures and no worsening | Transparency without adverse financial consequence. |  |
| 6 | In New York hospitals’ program, what share of events involved substandard care? | About a quarter | Three quarters did not. |  |
| 7 | A reporter calls about a patient. What should reception say? | That the clinic cannot discuss any patient, and give the spokesperson’s contact | Privacy and accuracy both protect the patient. |  |
| 8 | Who decides on external notifications? | The leadership team with quality, privacy, and compliance, recording the decision and time | Check obligations and deadlines. |  |
| 9 | What share of clinicians involved in adverse events experience a second-victim response? | Between 10% and 43% | Support them separately from the review. |  |
| 10 | Why keep staff support separate from fact-finding? | So seeking help is not confused with being investigated | Supported staff report earlier. |  |
| 11 | What three lists should the first review separate? | Confirmed facts, open questions with owners, decisions with times | Keeps uncertainty visible. |  |
| 12 | You promised to call the family at 6 p.m. but have little new. What should you do? | Call at 6 p.m. and say so | Keep the promise. |  |
| 13 | Which is evidence that a cluster may be environmental? | Similar reactions in patients on the same loop and shift | Shared timing and location suggest shared exposure. |  |
| 14 | Who should lead family communication? | One named person, through the disclosure process | One voice, consistent messages. |  |
| 15 | Should you apologize before the cause is known? | Yes, that the event happened, without assigning blame | Sorry that it happened is not an admission of fault. |  |

## Module 14: New Services, Handover, and Your First 90 Days

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What should you write before evaluating a new technology? | The question: population, intervention, comparison, outcome | Every technology answers a question. |  |
| 2 | In CONVINCE, what was the hazard ratio for death with high-dose HDF? | 0.77 | 95% confidence interval 0.65 to 0.93. |  |
| 3 | Deaths were 21.9% with HD and 17.3% with HDF. What is the absolute risk reduction? | 4.6 percentage points | 21.9 minus 17.3. |  |
| 4 | What is the number needed to treat for an absolute reduction of 4.6 percentage points? | About 22 | 1 divided by 0.046, over about 30 months. |  |
| 5 | A brochure says HDF lowers mortality by 23%. What is missing? | The absolute effect, the population, the convection volume achieved, and the time | Relative effects alone overstate benefit. |  |
| 6 | Which patients may not gain the survival benefit of HDF? | Those who cannot achieve high convection volumes | Benefit depends on dose. |  |
| 7 | What did the 2025 EuDial consensus conclude? | HDF appears associated with improved survival provided high convection volumes are achieved | Consensus, not a standard of care. |  |
| 8 | What is the status of the UK H4RT trial in this course? | Follow-up ended in 2025; results not found in the literature searched | Check whether it has reported before deciding. |  |
| 9 | Which infrastructure question is specific to HDF? | Whether the water system can deliver ultrapure water in larger volumes with monitoring | HDF uses the fluid as a replacement infused into the blood. |  |
| 10 | What is a stop criterion? | A condition agreed in advance that pauses or ends a pilot | Agree it before you start. |  |
| 11 | The owner announced a night shift before readiness was checked. What do you do? | Publish readiness criteria and a review date; move the start if they are not met | A launch that must be pulled back hurts more than a delay. |  |
| 12 | When is a handover complete? | When the incoming lead acknowledges every open item, owner, and deadline in writing | What is not written is not handed over. |  |
| 13 | What should your first week focus on? | Work people are already waiting for: referrals, results, repairs, staffing | Give each an owner first. |  |
| 14 | Two machines have no maintenance records since June. When do you act? | This week: hold them until released | Containment cannot wait for the plan. |  |
| 15 | What should a pilot’s review decide? | Whether to continue, extend, or stop, against agreed safety and performance criteria | Set the review date at the start. |  |

# Peritoneal Dialysis Nurse

## Module 1: How PD Works, and What the PD Nurse Does

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which process moves urea from blood into dialysis solution during a dwell? | Diffusion down a concentration gradient | Urea moves from the high level in the blood to the low level in fresh solution. Aquaporins move water, not urea. Lymphatics return fluid to the body. Tubular secretion happens in the kidney, not the peritoneum. |  |
| 2 | Why does net ultrafiltration slow, and sometimes reverse, late in a long glucose dwell? | Glucose is absorbed, the osmotic gradient fades, and reabsorption continues | The osmotic pull is spent as glucose crosses into the blood, while lymphatic and tissue reabsorption keep going. Obstruction causes a poor drain, not a reversal. Blood urea and urine output do not drive water removal in the dwell. |  |
| 3 | Why is icodextrin often used for the long dwell? | It is absorbed slowly and sustains water removal over many hours | Its large molecules stay in the abdomen and keep drawing water through the small pores. It does not prevent infection or replace the kidneys. Whether it is used is the prescriber’s decision. |  |
| 4 | Aling Rosario’s exchanges show positive net UF, but her weight is rising and her urine has fallen. What is the best interpretation? | Total fluid removal has fallen; assess the whole balance and report the trend | Urine and UF are two routes for the same fluid. When urine falls and UF does not rise, the difference stays in the body. Check the scale, but do not assume it. Skipping an exchange is unsafe. |  |
| 5 | Which statement about CAPD and APD is correct? | APD adds programming and machine-related failure modes to the human ones | APD keeps the human risks and adds program mismatch, alarms and power dependence. Many APD prescriptions include a day dwell. The cycler belongs to APD. Neither form suits everyone. |  |
| 6 | What does sodium sieving early in a strong glucose dwell reflect? | Water crossing aquaporins without sodium | Water moving through aquaporins leaves sodium behind, so the solution’s sodium falls briefly. The other options describe unrelated processes. |  |
| 7 | Why do PD patients lose albumin into the effluent every day? | Through large pores in the peritoneal capillaries | A small number of large pores lets protein out. Losses rise during peritonitis but happen every day. Glucose binding and tubular damage are not the mechanism. |  |
| 8 | Mr. Arnel feels well, but his night drains are always short and his ankles swell. Which membrane pattern fits best? | Fast transport, with early loss of the glucose gradient in long dwells | Fast transporters absorb glucose quickly, lose their osmotic pull early and drain short after long dwells. Slow transporters do the opposite. The prescriber confirms the pattern with a PET. |  |
| 9 | Which action is within the PD nurse’s role without a new order? | Comparing delivered therapy with the written order and reporting a discrepancy | Verification and reporting are core nursing work. Solution, icodextrin and antibiotics are prescription decisions. Antibiotics may be given under a written standing protocol, but never on the nurse’s own decision. |  |
| 10 | Aling Rosario’s urine fell from about 700 mL to about 250 mL a day, and her exchange UF did not change. About how much extra fluid stays in her body each day? | About 450 mL | 700 minus 250 is about 450 mL a day that no longer leaves by the kidneys. With unchanged UF and intake, it accumulates, which matches her weight gain. |  |
| 11 | Which finding in this module calls for the emergency pathway rather than routine review? | New breathlessness at rest | Breathlessness at rest may mean acute fluid overload or a heart problem. Edema and one small drain need assessment and trend review. A diet question is routine teaching. |  |
| 12 | How should you report a worsening fluid pattern? | Weight trend, blood pressure, edema, urine and UF totals, intake change, and what you checked | A complete report with numbers lets the prescriber decide without calling you back. A vague word, a single number or a delay does not. |  |
| 13 | Mang Tonyo’s night dwell drains 150 mL less than was filled, but his day total is +550 mL. What is the best response? | Check the measurement, then report the net-negative long dwell with his weight, blood pressure and urine trend | A positive total can hide a long dwell that gives fluid back. Confirm the numbers and report the pattern. Changing dwell length or solution is a prescription decision. |  |
| 14 | In observational studies, preserved residual kidney function in PD is: | Associated with better survival | The evidence is observational. It shows an association, not proof that protecting urine output causes longer survival. The other options overstate or contradict the data. |  |
| 15 | A patient asks why he cannot leave the night dwell in longer “for more cleaning.” The best answer explains that: | Transport slows as the gradient flattens and fluid may be absorbed back, so the prescriber sets the dwell length | Small-solute transport plateaus and a long glucose dwell can turn net negative. Blockage and infection are not the reason. |  |

## Module 2: Modality Counseling, Shared Decisions and Home Readiness

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the best opening for a modality conversation? | “What does a good week look like for you, and what do you want to keep doing?” | Goal-directed care starts from the person’s priorities. The other openings are directive, unsupported as stated, or start from the equipment. |  |
| 2 | How should observational survival comparisons between PD and hemodialysis be described to a patient? | For people eligible for both, survival is broadly similar; the choice rests largely on lifestyle and values | The data are observational and shaped by who chooses each modality. Claims of proven superiority overstate them. |  |
| 3 | According to the 2026 ISPD position statement, about what proportion of people who start PD are no longer on it at two years? | Nearly half | Nearly half, mostly through transfer to hemodialysis, death or transplantation. Transplantation is a good outcome; much of the rest is the target of better readiness. |  |
| 4 | Which barrier is usually remediable rather than a suitability question for the nephrologist? | Poor near vision that is adequate with lighting and a magnifier | Vision aids can solve it, and you can verify that by watching. The others are clinical suitability questions. |  |
| 5 | What establishes readiness after a home barrier is addressed? | Observed safe performance under the revised conditions | Readiness is demonstrated, not declared. Statements, forms and calendars do not show performance. |  |
| 6 | A caregiver will perform the cycler setup. Who must demonstrate it? | The designated caregiver, with the patient’s own role and a backup arrangement also assessed | The person who does the task shows it. A backup is needed for illness, work or travel. |  |
| 7 | The 2020 ISPD goal-directed prescribing recommendations are best described as: | Practice points based on limited evidence | They are labeled practice points. Never assign a grade the source did not give. |  |
| 8 | Why does a readiness plan need a reassessment date? | Because function, caregivers and homes change over time | Readiness is a judgment at one point in time. A patient slowed by uremia may learn much better after starting dialysis. |  |
| 9 | During a home visit, the family cat sleeps in the planned exchange room. What is the best action? | Plan to keep pets out of the exchange area and confirm at follow-up | The 2022 ISPD recommendations address pets as a modifiable risk. Unsuitability or giving the pet away is out of proportion. |  |
| 10 | Mrs. Teresita says, “If I choose PD, I can never have a transplant.” What is correct? | Transplant evaluation can proceed while she is on PD | PD does not prevent transplantation, and evaluation can run alongside. |  |
| 11 | A patient has a tremor and struggles with clamps. What should you consider first? | Assistive devices, a cycler-based regimen or reassigning that task, then observing performance | Adapt, then verify by watching. Skipping a step is unsafe, and declaring PD impossible is premature. |  |
| 12 | What is the nurse’s role when a patient has had several major abdominal operations? | Document the history and route the suitability question to the nephrologist | Suitability belongs to the nephrologist. Your part is an accurate history routed to the right person. |  |
| 13 | What does assisted PD mean in the 2024 ISPD position paper? | Help with PD tasks from family, paid caregivers or health workers, temporary or long term | Assistance is defined broadly and can be short term, such as after a hospital stay, or long term. |  |
| 14 | Mr. Rolando’s ground floor floods each rainy season. What is the best readiness action? | Move storage and the exchange corner upstairs, keep a reserve supply before typhoon season, and confirm at follow-up | Name the barrier, the fix and how you will verify it. A flood-prone ground floor is a storage and location problem, not a reason to deny PD. |  |
| 15 | Which documentation best reflects a remediable barrier? | Barrier, owner, action, observable criterion, reassessment date | It names who does what and how success will be checked. The others give the next nurse nothing to act on. |  |

## Module 3: The Catheter, the Tunnel and the Exit Site

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which finding defines exit-site infection in the 2023 ISPD recommendations? | Purulent discharge, with or without redness | Pus is the defining feature. Redness alone may be mechanical or part of healing. Crust and discomfort are nonspecific. |  |
| 2 | What defines tunnel infection? | Clinical inflammation along the tunnel, with or without fluid around the catheter on ultrasound | Tunnel infection is inflammation along the course under the skin. Cloudy effluent points to peritonitis; fever alone is nonspecific. |  |
| 3 | Mr. Dante, three weeks after insertion, climbs scaffolding with his catheter tucked loosely in his waistband. Why does this matter? | Traction tears the healing tract and opens a path for bacteria | The new lining of the tract is fragile for weeks. Repeated pulling injures it and invites infection. |  |
| 4 | Which organism is classically linked to periluminal infection and catheter loss? | Staphylococcus aureus | S. aureus, together with P. aeruginosa, are the classic periluminal organisms. |  |
| 5 | What is the 2023 ISPD target for the overall exit-site infection rate? | No more than 0.40 episodes per year at risk | The 2023 update set a target of no more than 0.40 episodes per year at risk. |  |
| 6 | Which prevention measure carries the only 1A recommendation in the 2022 peritonitis guideline? | Systemic antibiotic prophylaxis at catheter insertion | Insertion prophylaxis is the 1A recommendation. Routine topical antibiotics were downgraded in 2023. |  |
| 7 | How should the PDOPPS association between topical exit-site antibiotics and peritonitis be described? | A lower risk that did not reach statistical significance in observational data | The rate ratio was 0.79 with a confidence interval that crossed 1, in an observational design. That is a hint, not proof. |  |
| 8 | Ms. Cora has 5 mm of redness, no drainage and no tunnel signs. She carried heavy boxes last weekend. Best action? | Document the size, review trauma and immobilization, and recheck within the unit’s timeframe | Redness alone after trauma needs measurement and follow-up, not automatic treatment. |  |
| 9 | Purulent exit-site discharge plus new cloudy effluent should prompt: | Peritonitis pathway activation as well as exit-site evaluation | Cloudy effluent means possible peritonitis, which may be catheter-related. Both problems need attention today. |  |
| 10 | When should an exit-site swab be taken relative to ordered antibiotics? | Before the first dose when feasible, per protocol | Cultures taken before antibiotics are more likely to identify the organism, which guides the prescriber’s choice. |  |
| 11 | A program had 11 exit-site infections over 34.5 patient-years at risk. What is the rate? | About 0.32 episodes per year at risk | 11 divided by 34.5 is about 0.32, within the 2023 target of no more than 0.40. |  |
| 12 | A cuff visible at the exit site suggests: | Cuff extrusion, often linked to chronic infection; escalate | An extruding cuff is a same-day escalation. |  |
| 13 | Which catheter interventions short of full replacement did the 2023 update describe for persistent infection? | Shaving or removing the outer cuff and relocating the exit site | These are procedures done by the access team. |  |
| 14 | Why should exit-site findings be documented the same way at every visit? | To detect change over time and support decisions on treatment duration | Consistent descriptions, with measurements, show whether the site is improving or worsening. |  |
| 15 | Which route of infection is mainly addressed by exchange technique rather than exit-site care? | Intraluminal (touch contamination) | Technique protects the inside of the system. Exit-site care protects the outside. |  |

## Module 4: The Manual Exchange: Measurement and Contamination

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does flushing fresh solution to the drain bag before filling reduce peritonitis risk? | It washes bacteria introduced at connection into the drain bag instead of the peritoneum | That is the mechanism of flush before fill. It does not warm, remove glucose or measure anything. |  |
| 2 | In the Cochrane review of 12 trials, Y-set or double-bag systems compared with standard systems: | Reduced the proportion of patients with peritonitis (odds ratio 0.33) | Randomized evidence showed a large reduction, which is why twin-bag systems are the standard. |  |
| 3 | Fill 2,000 mL, drain 1,850 mL. What is net UF? | −150 mL | 1,850 minus 2,000 is −150 mL: fluid was absorbed. |  |
| 4 | Fill 2,000 mL, drain 2,350 mL. What is net UF? | +350 mL | 2,350 minus 2,000 is +350 mL: fluid was removed. |  |
| 5 | A bag has a small leak at the seal. Best action? | Replace it before beginning and report the defect | A compromised bag may be contaminated. Never use it, and report the defect so the supplier can check the batch. |  |
| 6 | The patient touches the tip of the new bag connector; the clamp is closed and no fluid has entered. This is: | Dry contamination | The system stayed closed and nothing was infused after the break. |  |
| 7 | During a brownout, the patient touches the connection with the clamp open and the fill continues. This is best classified as: | Wet contamination; clamp and call the PD team at once | The system was open at the touch and fluid entered afterward. |  |
| 8 | What information matters most in a contamination call? | What touched what, when, clamp status, whether fluid entered, damage and symptoms | These details tell the team which response fits. |  |
| 9 | Which warming method is acceptable? | Only the approved dry warming method per device instructions | Other methods risk overheating or contaminating the bag. |  |
| 10 | Mr. Gary’s UF fell sharply in one week, just after his son began weighing bags on a different scale. What should you consider first? | A change in measurement | Rule out a measurement change before reading a falling UF as physiology. |  |
| 11 | A patient wipes the touched connector with alcohol and continues. The best teaching response is: | Explain that a touched sterile part means stopping and calling, and make calling feel safe | Wiping does not restore sterility, and blame discourages reporting. |  |
| 12 | How can a patient check effluent clarity simply? | Look through it at printed text or a white background | If print cannot be read through it, the effluent may be cloudy. |  |
| 13 | Mr. Efren’s wife has been buying supplies from an eight-month-old prescription card. What is the best response? | Replace the card with the current dated order, confirm deliveries, and tell the nephrologist | Verification against the current order is nursing work. The mismatch may explain clinical changes, so the nephrologist needs to know. |  |
| 14 | Who decides whether preventive antibiotics are given after wet contamination? | The prescriber, per the unit’s protocol | Antibiotic decisions belong to the prescriber or a written standing protocol. |  |
| 15 | Why is a single touch contamination significant in PD? | PD solution is a warm, glucose-rich fluid where bacteria can multiply during a dwell | A few bacteria can grow during the dwell, which is why the response is time-sensitive. |  |

## Module 5: The Cycler: Setup, Treatment Record and Alarms

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The programmed last-fill solution differs from the written order. What should happen? | Pause the setup and obtain clarification before treatment | Never start on a mismatch, and never guess which side is right. Ask the prescriber and document who resolved it. |  |
| 2 | Why do short overnight dwells help small-solute clearance? | They keep the diffusion gradient steep | Frequent fresh fills restore the gradient before it flattens. |  |
| 3 | A long glucose day dwell may: | Become net negative as the osmotic gradient fades | The glucose is absorbed and reabsorption continues, which is why icodextrin is often used for the day dwell. |  |
| 4 | Mr. Ramon has sharp pelvic pain at the end of every drain. What does tidal mode do for him? | Leaves a reserve volume so the catheter tip does not sit on bare tissue | Partial drains keep some fluid in, which reduces end-of-drain pain and alarms. The prescriber decides on tidal mode. |  |
| 5 | In PDOPPS, higher facility APD use was: | Associated with modestly lower peritonitis risk, at the margin of significance | The rate ratio was 0.95 per 10 percentage points, confidence interval 0.91 to 1.00, in an observational design. |  |
| 6 | A drain alarm occurs and the patient reports severe abdominal pain. First priority? | Assess the person and arrange urgent assessment through the PD pathway | Person first. Severe pain with an alarm is a clinical problem until proven otherwise. |  |
| 7 | Which check is usually permitted for a drain alarm? | Checking for closed clamps or kinked lines | External checks per the device instructions are permitted. The others are unsafe. |  |
| 8 | Drain volumes fall over several nights and the patient has had no bowel movement for four days. This most suggests: | Constipation affecting catheter position | A loaded bowel can displace the catheter tip. Constipation is a common cause of catheter dysfunction. |  |
| 9 | What must accompany an escalation for recurring low drain? | Alarm type and phase, frequency, checks tried, symptoms, effluent and therapy delivered | Complete data let the team decide without calling back. |  |
| 10 | When may backup CAPD be used during a power failure? | When a prescribed backup regimen, suitable supplies and validated competence exist | Backup must be planned and validated before the storm, not improvised during it. |  |
| 11 | Swelling of the genitals during APD fill suggests: | A possible dialysate leak; report promptly | Leaks can track into the abdominal wall and genitals. |  |
| 12 | A patient’s drains and UF per night are unchanged, but three nights last week stopped early and weight rose 1.4 kg. What should you explore first? | Why therapy was stopped early, and report the lost therapy with the reason | Read completion before volumes. Undelivered therapy explains the weight gain; the reason may be social, technical or fear of alarms. |  |
| 13 | Breathlessness soon after the last fill should prompt: | Urgent assessment for overfill or hydrothorax | A pleural leak or overfill can make breathing difficult and needs urgent assessment. |  |
| 14 | Who sets the APD prescription? | The nephrologist | The nurse verifies the program against the order and monitors the result. |  |
| 15 | Why is the cycler record described as a monitoring tool? | It shows drain patterns, completion, UF and alarms that reflect the patient’s condition | The record carries clinical signals about the catheter, the bowel, the abdomen and the household. |  |

## Module 6: Monitoring, Membrane Testing and Goal-Directed PD

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In ADEMEX, increasing peritoneal creatinine clearance: | Did not change survival (relative risk 1.00) | Clearance rose and stayed higher, but survival was the same. |  |
| 2 | Kt/V urea is best described as: | A surrogate measure of dose | It is a laboratory surrogate, not an outcome patients feel. |  |
| 3 | The 2020 ISPD goal-directed prescribing recommendations are: | Practice points | They are labeled practice points because the evidence base is limited. Do not assign a grade. |  |
| 4 | When should the first PET be done according to the 2021 ISPD recommendations? | Between 6 and 12 weeks after starting PD | The timing carries GRADE 1A. Later PETs follow clinical indications. |  |
| 5 | A faster peritoneal solute transfer rate is associated with: | Lower survival on PD, partly through lower UF in long dwells | This is a GRADE 1A association in the 2021 recommendations. |  |
| 6 | Which 4-hour PET result suggests low UF capacity with 3.86% glucose? | Net UF less than 400 mL | The ISPD 2021 threshold for 3.86% glucose is less than 400 mL (GRADE 1B). |  |
| 7 | And with 2.27% glucose? | Net UF less than 100 mL | The threshold for 2.27% glucose is less than 100 mL. |  |
| 8 | What does the sodium dip at 1 hour of a 3.86% dwell help assess? | Water transport through aquaporins (intrinsic membrane function) | A reduced dip suggests impaired free-water transport. |  |
| 9 | Mrs. Gemma’s PET was filled 25 minutes late but sampled by the clock, and the final drain was estimated. What should you report? | The result together with the deviations, so the team can decide whether to repeat | Report deviations with the result. A spoiled test can mislabel a membrane and change a prescription unnecessarily. |  |
| 10 | A 24-hour effluent collection missed one bag. Best action? | Record the omission, inform the team, and ask whether to repeat | Transparency preserves the result’s meaning. An estimate looks like evidence but is not. |  |
| 11 | Which modifiable peritonitis risk factor named in the 2022 ISPD recommendations also appears in metabolic monitoring? | Low potassium | Low potassium is common in PD and is a named modifiable risk factor. |  |
| 12 | In the Hong Kong trial, patients assigned to the lowest Kt/V target (1.5 to 1.7): | Had similar survival but more clinical problems and more erythropoietin use | Survival did not differ significantly, but more were withdrawn for clinical problems. |  |
| 13 | Lola Pacita and Mr. Noel have the same Kt/V. He has lost 3 kg and stopped working; she is meeting her goals. What should your reviews show? | Each person’s symptoms, function, nutrition and goals alongside the number | Clearance is one input among many. Your report decides whether the nephrologist sees the difference. |  |
| 14 | Icodextrin compared with glucose for the long dwell: | Can improve fluid status and reduce fluid-overload episodes (GRADE 1A) | This is from the ISPD 2021 recommendations. Whether to use it is the prescriber’s decision. |  |
| 15 | When reporting a trend, which statement is most useful? | “Weight rose from 68.0 to 70.0 kg over five days while daily UF fell from about 750 to 450 mL and urine from about 600 to 350 mL; all exchanges done; mild breathlessness on exertion.” | Baseline, interval, current values and context. |  |

## Module 7: Peritonitis: Recognition, Sampling and Prevention

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A patient feels well but reports cloudy effluent. What is the response? | Activate urgent PD assessment, screen for instability and arrange sampling | Cloudy effluent without pain still counts, especially in APD and in older or diabetic patients. |  |
| 2 | How many of the three ISPD 2022 criteria are needed to diagnose peritonitis? | At least two | At least two of three: clinical features, the cell count criterion and a positive culture. |  |
| 3 | Which effluent result meets the cell-count criterion after a dwell of at least 2 hours? | White cells above 100/µL with more than 50% polymorphonuclear cells | That is the 2022 criterion. Red cells and lymphocytes do not meet it. |  |
| 4 | Why record dwell duration with the sample? | Short APD dwells may give a lower absolute count; the differential remains meaningful | The same count means different things after a 90-minute and an 8-hour dwell. |  |
| 5 | What is the ISPD 2022 target for culture-negative peritonitis? | Less than 15% of episodes | Less than 15% (GRADE 1C). Higher rates usually point to sampling problems. |  |
| 6 | Sampling before antibiotics is preferred. What if the patient is septic and sampling is delayed? | Do not delay urgent care; coordinate sampling as quickly as possible | Treatment must not wait for an unstable patient. |  |
| 7 | Mang Isko had five days without a bowel movement and grows mixed gram-negative organisms. Which route fits? | Transmural from the bowel | Constipation and mixed bowel organisms suggest enteric peritonitis. |  |
| 8 | S. aureus peritonitis 19 days after finishing treatment for S. aureus peritonitis is: | Relapsing | Same organism, within 4 weeks of completing therapy. |  |
| 9 | E. coli peritonitis 19 days after finishing treatment for S. aureus peritonitis is: | Recurrent | Different organism, within 4 weeks. |  |
| 10 | What did the 2022 update change about refractory peritonitis? | Expectant management beyond day 5 may be considered if the effluent white cell count is falling toward normal | A falling count beyond day 5 allows watchful management rather than mandatory removal. |  |
| 11 | Which is a modifiable peritonitis risk factor named in the 2022 ISPD update? | Low potassium | Along with pets in the exchange area and histamine-2 receptor antagonist use. |  |
| 12 | A unit finds 5 of 18 episodes culture-negative; three samples sat over a weekend. What is the best interpretation? | A sampling and transport process problem | 28% is above the target. Transport delays, late sampling and wrong containers are fixable process problems. |  |
| 13 | Why does UF often fall during peritonitis? | Inflammation increases membrane permeability and glucose absorption, so the osmotic gradient fades | An inflamed membrane absorbs glucose faster. Watch weight and UF during the episode. |  |
| 14 | Ms. Andrea’s bag is pink and hazy on day 2 of her period, with no pain or fever. What should happen? | Report it; the team decides whether it is blood alone or infection | Treat it as possible peritonitis until the team decides. |  |
| 15 | Which element makes a peritonitis handoff closed-loop? | Accepting clinician named, read-back, time stamp, disposition and a named person to follow the culture | Closing the loop means someone has accepted the plan, and someone will follow the result. |  |

## Module 8: Mechanical, Volume and Urgent Problems

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which finding changes low-drain troubleshooting into an emergency response? | New breathlessness at rest | Breathlessness at rest may mean acute fluid overload or a pleural leak. The others are routine to same-day concerns. |  |
| 2 | What is a common reversible cause of outflow failure in many programs? | Constipation displacing the catheter | A loaded bowel can push the catheter tip out of the pelvis. Bowel care often restores drainage. |  |
| 3 | Which action is unsafe without a specific order and training? | Forceful syringe flushing of the catheter | Force can push a plug deeper, tear tissue or introduce bacteria. The other checks are permitted when the patient is stable. |  |
| 4 | Ms. Weng has new right-sided heaviness and breathlessness after every fill, with falling UF. This suggests: | Pleural leak (hydrothorax); stop fills and call urgently | Dialysate may be crossing into the chest. Stop fills and call. |  |
