# Dialysis Head Nurse

## Module 1: Your Role on the Dialysis Floor

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why can a small process failure harm a dialysis patient faster than a ward patient? | The patient’s blood meets a large volume of prepared fluid, and the patient cannot correct water, potassium, or pressure on their own | The circuit, the fluid, and the missing kidney function remove the body’s own safety margin. |  |
| 2 | Which source sets out the three-month structured renal dialysis training for a head nurse? | The PSN standards for hemodialysis clinics | The PSN standards describe the head nurse’s preparation. DOH and the Nursing Act set other requirements. |  |
| 3 | A nurse holds a renal dialysis training certificate but has not been observed connecting a patient in your unit. How is she counted today? | As supernumerary until site sign-off | A certificate and a site sign-off are different evidence. Only signed-off nurses count toward the rule. |  |
| 4 | Twelve stations are planned. How many nurses with sign-off does the DOH provision require? | 3 | Twelve divided by four is three. |  |
| 5 | Two signed-off nurses are present for twelve planned stations. How many stations may open on nursing grounds alone? | 8 | Each nurse covers four stations under the provision. |  |
| 6 | The only ACLS-certified nurse on the roster has an expired certificate. What is the status of that gate? | Closed until a nurse with a current certificate is on the shift | The licensing order expects a nurse with an updated certificate on each shift. |  |
| 7 | Machine 7 was repaired but its release is unsigned. Who may put it back into use? | The designated technical staff, by signing the release | Technical release belongs to the technical staff. You confirm it exists. |  |
| 8 | Which decision belongs to the attending nephrologist or physician on duty, not the head nurse? | Which waiting patient can safely be delayed | Whether a patient can wait is a medical judgment about that patient. |  |
| 9 | Units where every nurse had two years of dialysis experience met more targets. What kind of evidence is this? | Cross-sectional, showing association only | Better-resourced units may have both experienced nurses and healthier patients. |  |
| 10 | What did the DOPPS analysis find about highly trained staff hours? | A higher share was associated with fewer skipped sessions | Skipping was itself associated with 30% higher mortality in the same cohort. |  |
| 11 | The administrator asks you to start now and fix the paperwork later. What is the best response? | Hold the affected stations, call the owners of each gate, and ask the physician who cannot wait | A hold with an owner protects patients without abandoning them. |  |
| 12 | What is the main purpose of an evidence register? | To show each requirement, the proof, the owner, and the expiry in one place | It turns “show me” into a two-minute answer. |  |
| 13 | How far ahead should the register be reviewed for expiring certificates? | About ninety days | Ninety days leaves time to book renewal courses. |  |
| 14 | Older PhilHealth circulars listed head nurse credentials. What should you do about them? | Ask the regional office in writing and file the answer | Their current status could not be confirmed from public documents. |  |
| 15 | Where a limit is not written in your unit’s protocol, what does this series ask you to do? | Follow the protocol, and ask the attending nephrologist for guidance | Inventing a limit at the chairside is how errors start. |  |

## Module 2: Reading Trends Across Sessions

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | For a Monday-Wednesday-Friday patient, on which day is mortality highest? | Monday, after the two-day gap | In the Foley cohort, deaths were higher on the day after the long interval. |  |
| 2 | Why is breathlessness when lying flat important even when the weight gain looks usual? | It suggests fluid in the lungs, and dry weight may no longer fit the body | A patient who has lost muscle can be overloaded at an unchanged dry weight. |  |
| 3 | A 60-kg patient needs 3,400 mL removed in 4 hours. What is the UF rate? | About 14 mL/kg/h | 3,400 ÷ 4 ÷ 60 = 14.2. |  |
| 4 | The same patient wants to leave after 3 hours with the same goal. What happens to the rate? | It rises to about 19 mL/kg/h | Less time for the same fluid means faster removal. |  |
| 5 | What did Flythe and colleagues find for UF rates above 13 mL/kg/h? | An association with higher all-cause and cardiovascular mortality | The study was observational. No trial has shown that a cap saves lives. |  |
| 6 | In DOPPS, the four-month risk of missing a session ranged from under 1% to 24% between countries. What does this suggest? | Systems such as transport and scheduling drive much of it | A fiftyfold difference between countries points to systems. |  |
| 7 | Which nadir pressure definition was most strongly associated with death? | A nadir systolic below 90 mm Hg | Definitions built on symptoms or interventions were not associated with death. |  |
| 8 | Why does water removal faster than refilling lower the pressure? | Blood volume falls because the tissues cannot refill the blood fast enough | The machine removes water only from the blood. |  |
| 9 | What did the lung ultrasound outcome trial show? | More lung congestion cleared, but no reduction in death or major cardiac events | The benefit was seen on images, not on outcomes patients feel. |  |
| 10 | A patient missed Friday, has palpitations, and has a critical potassium with no documented call. Which lane? | Now | Each finding alone is urgent. Together they are a Now finding. |  |
| 11 | What closes a critical result loop? | A named recipient acknowledges it, a decision is recorded, and follow-up has an owner | Until someone owns the next step, the loop is open. |  |
| 12 | Hemoglobin has drifted from 10.8 to 9.9 g/dL over three months. Which lane? | This month | It is a trend for the monthly review with the nephrologist. |  |
| 13 | Which question has outcome data behind it as a routine check? | How long does it take you to recover after a session? | Longer recovery was associated with hospitalization and death in DOPPS. |  |
| 14 | A family member asks for an extra hour after a missed session. Who decides? | The prescriber | Time, rate, and dialysis fluid are part of the prescription. |  |
| 15 | Why should a potassium from a hemolyzed sample still be reported? | The physician decides whether to repeat it, with the context given | Report the value with its context. Do not decide alone that it is false. |  |

## Module 3: The Order, the Machine, and the Record

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which process clears urea and potassium mainly? | Diffusion | Small molecules move down their concentration gradient across the membrane. |  |
| 2 | Why does dialysis fluid flow opposite to the blood? | To keep the concentration gradient steep along the whole fiber | Countercurrent flow keeps fresh fluid meeting blood that still carries solute. |  |
| 3 | The signed order says four hours, the machine is set for three, and the handover sheet says three. What governs? | The current signed order | The current order governs when it is clear. Remove the outdated sheet. |  |
| 4 | When should the nurse call the prescriber about a mismatch? | When the order itself is unclear, incomplete, or seems unsafe today | A clear current order is simply followed. An unclear one is clarified. |  |
| 5 | Pre-dialysis urea is 80 and post-dialysis urea is 28 mg/dL. What is the URR? | 65% | (80 − 28) ÷ 80 = 0.65. |  |
| 6 | A patient’s Kt/V jumps from 1.25 to 1.9 with no change in prescription. What do you check first? | The post-dialysis sampling method | A sample taken at full flow or after rinse-back gives a falsely good result. |  |
| 7 | What did the HEMO Study show? | Higher dose and high-flux membranes did not reduce deaths | The relative risk of death with the higher dose was 0.96. |  |
| 8 | What is the KDOQI 2015 minimum delivered single-pool Kt/V for thrice-weekly dialysis? | 1.2 | The target is 1.4, with a minimum delivered dose of 1.2. |  |
| 9 | Why is the TiME trial weak evidence against longer sessions? | The two groups differed by only nine minutes | A trial cannot show the effect of a difference it did not achieve. |  |
| 10 | What did MyTEMP show about cooler dialysis fluid as a center-wide policy? | It did not reduce cardiovascular death or admission | It may still help an individual patient with frequent low pressure. |  |
| 11 | In CONVINCE, death was 17.3% with HDF and 21.9% with hemodialysis. What is the absolute difference? | 4.6 percentage points | 23% is the relative reduction, and 0.77 is the hazard ratio. |  |
| 12 | Which patients were eligible for CONVINCE? | Patients able to reach a convection volume of at least 23 liters | High-volume HDF needs good access flow. |  |
| 13 | One hour is lost from a four-hour session with the same fluid goal. What happens to the UF rate? | It rises by a third | Four hours of work done in three is a third faster. |  |
| 14 | Unit delivered time is 97%. Why is that not enough? | An average can hide individual sessions that lose 30 minutes or more | Look at the sessions, not only the average. |  |
| 15 | What should happen to a laminated card at a station that lists dialysis settings? | Version-control it from the order or remove it | Any paper at the chair that can disagree with the order eventually will. |  |

## Module 4: Protecting the Access

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does a healthy fistula feel and sound like? | A soft continuous thrill and a low-pitched continuous bruit | Flow continues through the whole heartbeat. |  |
| 2 | A fistula is pulsatile, the bruit is high-pitched and systolic only, and bleeding lasts longer after needle removal. What is likely? | Narrowing on the outflow side | Downstream narrowing raises pressure in the access. |  |
| 3 | The usual thrill is absent before treatment. What do you do? | Do not cannulate and call for urgent review | No thrill suggests clotting. |  |
| 4 | You see shiny, scabbed skin over a fistula bulge. What is correct? | Do not cannulate there, arrange urgent review, and teach direct pressure | Scabbed skin over an aneurysm signals a risk of rupture. |  |
| 5 | Why can the venous pressure alarm stay silent when the needle comes out? | Most of the reading is needle and line resistance, which does not change | The reading may stay inside its alarm window. |  |
| 6 | In the bench simulation, how often did the machine alarm sound after dislodgement? | In 8 of 19 cases | It sounded in 42% of simulations. |  |
| 7 | The pump runs at 300 mL/min and a dislodgement goes unnoticed for 5 minutes. What is the potential loss? | 1,500 mL | 300 × 5 = 1,500 mL. |  |
| 8 | What is the first action when a needle site bleeds heavily? | Stop the blood pump | Stopping the pump stops the loss from the circuit. Clamp, press, and call follow at once. |  |
| 9 | Why is a blanket over the access arm unsafe? | It hides bleeding that the alarm may not detect | Only a visible access protects against silent bleeding. |  |
| 10 | For catheters used for months, where do most bloodstream infections start? | Inside the lumen, from the hub | That is why hub handling matters most. |  |
| 11 | What did the Philippine catheter cohort report? | 6.72 infections per 1,000 catheter-days, mostly gram-negative | Use of the catheter for transfusion or medicines was a risk factor. |  |
| 12 | What should the gram-negative pattern make you audit? | Water, solutions, multidose containers, and non-dialysis use of the catheter | This is an inference from the organisms, not a study finding. |  |
| 13 | What did the systematic review find about buttonhole cannulation? | No consistent pain benefit in randomized trials and more infection | Use it only for selected patients under a strict protocol. |  |
| 14 | What did the KDOQI 2019 guideline change about access planning? | An access plan built around each patient’s life | It replaced fistula first with an individualized life plan. |  |
| 15 | Nine of 95 patients have had a catheter for more than 90 days. What is the catheter dependence? | 9.5% | 9 ÷ 95 = 9.5%. |  |

## Module 5: Making Infection Prevention Visible

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why does hepatitis B need a dedicated room, machine, and staff? | It survives on surfaces for days at high concentrations | Durability on surfaces drives spread through shared equipment. |  |
| 2 | Why is hepatitis C not isolated? | Guidelines rely on strict standard precautions, because it is less hardy | Outbreaks have followed lapses such as shared vials. |  |
| 3 | For catheters used for months, where should prevention effort focus most? | The hub, each time it is opened | Most long-term catheter infections start inside the lumen. |  |
| 4 | Eighteen of 24 hand-hygiene opportunities were met. What should the audit report? | 75% | 18 ÷ 24. The interval is wide because the sample is small. |  |
| 5 | What goes in the denominator of a hand-hygiene audit? | All observed eligible opportunities | Every observed opportunity counts. |  |
| 6 | Why are 10 observations too few? | The 95% confidence interval is too wide to compare months | Eight of ten has an interval from about 49% to 94%. |  |
| 7 | Three bloodstream infections occur among 95 patients in one month. What is the rate? | 3.2 per 100 patient-months | 3 ÷ 95 × 100. |  |
| 8 | What did the United States collaborative report? | A 54% modeled fall in access-related bloodstream infection, without a control group | No control group, and volunteer facilities, so the true effect may be smaller. |  |
| 9 | Two patients develop chills twenty minutes after connection. Which cause is most likely? | A pyrogenic reaction from fluid or a product | Early onset points to the fluid or a product. |  |
| 10 | What is the first priority when symptoms cluster? | Assess and treat the patients, then escalate and contain | Patients first, with the investigation running alongside. |  |
| 11 | Where should medicines be drawn up? | In a clean area away from the stations | Medicine preparation stays inside the clean boundary. |  |
| 12 | The gap between shifts is too short to clean stations properly. What is the fix? | Change the schedule | When the schedule makes the process impossible, the schedule is the hazard. |  |
| 13 | What should you preserve when a new lot may be the source? | The opened containers, lines, and lot numbers | Evidence must still exist when the investigation asks for it. |  |
| 14 | Why should infection results be posted for staff? | Feedback is one of the core interventions | Surveillance with feedback to staff is part of the bundle. |  |
| 15 | Two catheter infections occur among 30 catheter patients over 30 days. What is the rate per 1,000 catheter-days? | 2.2 | 2 ÷ 900 × 1,000 = 2.2. |  |

## Module 6: Water, Machines, and Supplies

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is drinking water not safe for dialysis? | The blood meets about 120 liters across a thin membrane, without the gut’s protection | Small contaminants pass the membrane freely. |  |
| 2 | Which stage is the only reliable barrier to chloramine? | Carbon tanks | Reverse osmosis does not reliably remove chloramine. |  |
| 3 | Why is total chlorine tested every shift when cultures are monthly? | Carbon can exhaust within hours, and chloramine causes hemolysis | The test is the last line of defense. |  |
| 4 | Three connected patients develop chest pain and dark, cherry-red blood in the venous lines. What is the rule about the blood? | Do not return it. Stop and clamp | Suspected hemolysis means the circuit is the cause. |  |
| 5 | What is the ISO limit for bacteria in dialysis water? | Under 100 CFU/mL | Endotoxin must be under 0.25 EU/mL. |  |
| 6 | Which fluid must be ultrapure? | Fluid for hemodiafiltration | High-volume HDF infuses fluid directly into the blood. |  |
| 7 | What is an action level? | A lower level, usually half the limit, that triggers corrective action | It lets the unit act before the limit is reached. |  |
| 8 | Can yesterday’s normal result clear today’s abnormal one? | No. Today’s verification and the release process do | Each result stands for its own time. |  |
| 9 | How should a missing water result be treated? | As abnormal | If you cannot see it, the gate is closed. |  |
| 10 | What three documents are required before restart? | Corrective action, a repeat test within limit, and a signed release | A verbal assurance is none of them. |  |
| 11 | Feed conductivity is 400 and product is 12 µS/cm. What is the RO rejection? | 97% | (1 − 12 ÷ 400) × 100 = 97%. |  |
| 12 | Why must carbon contact time be recalculated when stations are added? | Higher flow shortens contact time | Contact time is volume divided by flow. |  |
| 13 | What enables a fast, targeted recall of a dialyzer lot? | Recording lot numbers for each patient and session | Lot numbers link the product to the patients. |  |
| 14 | Two patients have chills and fever within the first hour. Which contaminant group comes first to mind? | Bacteria and endotoxin | Endotoxin causes early pyrogenic reactions. |  |
| 15 | Who releases a water system after correction? | The designated technical authority | You confirm that the signed release exists. |  |

## Module 7: Leading the First Minutes of an Emergency

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the first question in any dialysis emergency? | Is the circuit the cause or a bystander? | The answer decides whether blood may be returned. |  |
| 2 | In which events is blood not returned? | Air embolism, hemolysis, and severe dialyzer reaction | In these, the circuit is the cause. |  |
| 3 | A defibrillator advises “no shock” during an in-clinic arrest. What next? | Continue compressions per algorithm | Two thirds of in-clinic arrests were nonshockable. |  |
| 4 | What was staff-started CPR associated with? | About three times the odds of leaving hospital alive | The study was observational, but no stronger evidence is likely. |  |
| 5 | Air is seen in the venous line and the patient coughs. What position is taught? | Left side down, head down | It keeps air in the right heart away from the lung outflow. |  |
| 6 | Why does “somebody call the doctor” fail? | No one owns the task, so no one confirms it was done | A name plus a report back closes the loop. |  |
| 7 | Why must one nurse watch the rest of the floor during an emergency? | Other connected patients still need monitoring and alarms continue | An emergency at one chair does not stop the risks at the others. |  |
| 8 | Pressure stays low five minutes after stopping UF, lying flat, and giving fluid. What has changed? | The working diagnosis. Look for a heart, bleeding, infection, or pericardial cause | Failure to recover is a warning sign in itself. |  |
| 9 | Why can some dialysis patients not hold their pressure during fluid removal? | Nerve damage, a stiff heart, and morning blood pressure tablets blunt their defenses | Compensation depends on vessel tightening and the heart’s response. |  |
| 10 | A patient becomes itchy and wheezy ten minutes into a session, and pressure falls. What happens to the blood? | Do not return it. Stop and clamp | Early onset with itching and wheeze suggests a dialyzer reaction. |  |
| 11 | Which dialysis fluid factor was associated with in-clinic arrest in a case-control study? | Low potassium | Low-potassium fluid was among the associated factors. |  |
| 12 | What must the transfer handoff include that is specific to dialysis? | Whether blood was returned, fluid removed, and estimated blood loss | The receiving team needs to know the volume state. |  |
| 13 | Does a defibrillator on site guarantee better survival? | Not by itself. The early cohort found no survival advantage, consistent with mostly nonshockable rhythms | Compressions remain the core intervention. |  |
| 14 | What did the structured handoff program show in hospital residents? | 23% fewer medical errors | Applying this to dialysis transfers is an inference. |  |
| 15 | What should happen after the emergency? | A short debrief, cart restocking, and checking on staff | The station is not ready until the cart is restocked. |  |

## Module 8: Staffing, Delegation, and Coaching

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Under the DOH provision, how many signed-off nurses are needed for 16 stations? | 4 | 16 ÷ 4 = 4. |  |
| 2 | Which staff count toward the staffing rule? | Only nurses with site sign-off to work alone | Orientees are supernumerary. |  |
| 3 | Which tasks are dropped first under overload? | Tasks without an alarm: looking, asking, teaching, checking | Those are the safety layers that catch what alarms miss. |  |
| 4 | What kind of evidence links staffing to outcomes in dialysis? | Mostly observational studies | They show association and a plausible mechanism, not a proven ratio. |  |
| 5 | Among young patients, what was the most stretched nursing quartile associated with? | 14% lower incidence of transplant | Referral work is among the tasks left undone. |  |
| 6 | Two signed-off nurses are present for twelve stations and neither has ACLS. How many stations may open? | None until ACLS cover arrives | ACLS cover is a separate gate from the ratio. |  |
| 7 | Who decides which waiting patients can be deferred? | The physician on duty or attending nephrologist | It is a medical decision about each patient. |  |
| 8 | What is a single point of failure on a shift? | A critical skill held by only one person | Each one needs a backup or a reliever. |  |
| 9 | What must follow delegation? | Supervision matched to the risk and confirmation of the result | You keep the accountability for delegating well. |  |
| 10 | Which of these is one of the five rights of delegation? | Right person, with competency verified for this task | The others are task, circumstance, direction, and supervision. |  |
| 11 | Which feedback is most likely to change a nurse’s technique? | “The hub was open about twenty seconds at station 6. That is when organisms enter. What made it awkward?” | Specific, observed, linked to risk, and asking about conditions. |  |
| 12 | Why ask what made the correct step difficult? | The cause is often the setup, which you can fix | Fixing the setup protects every nurse who comes next. |  |
| 13 | Why is retention a safety intervention? | Turnover lowers the experience mix, which feeds the overload loop | Experience mix is associated with outcomes. |  |
| 14 | What does a competency matrix show? | Each nurse’s signed-off skills with dates | Empty and expired cells become the teaching plan. |  |
| 15 | A reliever arrives and the arithmetic now works. What else do you check? | Close-observation cover, break cover, and whether the ACLS nurse is also a single point of failure | Arithmetic is where staffing starts, not where it ends. |  |

## Module 9: Partnering With Patients

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What question should replace the word non-adherent? | What is competing with dialysis today? | Most early sign-offs are trades with information the team does not yet have. |  |
| 2 | What did DOPPS find about missed sessions? | An association with 68% higher mortality | Longer travel time and depressive symptoms predicted missing. |  |
| 3 | Why does a fiftyfold difference between countries matter? | It points to systems such as transport, scheduling, and cost | Patient character cannot explain that range. |  |
| 4 | A patient cramps every session in the last hour and leaves early. What kind of problem is this? | A prescription problem to raise with the nephrologist | Review dry weight, rate, and time. |  |
| 5 | How is teach-back different from asking “Do you understand?” | The patient explains or shows the plan, so gaps become visible | A yes to “Do you understand?” measures politeness. |  |
| 6 | How strong is the evidence for teach-back in kidney disease? | Modest, from six small and varied studies | It is used because it is free, harmless, and shows what was not understood. |  |
| 7 | What fraction of new hemodialysis patients had limited health literacy in the Cavanaugh study? | About a third | 32%, and it was associated with higher mortality. |  |
| 8 | Tropical storm exposure was associated with what change in missed sessions? | About 55% higher risk over the following week | Plan for this every rainy season. |  |
| 9 | Which routine question has outcome data behind it? | How long does it take you to recover after a session? | Longer recovery was associated with admission and death. |  |
| 10 | Who individualizes nutrition advice? | The renal dietitian and clinical team, with nurses reinforcing it | Nurses reinforce the agreed plan. |  |
| 11 | When is a referral closed? | When an outcome is recorded | A referral without a recorded outcome is still open. |  |
| 12 | A patient wants to sign off early. What must happen? | Assess symptoms, explain the specific risk, confirm the next session, inform the physician, and document | Signing off is the patient’s right. Leaving unassessed is the unit’s failure. |  |
| 13 | A patient says he feels there is no point continuing dialysis. What do you do? | Listen, and inform the attending nephrologist promptly | Hopelessness and a wish to stop need the nephrologist’s attention. |  |
| 14 | How should a chart entry read when a patient left early because of transport? | Signed off early because of transport, options under review, with an owner | Accurate words lead to the right fix. |  |
| 15 | Which staffing finding relates to referrals? | The most stretched nursing quartile was associated with lower incidence of transplant | Referral work is among the tasks left undone. |  |

## Module 10: From the Chart to the PhilHealth Claim

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How many hemodialysis sessions does PhilHealth cover per calendar year? | 156 | Three a week for 52 weeks. |  |
| 2 | What is the current rate per session? | PHP 6,350 | Effective October 9, 2024. |  |
| 3 | Can unused sessions be carried to the next year? | No | The allocation is per calendar year. |  |
| 4 | Where is the list of covered services? | Table 2 of Circular 2024-0023, checked against any newer changes | No balance billing or co-payment for those services. |  |
| 5 | A prepared claim conflicts with the machine log. What do you do? | Hold and reconcile through clinical, billing, and compliance channels | A claim must rest on verified evidence. |  |
| 6 | How should an error in a signed note be corrected? | An authenticated addendum showing the original, the correction, author, and time | Correct, never erase. |  |
| 7 | Why is a copied note a clinical hazard? | It hides a missed session from the team | The billing error and the clinical error are the same error. |  |
| 8 | What does accreditation answer? | Whether the facility may claim at all | Coverage and quality are separate questions. |  |
| 9 | A patient has used 140 sessions by November 1 and dialyzes three times a week. When will the allocation run out? | Around December 8 | 16 sessions remain, about five weeks. |  |
| 10 | Five claims with five rungs each are traced, and 23 rungs are verified. What is the completeness? | 92% | 23 ÷ 25. |  |
| 11 | Are PhilHealth hemodialysis services given on the patient’s request? | No, when clinically indicated for that session | Clinical indication governs each service. |  |
| 12 | When is a late entry acceptable? | When labeled as late, with the real time and author | Never back-date. |  |
| 13 | What should training materials for the claims tracer contain? | Synthetic identifiers only | Patient privacy applies to training too. |  |
| 14 | What must a patient have before the hemodialysis benefit can be claimed? | Registration in the PhilHealth Dialysis Database | Registration is a condition of coverage. |  |
| 15 | A template pre-fills “session completed.” What kind of problem is this? | A system problem that allows false records | Fix the system so the record cannot say completed without times and fluid entered. |  |

## Module 11: Learning From Incidents and Proving Improvement

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is blaming the nurse who slipped not enough? | It removes one person and leaves the conditions that caused the slip | Design the work so the slip cannot reach the patient. |  |
| 2 | Which question is best when reconstructing an incident? | What made the safe action hard at that moment? | The answer reveals the conditions. |  |
| 3 | Rank from strongest to weakest: retraining, a forcing function, a checklist. | Forcing function, checklist, retraining | Remove or force, standardize, check, then train. |  |
| 4 | A review produces only a memo and a retraining session. What do you ask? | What stronger fix would stop the same slip on a busy morning? | Weak fixes fade within weeks. |  |
| 5 | Baseline errors were 6 of 60 and after a test 2 of 55. What is the best conclusion? | Promising, but the confidence intervals overlap. Keep measuring | Small samples move a lot by chance. |  |
| 6 | Why keep the denominator beside the rate? | It shows how much the rate could move by chance and whether volume changed | Rates from small denominators are unstable. |  |
| 7 | What is a balancing measure? | A measure that watches for harm caused by the change itself | A new check might delay starts. |  |
| 8 | Six consecutive weekly points fall below the median. What does that indicate? | A shift, a likely real change | Six or more on one side is the shift rule. |  |
| 9 | Five consecutive points each rise above the one before. What is this? | A trend | Five or more rising or falling is a trend. |  |
| 10 | What should happen before testing a change across the whole unit? | A small test on one station group or shift, with a prediction | Test small, learn, then scale. |  |
| 11 | When is a corrective action closed? | When the owner and reviewer have recorded evidence that it worked | Doing it is not the same as showing it worked. |  |
| 12 | What did the structured handoff program show? | 23% fewer medical errors in hospital residents | Before-and-after data in residents. Applying it to dialysis is an inference. |  |
| 13 | Which is a forcing function? | A machine that will not start until the scanned order matches the settings | It makes the wrong action impossible. |  |
| 14 | Which six elements define an indicator? | Numerator, denominator, inclusions, period, data source, owner | Without them, a trend cannot be trusted. |  |
| 15 | What is the median line on a run chart for? | A reference for the shift and run rules | The rules are counted against the median. |  |

## Module 12: Keeping Dialysis Running Through a Disaster

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why is a dialysis patient’s deadline in a disaster counted in days? | Potassium and fluid build up each day without treatment | Risk rises fastest after the third day. |  |
| 2 | Why can relief food make things worse? | Canned food is often salty and high in potassium | Salt drives fluid gain and potassium drives arrhythmia. |  |
| 3 | What is a disaster diet? | A short-term, stricter limit on potassium, salt, and fluid to buy days | It is a bridge, not a substitute for dialysis. |  |
| 4 | After Hurricane Sandy, what fraction of surveyed patients carried a detailed medication list? | About 44% | Emergency cards close this gap. |  |
| 5 | What did the Hurricane Katrina study show? | A coordinated response that moved patients quickly prevented excess deaths | The finding is evidence that planning works. |  |
| 6 | Tropical storm exposure was associated with what change in missed sessions? | About 55% higher risk over the following week | Plan for it every rainy season. |  |
| 7 | Who decides which patients are placed first? | The physician, using the facts you provide | It is a medical decision. |  |
| 8 | Which facts help the physician prioritize most? | Days since the last session, last potassium, fluid status, and symptoms | Risk builds with time, potassium, and fluid. |  |
| 9 | Fifteen patients need a session in 48 hours and six slots are confirmed. What is the capacity gap? | 9 | 15 − 6 = 9. Call the next receiving unit now. |  |
| 10 | What closes a patient transfer? | A named acceptor, a handoff, transport, notice to the patient, and a log entry | Acceptance and continuity are verified for each person. |  |
| 11 | What should receiving agreements include? | At least two units in different directions, each with a named contact and backup number | One road may be closed. |  |
| 12 | Which is one of the five restart gates? | Water and machines tested and released | The others are building, staff, supplies and cart, and records. |  |
| 13 | When is a disaster incident closed? | When every patient on the roster has been accounted for | Reconcile who was dialyzed, where, and who was missed. |  |
| 14 | What should be done when a warning is issued? | Ask the nephrologist about moving sessions earlier and call every patient | Dialyzing before landfall buys time. |  |
| 15 | What is a call-down drill? | A test of the patient call list before it is needed | Wrong numbers are found in calm weather, not in a storm. |  |

## Module 13: The Schedule, Absences, and Replacements

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A unit has 16 stations, runs 3 shifts a day, 6 days a week. How many nurse-shifts does it need each week at one nurse per four stations? | 72 | 16 ÷ 4 = 4 per shift; 3 × 6 = 18 shifts; 4 × 18 = 72. |  |
| 2 | What does a relief factor of 1.2 mean? | One extra nurse is employed for every five on the roster, to cover leave, sickness, and training | It is the margin that keeps the ratio when people are away. |  |
| 3 | What is a quick return? | Less than 11 hours of rest between two shifts | An early shift right after a late one is the most common example. |  |
| 4 | In the Norwegian registry study, what followed a month with more quick returns? | More sick leave the next month | Quick returns were associated with more sick-leave days and spells. |  |
| 5 | What kind of evidence links shift patterns to errors and sickness absence? | Observational studies in hospital nurses and public-sector workers | Consistent associations and a plain mechanism, but no trial and no dialysis-specific data. |  |
| 6 | A nurse finished at 9:00 PM. When is the earliest she should start again under an 11-hour rule? | 8:00 AM | 9:00 PM plus 11 hours is 8:00 AM. |  |
| 7 | Two nurses swap shifts by text and tell no one. What is the status of the swap? | Not a swap until it is approved and posted | The posted roster is the only roster. |  |
| 8 | Which check is most likely to be missed when approving a swap? | Whether the swap removes the only ACLS nurse from a shift or creates a quick return | Check skills on the floor and both nurses’ whole weeks. |  |
| 9 | A nurse calls in sick at 5:20. What do you do first? | Log the call and check the shift against the minimum and ACLS cover | Log it, then count, then call relievers in order. |  |
| 10 | Why call relievers in a fixed list order? | It is fair, avoids favorites, and makes the process predictable | Fairness is part of keeping relievers willing. |  |
| 11 | Relief has not arrived at 6:00 and you are below the minimum. What do you do? | Escalate to administration, the clinical head, and the physician; do not open short | Which patients wait or move is the physician’s decision. |  |
| 12 | What is the best first response to a pattern of Monday absences? | A private conversation that states the dates and asks what is going on | Talk first. The cause is often solvable. |  |
| 13 | In a private unit, what does the first written notice under DOLE Department Order 147-15 give the employee? | At least five calendar days to explain in writing | Then an opportunity to be heard, then a written decision. |  |
| 14 | In a government unit, which pattern meets the CSC definition of habitual tardiness? | Late ten times a month for two consecutive months | Or ten times a month for at least two months in a semester. |  |
| 15 | A nurse resigns with one month’s notice. When does the staffing gap really end? | When the new nurse is cleared to work alone | Orientation and sign-off take weeks; plan the bridge. |  |

# Dialysis Charge Nurse

## Module 1: Taking Charge: Authority, Boundaries, and the Shift You Inherit

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which document can relax a DOH licensing ratio? | An official DOH amendment or written DOH interpretation | Only the regulator can change its own licensing condition. |  |
| 2 | What does the DOH ratio in AO 2012-0001 count? | Stations per shift | It sets a limit of one nurse to four hemodialysis stations per shift. |  |
| 3 | Which decision belongs to the charge nurse? | Holding a connection until a release is signed | Verify, hold, and escalate are the charge nurse’s tools. |  |
| 4 | What does completing this track mean? | An educational record; appointment is the employer’s separate decision | No appointment, license, or accreditation follows from completing a study series. |  |
| 5 | Which is the best escalation statement? | “Beds 13 to 16 have no independent nurse; I have not connected them; I need one nurse or approval to move four patients; I will call back at 6:00.” | Hazard, protection now, help needed, and recheck time. |  |
| 6 | What do the cross-sectional staffing studies in dialysis show? | An association between more patients per nurse and more reported adverse events | They show association, not causation, but the direction is consistent. |  |
| 7 | What does a PhilHealth circular mainly govern? | Reimbursement and covered services | Payer rules answer payer questions; licensing rules come from DOH. |  |
| 8 | The ACLS-trained nurse goes home sick at 9 a.m. What do you do first? | Call for a replacement and tell the physician on duty | The shift no longer meets a licensing condition; get a replacement and keep the physician close. |  |
| 9 | Which of these is not one of the five questions to ask of a rule? | Who benefits from it? | Issuer, jurisdiction, scope, clause, and version. |  |
| 10 | Why is acuity still important when the ratio is met? | Patients with new catheters or unstable pressure need more nursing than stable patients | The ratio is a ceiling; need decides how the load is shared. |  |
| 11 | The physician on duty asks you to type a new dry weight on his verbal word. What is the safe response? | Read it back, record it as a verbal order per procedure, and flag it for countersignature | The decision is his; recording it safely is yours. |  |
| 12 | What is a requirement register? | A table of the rules the unit follows, with source, clause, owner, and review date | It keeps the answers to the five questions in one place. |  |
| 13 | According to the PSN 2024 standards, how much dialysis experience is expected of a head nurse? | Two years | PSN lists two years of dialysis experience, plus structured training and life support certificates. |  |
| 14 | What should you find out before accepting charge for the first time? | Your job description, the escalation ladder, and whether you may pause an unsafe activity | Without the authority to pause, you have responsibility without tools. |  |
| 15 | What does “technical release” mean? | A signed statement that water or a machine is fit to use | Release belongs to the authorized technical staff; the charge nurse confirms it before use. |  |

## Module 2: The Opening Sweep: Five Gates Before the First Connection

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The water test is not entered at opening. How do you treat it? | As abnormal until tested and signed | Unknown water is treated as unsafe until it is tested. |  |
| 2 | Why is dialysis water riskier than drinking water? | A patient is exposed to far more water across a membrane directly into the blood | Over a hundred liters a session reach the blood across the dialyzer. |  |
| 3 | What is the charge nurse’s job at the water-and-machines gate? | Confirm that testing and release are done and signed | The technical staff test and release; the charge nurse confirms. |  |
| 4 | In clinic cardiac arrests, staff-started CPR was associated with: | About three times the odds of leaving hospital alive | Readiness at the first connection is what makes early CPR possible. |  |
| 5 | Where should a patient with hepatitis B surface antigen dialyze? | A dedicated area with a dedicated machine, equipment, and staff | The CDC recommends dedicated rooms or areas, machines, and staff. |  |
| 6 | A good hold message to patients contains: | The reason, the expected delay, and the plan for full time | Patients accept a delay they understand. |  |
| 7 | The defibrillator check is missing from the log. What do you do? | Run the check now and borrow one if it fails | Readiness is checked before the first connection. |  |
| 8 | Fifteen minutes lost for 11 patients equals: | 165 minutes | 11 × 15 = 165 patient-minutes, close to one full session. |  |
| 9 | The cart seal number does not match the log. What next? | Do the full content check before patients connect | A mismatched seal means the cart may have been opened. |  |
| 10 | Why does chloramine harm dialysis patients? | It damages red cells and causes hemolysis | Chloramine oxidizes hemoglobin when it reaches the blood. |  |
| 11 | Who sets the numerical water limits? | The unit’s approved water protocol under the DOH order | The charge nurse follows them; she does not set or change them. |  |
| 12 | A machine has a red tag. Where should it be? | Out of the bay or blocked so nobody uses it | Physical separation prevents a rushed mistake. |  |
| 13 | The physician’s phone goes to voicemail at 5:50. What do you do? | Call the backup number; do not start the first connection until a physician is reachable, per protocol | The physician gate is open only when someone can answer. |  |
| 14 | What does the previous shift’s flag list protect against? | Running an order that is already out of date | A new dry weight or a pending result can change today’s plan. |  |
| 15 | Why run the gates in the same order every day? | So a busy morning cannot skip one | Habit protects the check that would otherwise be forgotten. |  |

## Module 3: Building the Assignment: Acuity, Competence, and Breaks

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why weight patients by need? | To make different nursing needs visible | Points show load; judgment still decides. |  |
| 2 | An orientee without catheter sign-off is assigned a catheter patient. Best fix? | Pair her with a signed-off nurse for that connection | She learns under observation; the patient stays safe. |  |
| 3 | Which of these cannot be delegated? | Nursing judgment | The five rights of delegation exclude nursing judgment. |  |
| 4 | When should breaks be avoided? | The first and last 30 minutes of the sessions | Most low-pressure, needle, and bleeding problems cluster then. |  |
| 5 | Shifts of 12.5 hours or more were associated with: | About three times the odds of an error | Rest protects patients as well as nurses. |  |
| 6 | On a heavy morning, the charge nurse should carry: | At most two, near the center of the floor | Free attention is the charge nurse’s main tool. |  |
| 7 | Which is one of the five rights of delegation? | The right supervision | Task, circumstance, person, communication, supervision. |  |
| 8 | What is true of acuity tools for outpatient hemodialysis? | They help make load visible but are not outcome-tested | Use them for visibility, and say so honestly. |  |
| 9 | Using the module’s example scale, a stable fistula patient who is often hypotensive scores: | 3 | 1 for the stable patient plus 2 for frequent low pressure. |  |
| 10 | Two nurses on a four-nurse floor take their break together. What happens? | The other two each cover twice as many running machines | That is how ratios quietly double. |  |
| 11 | What should the covering nurse be told? | Each patient’s one key risk | “Bed 4 drops after the second hour.” |  |
| 12 | “Two years’ experience” tells you: | Little; check which high-risk tasks are signed off | Competence is specific to a task. |  |
| 13 | The nursing supervisor asks to borrow a nurse at 7:30. What do you do? | Say what you can release safely and when, and record it | Answer with facts from the board. |  |
| 14 | Why might the charge nurse take the isolation patient? | The room is beside the station and she can see the floor from it | Placement lets her keep the floor in view. |  |
| 15 | When should breaks be planned? | At assignment time, written on the board | Planned breaks protect cover; improvised ones break it. |  |

## Module 4: The Live Board: Rounding, Huddles, and Interruptions

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Expecting that a patient will faint in the next ten minutes is which level of awareness? | Predicting | Predicting is the third level, and the one that gives time to act. |  |
| 2 | In the medicine-administration study, each interruption was associated with: | About 12.7% more clinical errors | Experience did not protect against interruption errors. |  |
| 3 | What is a huddle’s core question? | What could hurt someone in the next hours, and who owns it? | Short, standing, and about safety. |  |
| 4 | How is the evidence on huddles best described? | Mostly positive for teamwork, from low-rigor studies | Probably helpful and cheap; not shown to reduce adverse events. |  |
| 5 | During rounds, look first at: | The patient | The patient shows trouble before the numbers do. |  |
| 6 | Which task should be protected from interruption? | Heparin preparation | Medicine preparation, the order-machine check, and catheter connection. |  |
| 7 | What does an amber flag mean? | Watch, with a named nurse and a timed recheck | Amber without an owner and a time is a wish. |  |
| 8 | In the case, the main failure was: | The way information moved | A system without a board or timed rechecks. |  |
| 9 | What belongs on a board line? | Bed, access, key risk, fluid goal, times, flag | Enough to see at a glance, nothing private. |  |
| 10 | When should the first round happen? | About fifteen minutes after the last connection | Early problems cluster soon after connection. |  |
| 11 | The huddle drifts into complaints about the air conditioner. What do you do? | Park the topic and return to what could hurt someone | Keep it short and focused so it stays cheap. |  |
| 12 | A physician interrupts you during heparin preparation. What do you do? | Finish the step, then talk | Protect the task; then give him your full attention. |  |
| 13 | What happens to the board at the end of the shift? | It becomes the handoff: every open flag closed or passed to a named person | Open flags move with an owner. |  |
| 14 | Why should a charge nurse avoid her own difficult cannulations after the first connections? | Her attention is needed for the whole floor | A busy pair of hands means a lowered head. |  |
| 15 | A drowsy patient’s machine shows normal numbers. What do you do? | Assess the patient: rouse, pressure, glucose if indicated | The patient shows trouble first. |  |

## Module 5: Sorting Risk Before Connection

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | For a Monday-Wednesday-Friday patient, mortality was highest on: | The day after the two-day gap | Monday, after the long gap, in the Foley cohort. |  |
| 2 | What is the ultrafiltration rate for 3 liters over 4 hours in a 60-kg patient? | 12.5 | 3,000 ÷ 4 ÷ 60 = 12.5. |  |
| 3 | A rate above 13 mL/kg/h was: | Associated with higher mortality in a cohort | Observational; no trial has proven a cap. |  |
| 4 | Orthopnea before dialysis belongs in which lane? | Now | Call before connection. |  |
| 5 | When is a result loop closed? | When a named person owns the next step | Received, told, decided, recorded, owned. |  |
| 6 | Older patients on hemodialysis fell about how often in the cohort? | 1.6 times per person-year | Nearly half fell at least once. |  |
| 7 | In the glucose monitoring study, glucose during dialysis: | Fell about a third, lowest about two hours in | And 20% of participants had low blood sugar during a session. |  |
| 8 | Who sets the fluid goal? | The attending nephrologist or physician on duty | The nurse presents the numbers; the prescriber decides. |  |
| 9 | The same 3 liters over 4 hours in a 45-kg patient is about: | 17 mL/kg/h | 3,000 ÷ 4 ÷ 45 = 16.7. |  |
| 10 | A potassium of 5.8 after 4.6 last month is: | A change worth reporting today | Trends matter more than single values. |  |
| 11 | Which is a Today finding? | Missed medicines | The others are Now. |  |
| 12 | What two questions should a patient on insulin hear before connection? | Did you take your diabetes medicine today, and what did you eat? | Low blood sugar during dialysis is common in this group. |  |
| 13 | A patient lost muscle during a hospital stay but returns at his old dry weight. What may be true? | He may be overloaded, because the same weight now carries more water | Dry weight must fit the body he has now. |  |
| 14 | Why is a long-gap patient who also missed Friday especially risky? | Five days of water and potassium have built up | The longest gap of all. |  |
| 15 | A result arrives with no named owner. Who owns it? | The charge nurse, until someone else does | Unowned results are how loops break. |  |

## Module 6: The Order, the Machine, and the Minutes

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the KDOQI target spKt/V per thrice-weekly session? | 1.4 | With a minimum delivered of 1.2. |  |
| 2 | What did the HEMO Study show about a higher-than-standard dose? | It did not improve survival | Below standard is harm; above standard did not add benefit. |  |
| 3 | Ten minutes lost per session over a year is about: | 26 hours | More than six full four-hour sessions. |  |
| 4 | A dialysate potassium below 2 mmol/L was associated with: | Sudden cardiac arrest | Exact matching of the bath matters. |  |
| 5 | The three-way check compares: | Order, machine, and patient | Read aloud, two people, before connection. |  |
| 6 | A signed refusal form: | Records a decision; the cause still needs action | Find the reason and fix what can be fixed. |  |
| 7 | In DOPPS, shortened sessions were: | Associated with higher mortality | Observational, but consistent. |  |
| 8 | Which lines most often differ between order and machine? | Potassium, fluid goal, time, anticoagulation | Check these four aloud every time. |  |
| 9 | Mrs. Lim loses 35 minutes per session, 12 sessions a month. How much is that? | 420 minutes | 35 × 12 = 420 minutes, about 1.75 sessions. |  |
| 10 | Why does a silent single check miss errors? | The eye sees what it expects | Reading aloud forces each value to be seen. |  |
| 11 | What lowers the real dose even when the screen shows the right blood flow? | Recirculation in the access | Cleaned blood returning to the arterial needle is cleaned again. |  |
| 12 | A patient stops early every Wednesday because of her ride. Best action? | Change the slot or arrange transport | Fix the reason, not the minutes. |  |
| 13 | Cramps in the last hour on high-gain days suggest: | Fast fluid removal | Ask the physician to review the fluid plan. |  |
| 14 | Minutes added back at the end of the session are: | Not lost | Count only minutes not given back. |  |
| 15 | A machine turned over between patients still shows the last bath. What prevents this? | Resetting dialysate settings at turnover and the read-aloud check | Two layers: reset, then check. |  |

## Module 7: Mid-Session Surveillance: Pressure, Cramps, Glucose, and the Patient Who Goes Quiet

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Which nadir was most strongly associated with death in the cohort (start below 160)? | Below 90 | Below 100 for patients who started at 160 or more. |  |
| 2 | What is the first step when pressure falls? | Stop or reduce fluid removal and lay the patient flat if breathing allows | Let refill catch up. |  |
| 3 | What did MyTEMP show for cooler dialysate as a unit-wide policy? | No difference in cardiovascular death or admission | Cooling may still help a particular patient. |  |
| 4 | In the glucose monitoring study, how many participants had low blood sugar during a session? | 20% | One in five, with the lowest point about two hours in. |  |
| 5 | A drowsy patient on insulin needs: | A fingerstick glucose and a pressure check | Low sugar and low pressure look alike. |  |
| 6 | Pressure does not recover after stopping fluid removal and lying flat. Think of: | Heart attack, arrhythmia, bleeding, infection, or fluid around the heart | Call the physician now. |  |
| 7 | Cramps are associated with: | Fast fluid removal and low blood volume | Slow or stop removal and stretch the muscle. |  |
| 8 | When is the riskiest moment for a fall? | The first steps after disconnection | Pressure is lowest just after the session. |  |
| 9 | What defines intradialytic hypotension in the EBPG? | A fall of 20 mm Hg systolic or 10 mm Hg mean pressure, with symptoms or a need for action | Numbers plus symptoms or a need for nursing action. |  |
| 10 | What is the pooled prevalence of post-dialysis fatigue in the meta-analysis? | About 61% | Common, which is why a sleepy patient is easy to dismiss. |  |
| 11 | Mrs. Cruz has 1.6 liters removed in 2.33 hours at 48 kilograms. What is the rate? | About 14 mL/kg/h | 1,600 ÷ 2.33 ÷ 48 = 14.3. |  |
| 12 | A patient asks for the fluid goal to be “finished” in the last hour. What do you do? | Explain the risk and ask the physician; do not raise the rate on your own | Catching up means a dangerous rate. |  |
| 13 | What is the second drop of pressure in one session a signal of? | The plan needs changing today; call the physician | Repeated drops need a decision. |  |
| 14 | Why is “sleeping comfortably” a dangerous chart entry? | It can hide low pressure, low sugar, high potassium, stroke, or low oxygen | Rouse every patient at every round. |  |
| 15 | When should blood pressure tablets usually be timed, if the physician agrees? | After dialysis | Taking them before dialysis weakens the body’s defense against falling pressure. |  |

## Module 8: Watching Every Access on the Floor

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the bench study, how often did the alarm sound for a dislodged needle? | 42% | 8 of 19 dislodgements. |  |
| 2 | What is the first action in access bleeding? | Stop the pump and press firmly | Stop the loss first. |  |
| 3 | Where did most fatal access bleeds with a known location happen? | At home or in nursing homes | 78% of those with a known location. |  |
| 4 | A scab on thin, shiny skin over an aneurysm. What do you do? | Hold and call for urgent assessment | Skin erosion over an access can rupture. |  |
| 5 | Which access carries the highest infection risk? | Catheter | Catheters are the riskiest access. |  |
| 6 | The access should be: | Visible at all times | Never covered. |  |
| 7 | At 300 mL a minute, an unwatched minute risks up to: | 300 mL | The arithmetic behind the rule. |  |
| 8 | On the access arm: | No cuffs, tight sleeves, watches, IV lines, or blood draws | The rule travels with the patient. |  |
| 9 | Why does the venous pressure reading barely change when the needle comes out? | Most of the reading comes from the line and needle resistance | That is why eyes beat alarms. |  |
| 10 | No thrill or bruit before needling means: | Hold and call | The access may have clotted. |  |
| 11 | Air enters a catheter line. First steps? | Clamp, position per protocol, oxygen, call | Stop more air entering, then get help. |  |
| 12 | After a bleed is controlled, who decides about reconnection? | The physician | Reconnection follows the physician’s assessment. |  |
| 13 | A cold patient insists on covering his arm. Best response? | Offer a blanket under the arm or a second blanket, and explain why | Warmth and visibility can both be met. |  |
| 14 | Why stagger three catheter connections in one pod? | So each is a protected, uninterrupted task | An open hub plus an alarm is how infections start. |  |
| 15 | Who should be taught to press and call at home? | Every patient and family member | Most fatal bleeds happen outside the unit. |  |

## Module 9: Medicines, Heparin, and Blood on the Floor

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | An independent double-check means the second nurse: | Works it out herself before comparing | Two readers, not one reader and a witness. |  |
| 2 | 2,000 units/h with a 1,000 units/mL syringe runs at: | 2 mL/h | 2,000 ÷ 1,000 = 2. |  |
| 3 | A high-alert medicine order appears only on a discharge note. What do you do? | Hold until it is reconciled into a current order | No note becomes an order by itself. |  |
| 4 | In the Canadian transfer study, unintended discrepancies affected about: | Four in five patients | Most often a medicine left off the new orders. |  |
| 5 | First step in a suspected transfusion reaction? | Stop the transfusion, keep the line open, call | Then return the bag and tubing to the blood bank. |  |
| 6 | Chills during a transfusion in a catheter patient. Think of: | Transfusion reaction, catheter infection, or dialyzer reaction | Tell the physician all three. |  |
| 7 | Which is a high-alert medicine? | Intravenous iron | Heparin, insulin, IV iron, potassium, and anticoagulant locks. |  |
| 8 | When do serious transfusion reactions usually show? | In the first fifteen minutes | Start slowly and watch closely. |  |
| 9 | What did adding a dialysis pharmacist show in a United States program? | Fewer medication discrepancies over time | Reconciliation improves with dedicated effort. |  |
| 10 | A pump rate ten times the patient’s usual means: | Stop and check the order and the syringe | Arithmetic is a plausibility check. |  |
| 11 | Two heparin strengths sit on one shelf. What do you do? | Separate them and report the storage risk | Look-alike errors start on the shelf. |  |
| 12 | Who sets the heparin dose? | The prescriber | The nurse checks the arithmetic. |  |
| 13 | What is returned to the blood bank after a reaction? | The bag and tubing | The blood bank investigates the unit. |  |
| 14 | A near miss caught by a double-check is: | A success of the system and a lesson to report | Report it so the cause is fixed. |  |
| 15 | What should travel with a reconciled list? | One current list agreed with the prescriber | One list, current, agreed. |  |

## Module 10: Infection Prevention in Real Time

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | In the CDC collaborative, access-related bloodstream infections fell by a modeled: | 54% | From 0.73 to 0.42 per 100 patient-months. |  |
| 2 | Where should medicines be prepared? | In a clean area away from stations and blood handling | Clean and dirty never cross. |  |
| 3 | Two new hepatitis C positives on one shift at neighboring stations are: | A possible cluster to escalate today | Call the same day and preserve records. |  |
| 4 | Who decides testing and station changes in a cluster? | The infection lead and the physician, per protocol | The charge nurse notices, calls, and preserves evidence. |  |
| 5 | An audit that shows 100% every month suggests: | Possibly that nobody is looking closely | Honest audits find gaps. |  |
| 6 | 15 of 24 observations equals: | 62.5% | And the uncertainty is wide with only 24. |  |
| 7 | Recapping a used needle by hand is: | Never done | Sharps go straight into the container. |  |
| 8 | Single-dose vials are: | Used once | Shared vials have caused outbreaks. |  |
| 9 | What did the Belgian study show? | Strict infection control without isolation reduced seroconversion from 1.4% to 0% | Practice decides. |  |
| 10 | How long can hepatitis B survive on surfaces? | At least seven days | Why cleaning between patients matters. |  |
| 11 | Unused swabs from a catheter connection should: | Be discarded at the station | Nothing taken to a station returns to clean supplies. |  |
| 12 | A nurse has a needlestick. What do you do? | Send her for assessment now and cover her patients | Post-exposure assessment is needed within hours. |  |
| 13 | What were the 2008 to 2011 United States hepatitis C outbreaks linked to? | Injectable medicine handling, hand hygiene, and disinfection breaches | Everyday practice failures. |  |
| 14 | When should feedback on a hand hygiene lapse be given? | Privately, the same shift | Timely and private works best. |  |
| 15 | Why not move patients right away in a possible cluster? | It can destroy the evidence the infection lead needs | Preserve, then let the lead decide. |  |

## Module 11: Water and Machines: Hold, Escalate, Release

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does carbon remove? | Chlorine and chloramine | When carbon fails, chloramine reaches the blood. |  |
| 2 | How does chloramine harm dialysis patients? | It damages red cells and causes hemolysis | Oxidation of hemoglobin. |  |
| 3 | Several neighboring patients with similar symptoms soon after starting suggests: | A shared cause | Act on the pattern. |  |
| 4 | Dark venous-line blood with back pain. What do you do? | Stop and do not return the blood | Suspected hemolysis. |  |
| 5 | Who can restart after a water event? | The technical lead’s signed release with the medical director’s agreement | Restart needs signatures. |  |
| 6 | Where are the water limits? | In the approved water protocol | Read the protocol; do not recall it. |  |
| 7 | A conductivity alarm sounds. What do you do? | Take the machine out of use and tell biomedical | The dialysate mix may be wrong. |  |
| 8 | During a water hold, the other patients are: | Watched by an assigned nurse | The event is not only in three chairs. |  |
| 9 | What does reverse osmosis remove? | Most dissolved ions and many microbes | A membrane step after carbon. |  |
| 10 | What can bacteria and endotoxins in the loop cause? | Fever and chills during dialysis | Stagnant pipes grow bacteria. |  |
| 11 | What evidence should be saved? | Blood samples, dialysate, a water sample, lines and dialyzers, labeled with times | The investigation needs them. |  |
| 12 | The technician says, “Go ahead, I’ll sign later.” What do you do? | Wait for the signature | No signature, no restart. |  |
| 13 | Why does this module give no numerical water limits? | They belong to the unit’s approved protocol, and numbers in memory go out of date | The charge nurse checks that they were applied. |  |
| 14 | A log entry is out of range but has no action written beside it. What is it? | A hold until the action is documented | An abnormal result needs a recorded response. |  |
| 15 | Is there an automatic restart rule after a water event? | No; restart is a signed decision | Written release and agreement only. |  |

## Module 12: Leading the First Ten Minutes of an Emergency

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Staff-started CPR in clinic arrests was associated with: | About three times the odds of leaving hospital alive | Early CPR is the strongest lever. |  |
| 2 | In the registry, the first rhythm was not shockable in about: | 66% | Readiness and speed matter more than rhythm. |  |
| 3 | Suspected hemolysis during a collapse. What happens to the blood in the circuit? | Do not return it | Burst red cells release potassium. |  |
| 4 | Which role is most specific to dialysis emergencies? | The watcher of the other patients | Eleven other machines are still running. |  |
| 5 | In the simulations, requests with both a named recipient and a closed loop were completed: | Every time | Against 81% for neither or one. |  |
| 6 | What should the handoff to an ambulance include? | Time on machine, fluid removed, last potassium, access, and whether blood was returned | Details the receiving team cannot guess. |  |
| 7 | When is the risk of arrest highest? | After the long gap | The first session after the two-day gap. |  |
| 8 | The circuit is a bystander. What do you do? | Return blood quickly if safe or disconnect, and start resuscitation | Do not delay CPR. |  |
| 9 | Air is seen in the venous line at collapse. What do you do? | Stop the pump, clamp, and do not return the blood | The circuit may be the cause. |  |
| 10 | Two emergencies at once. What does the charge nurse do? | Keep the sicker patient and send the most experienced available nurse to the other with a named task | Delegate with a name, a task, and a report time. |  |
| 11 | What is the second beat of closed-loop communication? | Repeating the task back | Name and task, repeat back, report done. |  |
| 12 | Which is a reversible cause to consider in a dialysis patient’s arrest? | High potassium | Treatment follows the physician’s orders. |  |
| 13 | Which guidance should the unit follow for resuscitation? | Current AHA and ERC life support guidance | The ERC special circumstances chapter covers dialysis. |  |
| 14 | “Someone get the cart!” Why is this a weak command? | Nobody is named, so it may not be done | Unnamed requests are the ones nobody does. |  |
| 15 | What is the charge nurse’s first job in an emergency? | Lead: name the problem, assign roles, decide on the circuit, protect the others | Her hands may help too, but leadership comes first. |  |

## Module 13: Downtime, Typhoons, and Getting Through the Day

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is the first action when machines stop? | Check patients and disconnect safely | Patients before phones. |  |
| 2 | The generator is on but the water system is off. What do you do? | No new connections; continue only on confirmed water | Water must be confirmed safe. |  |
| 3 | How are downtime records entered later? | Labeled late, with the true event time | Honest late entries. |  |
| 4 | Who chooses which patients are dialyzed first? | The attending nephrologist | The charge nurse supplies the facts. |  |
| 5 | After Hurricane Sandy, about what share of surveyed patients missed dialysis? | 26% | 26.3% of 357 patients. |  |
| 6 | Which patients usually cannot wait? | High potassium, large gains, breathlessness, missed sessions, no urine | The list is facts; the physician chooses. |  |
| 7 | What does restart require? | Signed releases | The same rule as Module 11. |  |
| 8 | Patients sent home need: | Diet and fluid rules, when to return, where to go if breathless, a phone number, a medication list | Fewer than half had a medication list after Sandy. |  |
| 9 | Exposure to a tropical storm in the United States cohort was associated with: | A 55% higher risk of a missed session in the next week | Disruption reaches patients quickly. |  |
| 10 | In the Katrina study, what made the difference? | Coordination and preparation across the network | The storm was not associated with excess mortality in adjusted models. |  |
| 11 | What is hand-cranking? | Returning blood manually when a machine stops, per its instructions | Done per the machine’s instructions. |  |
| 12 | What goes on the downtime board? | Every patient, minutes delivered, fluid removed, status, and plan | The shift’s shared picture without electricity. |  |
| 13 | A patient’s road will be closed tomorrow. What do you do? | Arrange a partner unit with the head nurse and send a transfer summary | The summary travels with the patient. |  |
| 14 | Why keep downtime forms ready before the season? | So the record continues when the system fails | There is no time to design forms during a brownout. |  |
| 15 | Who prepares the unit before the season, and who leads on the day? | The head nurse prepares; the charge nurse leads on the day | Different horizons, shared plan. |  |

## Module 14: Handoffs That Close the Loop

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Who does the synthesis in I-PASS? | The receiver | The receiver repeats what she now owns. |  |
| 2 | In the I-PASS study, errors per 100 admissions fell from: | 24.5 to 18.8 | Preventable adverse events fell from 4.7 to 3.3. |  |
| 3 | Applying resident handoff data to nursing shift changes is: | A reasonable inference | Same mechanism, untested setting. |  |
| 4 | How does an SBAR call end? | With a recommendation or request, then read-back | Ask for a decision. |  |
| 5 | What concerns do reviews of bedside handover raise? | Privacy and time | Do the check at the chair; keep private details for the station. |  |
| 6 | A handoff item without an owner is: | An open loop | Owners close loops. |  |
| 7 | When is the outgoing nurse released? | When the receiver confirms | No release without a grip. |  |
| 8 | What was missing in Benjie’s handoff? | An action, an owner, and a time | A fact without an action. |  |
| 9 | What is a contingency statement? | “If this happens, do that” | It tells the receiver what to do when things change. |  |
| 10 | A physician gives an order by phone. What do you do? | Read it back, write it, and have it countersigned | Read-back confirms the order. |  |
| 11 | The incoming charge nurse is 30 minutes late. What do you do? | Stay until a handoff is complete and tell the head nurse | Responsibility does not drop to the floor. |  |
| 12 | “Doc, the pressure is a bit low” is weak because: | It gives no numbers and asks for nothing | SBAR makes the request clear. |  |
| 13 | Which parts of I-PASS are most often skipped? | Action list and synthesis | And they are the ones that protect actions. |  |
| 14 | A patient at the chair corrects the handoff. What do you do? | Thank her and update the board | Patients catch errors staff miss. |  |
| 15 | In the later rollout across 32 programs, reported handoff-related adverse events fell by about: | 47% | Observed in a multicenter implementation study. |  |

## Module 15: Delegation, Coaching, and Speaking Up

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is competence? | Having done it correctly under observation, signed off | Exposure is not competence. |  |
| 2 | Which cannot be delegated? | Nursing judgment | Judgment stays with the licensed nurse. |  |
| 3 | Good feedback is: | Specific, timely, and private | About the act, with a next step. |  |
| 4 | What does CUS stand for? | Concerned, Uncomfortable, Safety issue | Each step is harder to dismiss. |  |
| 5 | In the Philippine study, burnout was associated with: | Less caring behavior | Partly through lower engagement. |  |
| 6 | Repeated unsafe practice after feedback. What do you do? | Escalate to the head nurse with dates and observations | A fair process protects patients and the nurse. |  |
| 7 | After delegation, accountability for the patient stays with: | The delegating licensed nurse | The delegatee is responsible for the task. |  |
| 8 | A senior dismisses your safety concern. What do you do? | Repeat it, then escalate | Safety concerns move up when not heard. |  |
| 9 | Can an assistant take a pressure and report it below a number you set? | Yes, with clear instructions on when to call | The nurse keeps the judgment. |  |
| 10 | What are the coaching steps? | Watch, ask, let them act, step in only for safety, debrief | Then record it. |  |
| 11 | Why can hiya and pakikisama silence safety concerns? | They favor smooth relationships over awkward questions | Strengths that need a safe outlet. |  |
| 12 | Who should thank a junior who stops them? | The person stopped, out loud | That is how speaking up becomes normal. |  |
| 13 | What should the stop phrase mean in your unit? | Stop and listen, whoever says it | Agreed in advance by everyone. |  |
| 14 | What do cross-sectional burnout studies show? | An association, not cause | Calibrate the claim. |  |
| 15 | Why record coaching in the competence record? | So the next charge nurse knows what this nurse can do alone | The record carries competence across shifts. |  |

## Module 16: Patients, Families, and Transitions Beyond the Chair

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What does teach-back mean? | The patient explains or shows in their own words | It checks the teaching, not the patient. |  |
| 2 | Missed and shortened treatments are associated with: | Higher mortality | Ask about the reasons. |  |
| 3 | What overall peritonitis rate does the ISPD 2022 update recommend at most? | 0.40 | Every episode counts. |  |
| 4 | Cloudy peritoneal dialysis fluid. What do you do? | Call the PD team the same day | Possible peritonitis. |  |
| 5 | What must a transfer summary include? | Access arm rules, prescription, hepatitis status, medicines | And the unit’s phone number. |  |
| 6 | A patient raises stopping dialysis. What do you do? | Listen, record her words, and arrange a talk with the attending and family | She is asking to be heard. |  |
| 7 | Limited health literacy in a dialysis cohort was associated with: | Higher mortality | Plain language and teach-back matter. |  |
| 8 | A transplant recipient has missed anti-rejection medicines. What do you do? | Contact the transplant team today | Same-day red flag. |  |
| 9 | A patient says “yes” to everything. What should you suspect? | Possibly politeness, not understanding | Ask her to show or say it. |  |
| 10 | Who sends the summary when a patient is admitted from the unit? | The charge nurse on that shift | And she calls the ward. |  |
| 11 | What question uncovers treatment burden? | What makes it hard to come? | Practical problems have practical fixes. |  |
| 12 | When a patient returns from the hospital, the charge nurse should: | Reconcile medicines and check the access | Transitions run both ways. |  |
| 13 | What teach-back phrasing puts the burden on the teacher? | “So I know I explained it clearly, can you tell me what you will do?” | It is a check of the teaching. |  |
| 14 | A patient travels to another province. What goes ahead of her? | A transfer summary with hepatitis results and the prescription | The receiving unit needs it before the first session. |  |
| 15 | Who decides about stopping dialysis? | The patient and the attending, together | The charge nurse listens and arranges. |  |

## Module 17: The Record, the Claim, and Learning From What Went Wrong

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | How many hemodialysis sessions a year does PhilHealth cover? | 156 | For registered patients with CKD stage 5. |  |
| 2 | What is the PhilHealth payment per session since October 2024? | 6,350 pesos | Under PhilHealth Circular 2024-0023. |  |
| 3 | Writing four hours for a session of three hours twenty minutes is: | Falsification | The record must match what happened. |  |
| 4 | How is a record corrected? | A single line, the correct entry, date, time, signature | The original stays readable. |  |
| 5 | After an incident, what should be preserved? | Lines, dialyzer, labels, syringes, logs | The investigation needs them. |  |
| 6 | Which run chart pattern suggests a real change? | Six or more consecutive points on one side of the median | A run, not a single good week. |  |
| 7 | What does an investigation mainly ask? | How the system allowed it | System fixes work better than effort alone. |  |
| 8 | The claim depends on: | The clinical record | Not the other way around. |  |
| 9 | What is a late entry? | A record written after the event, labeled late, with the true time | Honest about when it was written. |  |
| 10 | In the case, 31 of 240 sessions were shortened. What percentage is that? | 12.9% | 31 ÷ 240 = 12.9%. |  |
| 11 | Who should hear about a request to falsify records? | The head nurse | So the pressure behind it can be addressed. |  |
| 12 | Who is often the second person hurt by an error? | The nurse who made it | Support staff after an incident. |  |
| 13 | Which fix works best after a look-alike medicine error? | Separating the products on the shelf | Change the system. |  |
| 14 | Why does the record matter most to the next nurse? | She acts on it, and a false record can hurt the patient | The first reader is clinical. |  |
| 15 | PhilHealth paid about how much for hemodialysis claims in the first half of 2025? | 27 billion pesos | More than two million claims. |  |

## Module 18: Ethics, Resilience, and the Integrated Shift

| Q | Stem | Keyed answer | Explanation | Objective |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | A patient refuses part of a treatment. What do you do first? | Make sure the choice is informed, record it, and tell the physician | Then ask why. |  |
| 2 | How do you protect privacy on an open floor? | Speak quietly and keep screens and charts away from others | Results are discussed at the station. |  |
| 3 | A family member is angry. What do you do first? | Move away from other patients and listen | Then say what you can do. |  |
| 4 | In the review, moral distress in dialysis nurses was linked to: | Patient care burdens, workload, and hierarchical barriers | Debriefing and support help. |  |
| 5 | What does the capstone test? | Priorities and safe sequencing | Patients before paperwork. |  |
| 6 | After a code, a colleague is distressed. What do you do? | Debrief and offer support | A short debrief helps the team come back. |  |
| 7 | In what year was the Philippine Data Privacy Act passed? | 2012 | Republic Act No. 10173. |  |
| 8 | Two emergencies at once. What do you do? | Take the sicker and delegate the other with a named task | Someone always watches the floor. |  |
| 9 | A family member is filming the floor. What do you do? | Ask politely to stop, to protect other patients’ privacy | Calm and respectful. |  |
| 10 | A patient refuses needling by a trainee. What do you do? | Respect the choice, reassign, and record it | Choice and safety together. |  |
| 11 | What is moral distress? | Knowing the right thing but being unable to do it | Common in dialysis nursing. |  |
| 12 | Why should the charge nurse take her own break? | Fatigue raises errors, and she models it for the team | A charge nurse who never eats teaches that eating is optional. |  |
| 13 | What does de-escalation start with? | Listening without interrupting | Then state what you can do. |  |
| 14 | Which item is in the capstone portfolio? | An improvement run chart | Evidence from every module. |  |
| 15 | What is the order of priorities in a busy shift? | Patients first, the sicker first, someone always watching the floor | The capstone’s core. |  |
